# A.20. Executing Entity information

Throughout the Funding Proposal, Executing Entity (EE) refers to any entity that signs a Subsidiary Agreement* with IIB to execute, carry-out, or implement a particular project or activity in accordance with this Funding Proposal and entering into any Subsidiary Agreement, following its policies and procedures. Information on such policies and procedures and the track record of selected EE is provided in Section 2.1. The EE is responsible for implementing and carrying out the functions of an EE for technical cooperation activities. More specifically:

* For each project or activity included in Component I, each EE will be responsible for the full execution of the loan and/or investment grant in accordance with the Subsidiary Agreement and this Funding Proposal. Each EE will be identified jointly with national governments based on the relevance of their capacity to deliver the project or activity, including its technical expertise, financial management and fiduciary management, and track record, including with the IIB. EEs may include public sector entities (e.g., government agencies), private sector entities such as FINAGRO (Colombia), CONAFIPS (Ecuador), COFIDE (Peru), and FONAGRO (Brazil) or other organizations such as FUNDAZIONE AVAMAZIA (Brazil).

* For the execution of equity investments referred to in Component 12, eligible EEs include for the Regeneraion Accelerator: Kaite Investments; an investment fund managed by a private sector entity with experience in investing in early-stage bioeconomy businesses; and/or direct investees (growth tech companies) that will receive funding support for activities related to climate-tech and biotech.

* For the execution of investment grants referred to in Component II, IIB will be responsible for the full execution of such grant. To defray the financial charges costs associated with the portion of the guarantee provided by IIB under the Subsidiary Agreement, IIB will require the approval of the relevant guarantee operation; the IIB will conduct an institutional capacity assessment of such public entity, as further detailed in Annex 20.

* For all Technical Cooperation activities (Components I.3., II.1 and III) IIB will be responsible for the full execution of such activity under the Subsidiary Agreement with other EEs.

**Page 8**
---

In 2019, governments of Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname signed the Leticia Pact for the Amazon Region, seeking cooperation among Amazon countries for the conservation of the region to preserve its biodiversity and the livelihoods of more than 35 million people of its population. The Pact includes action related to the fight against deforestation and forest degradation, land tenure, conservation, recovery and resilience of biodiversity and ecosystems; sustainable productive systems; active participation by indigenous peoples in decision-making processes for the sustainable development of the region; as well as the promotion of financial mechanisms to implement the Pact.

Building on this foundation, the Programme is designed to contribute to the implementation of a framework for promoting sustainability in the AFOLU sector. Shifting to business models and technologies that sustainably use natural capital and forest assets (i.e., bio-businesses) can contribute to lower the environmental impact of economic activities while generating new opportunities for growth. Attracting private investment in bio-business is significant and tapping it can be determinant of the ability of economies to transition towards more sustainable development paths.

The Programme aims at addressing some of the most critical barriers that hinder the development of bio-businesses, such as lack of access to finance, limited knowledge and financial markets, weak institutional environment for bio-business development, lack of standardized frameworks for measuring environmental performance, and a fragmented regulatory framework. The Programme activities are expected to help effectively overcome these barriers, ensuring the creation of enabling conditions for the development of bio-businesses in the region.

The Programme will also contribute to further support the development of these businesses. Targeted financing schemes and channels, such as blended finance mechanisms, can be used to address the specific financial needs of bio-businesses. These business needs can make financiers more willing to serve the sector and developers more willing to invest without risking profitability. Knowledge and institutional mechanisms can help ensure a continued flow of private investment in bio-businesses.

The Programme will also contribute to strengthening the enabling environment for bio-businesses through one-on-one technical assistance, capacity building workshops, and knowledge sharing events. It will also support the development of partnerships between public and private actors through concessional financing schemes.


Achievements:

* 100% reduction in deforestation (2015-2019) compared to baseline
* US$34 million annually (2015-2019) from forest and land-use, enhance carbon stocks of forests under improved management, leverage US$7.1 billion in private capital towards GCF funding (including US$186 million in concessional finance)
* 1.2 million hectares of degraded lands restored
* 3.5 million ha of new protected areas
* 40% increase in forest cover
* 1.3 plus US$400 million from other sources to support the resilience and adaptation of some 87,327 beneficiaries (direct and indirect)

Overall, the Programme will contribute to a significant reduction in deforestation rates, as well as changes of enriched behaviours (i.e., unsustainable practices in business in the Amazon), helping create the foundation for more sustainable economic development. It will play a role in attracting capital investments, ensuring the development of profitable, scalable and climate-friendly production models that private entities will be willing to participate.

Jumpstarting private participation in bio-businesses should produce demonstration effects, highlighting biodiversity as an intrinsic element of economies success beyond GCF support.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Climate change (max. 1500 words, approximately 2 pages)

1. Covering over 6,000,000 km² and portions of 9 countries in Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC), the Amazon is the world's largest river system, containing a tenth of the world's known species². The Amazon biome, which includes the Amazon rainforest, is home to one-fifth of all terrestrial biodiversity, being fundamental for carbon sequestration (at least approximately 150-200 billion tons of carbon are stored in its soils and vegetation)³. The Amazon River Basin is a major source of freshwater for South America's population and economy. The Amazon forms a huge biophysical entity, and many its parts depend on the integrity of the whole. It is home to millions of people.

#### Climate change and the Amazon basin: observed trends and projected impacts

- On average, temperatures have increased by 0.5°C since 1980 in the Amazon Biome, with the largest increments observed in the southwestern Amazon (USDA, 2018)⁴. A significant increase in heatwaves has been observed over the past decades, and more frequent extreme precipitation events have also been recorded continually over the past decade. Nonetheless, the following trends have emerged:
- Drier conditions are becoming more common across much of the Amazon basin each year; as well as the frequency of drought events has been increasing in the Amazon during the period 2015–2014 (Marango and Souza 2018, USDA 2018). Over the last 35 years there has been an increasing tendency for dry conditions to persist over longer periods.
- Espírito Santo et al. (2010) observed a significant increase in dry-day frequency in the southern Amazon during the period from June to September and in the northern Amazon between October and December, with the period between 1981 and 2017. In the northern Amazon, a diminution of dry-day frequency was observed for the period January–March.
- A significant increase in wet-day frequency has been observed over the entire Amazon basin along with an increase in wet-day frequency during the same period and throughout the year; were observed in the northern Peruvian and Brazilian Amazon.

2. The analysis of daily precipitation data from 1980 to 2017 found a statistically significant increase in the number of consecutive dry-days in the Southern Amazon (defined as south of S-5°) since November–March, which is consistent with the observed drying trend over this region. This was accompanied by an increase in heavy precipitation days, and the maximum number of consecutive wet-days from February until June.

3. According to the IPCC 5th Assessment Report for Central and South America⁵, rainfall dynamics in the Amazon are expected to change significantly due to climate change, with a projected decrease in rainfall (ENSO) or decadal variability⁶. Recycling from the Amazon rainforests is responsible for 90-75% of its annual precipitation; hence deforestation and land-use can play an important role in changing the regulating role of forests⁷.

4. Table 1 provides a reference on the various climate-related hazards to which the region is exposed. In terms of extreme weather events, the most frequent are droughts (60%), followed by floods (50%), landslides (27%), droughts (19%), and forest fires (4%)⁸. As shown in the table, the Amazon has experienced a significant increase in the frequency of flooding, and nearly all countries are considered high or medium risk for wildfires. Fires are not only attributed to drought and extreme heat but they are largely tied with anthropogenic activities, especially the use of fire for clearing forests and agricultural land⁹.

### References

1. The majority of the Amazon biome is located in Brazil (59-2%), followed by Peru (31-7%), Colombia (7-8%), Venezuela (6-7%), Bolivia (4-0%), Guyana (0-5%), Suriname (0-4%), Ecuador (1-0%), and French Guiana (1-0%). The Amazon basin is 6.3 million km².

## B. The Amazon region is projected to have some of the highest increases in temperature due to climate change within South America, with the central Amazon area particularly affected.

According to RCP projections, the average annual temperature for the Amazon could increase by between 0°C and 2°C by the end of the century for RCP2.6 scenario, "between 3°C and 4°C in RCP4.5 scenario", and "6°C under RCP8.5". Positive temperature trends are projected for all countries in the Amazon region.

## According to RCP2.6, RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 projections, the average annual precipitation in the Amazon could decrease by up to 10% (RCP2.6), increase by between 10% and 30% (RCP4.5) or increase by up to 20% (RCP8.5). In some areas it could experience a decrease in precipitation of up to 20%. Precipitation is expected to increase by 15% (RCP4.5) and 20% (RCP8.5) in some areas and decrease by 11% (RCP4.5) and 23% (RCP8.5) in others by the end of the century.

### 10 Floods are expected to increase in the future in terms of frequency and intensity[48]. This is likely to affect all countries in the Amazon basin. The western Amazon is expected to experience more frequent flooding in the rainy season, due to projected increases in precipitation. Zukunft et al.[56] project an increase in the number of days with heavy rainfall (≥ 20 mm) for the period 2041–2070 relative to 1986-2015 of 3.5 and 8.5, respectively, for the 100-year flood[57]. Deforestation further exacerbates these trends and contributes to increased flooding.

### 11. CMIP5 projections from Duffy et al.[45] and Sorribas et al.[62] project that by 2100, the area affected by heat and extreme drought will nearly double and triple, respectively, for the entire Amazon basin[63]. The region is expected to experience a significant increase in temperature and decrease in precipitation. The southern and eastern Amazon are particularly at risk of wildfires, and the northern Amazon is projected to be more vulnerable to droughts[45].

### 12. Summary for Policymakers point out that climate variability and change pose a great risk to the Amazon region. The Amazon is particularly at risk due to its direct exposure to climates, and its high vulnerability to changes in precipitation patterns and temperature extremes. It is home to over 30 million people living within or adjacent to the basin, including over 2 million indigenous peoples[47], many of which are dependent on agriculture, forests and other natural resources for their livelihoods.[48] At the same time, poverty levels in the Amazon region tend to be higher than those of most countries, which limits the populations' ability to prepare for and respond to climate change.

13. Expected changes in temperature and precipitation, and associated risks, will have impacts in key economic sectors such as agriculture, fisheries and tourism, and natural ecosystems in the Amazon region. These include impacts on suitability areas where crops are able to be produced, as well as loss of ecosystems and biodiversity, affecting ecosystem services.

### Deforestation and forest degradation

14. In addition to climate-related hazards, Amazon forests are being depleted at an alarming pace, exacerbating soil erosion and ecosystem degradation. Short-sighted, unsustainable development throughout the region has led to high levels of persistent deforestation rates over the last decade. The rate of deforestation in the Amazon biome is estimated to be 10% higher than that of Amazon forests in South American countries were listed in Table (2019). By mid-2020, deforestation had grown % compared with the previous year.

#### Table 2 - Overview of forest loss in the Amazon in 2019, by country

| Brazil | 1,290,600 | 94.8% |
| Colombia | 91,450 | 79.4% |
| Ecuador | 113,000 | 95.4% |
| Peru | 161,600 | 100.0% |

Source: GRII (based on Global Forest Watch data)

#### Key underlying causes of deforestation in each country are distinct; changes in land-use to meet growing demand for food, fibres, timber and energy, are considered a primary cause of forest loss and/or expansion of the agricultural frontier and pastures for cattle grazing. Illegal logging, mining and hunting also contribute (Ceretti et al., 2020; Potriokas et al., 2019). In Ecuador, small-scale ranching and subsistence farming are major drivers of deforestation in the Amazon biome. In Guyana and Suriname, shifting cultivation and forestry activities also lead to degradation impacts, attributed primarily to illegal logging (Bennett et al., 2015).

#### Brazil, commodity production and cattle ranching are main drivers of the conversion of forests to land use for agriculture and livestock production. The expansion of the agricultural frontier and pastures for cattle grazing, along with illegal crops, mining and logging have also contributed (Ceretti et al., 2020; Potriokas et al., 2019). In Ecuador, small-scale ranching and subsistence farming are major drivers of deforestation in the Amazon biome. In Guyana and Suriname, shifting cultivation and forestry activities also lead to degradation impacts, attributed primarily to illegal logging (Bennett et al., 2015).

#### Indeed, in Amazon countries, the Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land-use (AFOU) is at the same time economic development and a major source of environmental degradation, and carbon emissions (Table 3 and Figure 7). Over the period 1990-2018, AFOU accounted for % of total GHG emissions were driven by the AFOU sector (IPCC, 2020).

### Deforestation and forest degradation exacerbate climate change risk and bring the basin closer to its tipping point.
The findings of the last few decades support climatological models' forecasts of a warmer Amazon soon after the end of the 21st century. This will lead to increased evapotranspiration, soil moisture loss, and loss of its ecological functions to sequester carbon, retain carbon reserves and biodiversity, and control hydrological and biogeochemical cycles. Such trends towards ecosystem degradation and loss of ecosystem services are expected in the Amazon basin by 2050 (Moreira and Souza, 2018). Deforestation is closely linked with drought in the Amazon basin, where the conversion of

## Overview of the Amazon tipping point and forest dieback / "savannization" mechanism

| Source: Marenco and Souza, 2016, p. 21 |
| --- |

Without intervention, growth in demand on unsustainable use of agricultural land supply (for crop and livestock production) will continue to increase the rate of deforestation and forest degradation in the region, critically affecting ecosystems over the long term. The Amazon rainforest is a vital terrestrial carbon sink and its loss would have significant impacts on global climate change. Its unique biodiversity and ecosystem services are irreplaceable. The Amazon also provides essential health outcomes while addressing food insecurity and rural poverty[1]. The Amazon is projected to have its largest impact on global climate change by 2050, with a potential increase in temperature of between 4°C and 6°C by the end of this century (2030-2050). Should these trends remain unchecked, impacts on natural capital stocks and future health of ecosystems could be severe. Preliminary estimates from the 108 suggest that reaching an Amazon tipping point[2] would generate a cumulative GDP loss over US$25 billion.

[1] World Economic Forum (WEF), "The Future of nature and business", New Nature Economy, p. 2020.
[2] World Wide Fund for Nature, "Living Forests Report Chapter 3: Saving Forests at Risk", 2015.
[3] Climate change is defined as a long-term shift in the average temperature or precipitation of a region. It can be caused by natural factors such as changes in solar radiation and volcanic activity or human activities that increase greenhouse gas concentrations, such as deforestation and burning fossil fuels.

Climate change suggests that a transformed, degraded "savannization" of mostly southern and eastern Amazon may occur (Nobre, C., et al., 2015).

20. Climate change has economy-wide implications. No economic sectors are spared the impacts, which are yet to be fully understood and quantified, especially with regards to ecosystems, security, and the potential adaptation costs. The impacts of climate change on poverty persistence are known to be broad, including impacts on terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems, impacts in food production, livestock, fisheries and aquaculture, impacts on human health, mortality and morbidity, quality, quantity and reliability of water resources, infrastructure, energy supply, agriculture, transport, tourism and labour productivity. Climate change could further induce the displacement of people, sometimes leading to conflicts over land use and access. These impacts are expected to reduce economic growth and higher levels of poverty.

Cutting these trajectories requires concerted regional efforts to increase private participation.
1. The Amazon Pact is a multi-stakeholder agreement on an Amazon basin to restore deforestation, increase forest cover, and restore degraded ecosystems to strengthen climate mitigation, improve the resilience of local communities, support biodiversity conservation, promote species’ recovery and the enhancement of biodiversity, and ultimately restore ecosystem functionality.

On September 8th, 2019, the Heads of State and Heads of Delegation of Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru, Suriname, and Venezuela signed the Amazon Pact. The agreement established that the cooperation among Amazon countries is an important condition for the conservation of the Amazon basin’s biodiversity and ecosystems. The pact established 16 actions related to the fight against deforestation and forest degradation; the conservation and restoration of degraded areas; the strengthening of the capacity of local communities, indigenous peoples, and civil society organizations in managing natural resources and protected systems; the active participation by women, by indigenous and tribal peoples and by local communities in the sustainable development of the region; as well as the promotion of financial mechanisms to implement these actions. The pact also established a mechanism for monitoring progress (the Global Environment Facility’s (GEF) Amazon Sustainable Landscapes Program (ASL)). On August 11th, 2020, the Pact signed an agreement with the World Bank to support the implementation of the pact and mitigate the repercussions of the COVID-19 pandemic; and the urgent need for a financing initiative for the region’s sustainable development. The pact is also a platform for dialogue between governments and civil society in an efficient way makes it impossible to think of governments as sole leaders on this agenda. The business community has a key role to play, not only by supporting the Pact but also by committing itself to invest more in sustainable productive practices. In fact, the Leticia Pact includes specific action for the promotion of agroforestry systems and production systems, including value chains, as well as sustainable business development.

On September 8th, 2019, the Heads of State and Heads of Delegation of Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru, Suriname, and Venezuela signed the Amazon Pact. The agreement established that the cooperation among Amazon countries is an important condition for the conservation of the Amazon basin’s biodiversity and ecosystems. The pact established 16 actions related to the fight against deforestation and forest degradation; the conservation and restoration of degraded areas; the strengthening of the capacity of local communities, indigenous peoples, and civil society organizations in managing natural resources and protected systems; the active participation by women, by indigenous and tribal peoples and by local communities in the sustainable development of the region; as well as the promotion of financial mechanisms to implement these actions. The pact also established a mechanism for monitoring progress (the Global Environment Facility’s (GEF) Amazon Sustainable Landscapes Program (ASL)). On August 11th, 2020, the Pact signed an agreement with the World Bank to support the implementation of the pact and mitigate the repercussions of the COVID-19 pandemic; and the urgent need for a financing initiative for the region’s sustainable development. The pact is also a platform for dialogue between governments and civil society in an efficient way makes it impossible to think of governments as sole leaders on this agenda. The business community has a key role to play, not only by supporting the Pact but also by committing itself to invest more in sustainable productive practices. In fact, the Leticia Pact includes specific action for the promotion of agroforestry systems and production systems, including value chains, as well as sustainable business development.

20. Climate change has economy-wide implications. No economic sectors are spared the impacts, which are yet to be fully understood and quantified, especially with regards to ecosystems, security, and the potential adaptation costs. The impacts of climate change on poverty persistence are known to be broad, including impacts on terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems, impacts in food production, livestock, fisheries and aquaculture, impacts on human health, mortality and morbidity, quality, quantity and reliability of water resources, infrastructure, energy supply, agriculture, transport, tourism and labour productivity. Climate change could further induce the displacement of people, sometimes leading to conflicts over land use and access. These impacts are expected to reduce economic growth and higher levels of poverty.

Cutting these trajectories requires concerted regional efforts to increase private participation.
1. The Amazon Pact is a multi-stakeholder agreement on an Amazon basin to restore deforestation, increase forest cover, and restore degraded ecosystems to strengthen climate mitigation, improve the resilience of local communities, support biodiversity conservation, promote species’ recovery and the enhancement of biodiversity, and ultimately restore ecosystem functionality.

On September 8th, 2019, the Heads of State and Heads of Delegation of Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru, Suriname, and Venezuela signed the Amazon Pact. The agreement established that the cooperation among Amazon countries is an important condition for the conservation of the Amazon basin’s biodiversity and ecosystems. The pact established 16 actions related to the fight against deforestation and forest degradation; the conservation and restoration of degraded areas; the strengthening of the capacity of local communities, indigenous peoples, and civil society organizations in managing natural resources and protected systems; the active participation by women, by indigenous and tribal peoples and by local communities in the sustainable development of the region; as well as the promotion of financial mechanisms to implement these actions. The pact also established a mechanism for monitoring progress (the Global Environment Facility’s (GEF) Amazon Sustainable Landscapes Program (ASL)). On August 11th, 2020, the Pact signed an agreement with the World Bank to support the implementation of the pact and mitigate the repercussions of the COVID-19 pandemic; and the urgent need for a financing initiative for the region’s sustainable development. The pact is also a platform for dialogue between governments and civil society in an efficient way makes it impossible to think of governments as sole leaders on this agenda. The business community has a key role to play, not only by supporting the Pact but also by committing itself to invest more in sustainable productive practices. In fact, the Leticia Pact includes specific action for the promotion of agroforestry systems and production systems, including value chains, as well as sustainable business development.

# Bioeconomy: Private role in bio-businesses and forest conservation

25. Because of the Amazon’s diverse biological, land and water resources, the bioeconomy[1] follows the ideal framework for the development of sustainable, productive models and practices in AFOLU in the Amazon region. The bioeconomy is defined as a set of economic activities that are based on the use of the Amazon's natural capital and forests (from this point forward defined as bio-businesses, see [5]). This concept includes the production of goods and services from forest resources while conserving them and reducing GHG emissions. In parallel, being conscious of risks and vulnerabilities in the mainstreaming of adaptation and mitigation strategies is essential to ensure that the region’s response to future climate-related impacts already unavoidable to this day (Table 4).

## Table 4: Overview of potential climate change impacts in the Amazon region, and adaptation and mitigation interventions and benefits for selected bio-businesses

| Sector / economic value chain | Impacts of climate change | Adaptation interventions and benefits | Mitigation interventions and benefits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sustainable agroforestry: Shea Butter/Cacao | Zones better suited for arable coffee production will be lost due to increased insect pests (Gomes et al. 2015; Duarte-Carvalho et al., 2016). Droughts and floods will reduce coffee production in Brazil (Gomes et al., 2020) | Interactions: Move to agroforestry systems, intercropping with other crops, pest management and integrated pest control practices. Increase shade cover to maintain 75% of the area suitable for coffee production. Reduce water use by decreasing the mean daily temperature in the growing season from 31°C to a maximum temperature of 2–3°C (Gomes et al., 2015). Optimize irrigation and erosion adaptation practices such as terracing, mulching, improved planting material, and management practices that reduce chemical inputs. | Interactions: Agroforestry systems combining food crops, fruit trees, and shade trees; manual weeding; intercropping with other tree species. New coffee agroforestry areas should be established in the 12.30°C/year separation zone relative to the current conditions and up to 15.2°C/year separation zones on degraded land. Plantations established on degraded land. |
| Sustainable agroforestry: Shea Butter/Cacao | Higher temperatures and erratic rainfall changes cause decreased fruiting success and yields, with yield reduction in areas where coffee is grown (Gomes et al., 2020). Reduction in current suitable cacao production areas, with negative economic impacts on cocoa production. | Agroforestry systems combining food crops, fruit trees, and shade trees; manual weeding; intercropping with other tree species. Manual weeding; composting; mulching; improved planting material; and management practices that reduce chemical inputs. Optimize irrigation and erosion adaptation practices such as terracing, mulching, improved planting material, and management practices, which will increase water use efficiency. Correct quality and timing of fertilizer application to avoid soil depletion | Interactions: Agroforestry systems combining food crops, fruit trees, and shade trees; manual weeding; intercropping with other tree species. The converted and new areas should be established in the 12.30°C/year separation zone relative to the current conditions and up to 15.2°C/year separation zones on degraded land.

### Native perennial forest systems (Value: Colossal)

### Non-timer natural forest products (NFTP) (Value: Not real)

Studies indicate that a warming climate will reduce the mean primary productivity (NPP) by 10-45% and increase GHG emissions scenarios. When the direct effects of increased NPP are considered, this reduction in emissions reduces but to a lesser extent of 33% due to the increased NPP. Decrease in quality and quantity of timber and other forest products and pest and disease outbreaks (Dufres, 2019).

### Native species aquaculture (Value: Colossal)

Changes in the ecological balance that foresee decreased availability of fish food resources and changes in the behaviour of the species, degradation of areas that are suitable for fish production, and decrease in river flows (Stern et al., 2018). Loss of 1-3% of total fish stocks is expected with decreasing in fish catches (de Faria Brito et al. 2020). The loss of fish stocks is strongly correlated to drought intensity (Fabbri et al., 2023).

### Interactions: Use of deep-rooted species and non-tree crops

Increased productivity is supported by above below ground interactions with C4/PCo2/Farmer sequestration avoided.

Brack nut enrichment and other agroforestry practices (e.g., silvopastoral management) can be used to reduce agricultural environmental degradation. Enrichment in Brazil nut plantations has been subjected to logging or conversion activities that could lead to increased carbon emissions.

### Interactions: Growing indigenous local fish species (Colossal)

In the case of small-scale aquaculture operations, it is expected that demand total fish species in small-scale aquaculture operations will increase. This will demand higher water quality and quantity, hence improving access to clean water for aquaculture biodiversity, better water use efficiency, increased irrigation water flow and income generation (McGrath et al., 2023).

### Activities: Reference forest and native forestry plantations (p. 37; see also Oromia p.)

- **Activities:** Restoration of reference forest and native forestry plantations can increase ecosystem services, such as carbon sequestration and soil organic matter accumulation. Tree topping by trees similar to those in the reference forest is recommended.
- **Benefits:** Reduced deforestation and degradation (Liu et al., 2013). Increased soil organic matter and soil below tree canopy. Improved water retention capacity which should reach an expected 50–70% CO2-equivalent sequestration.

### Activities: Community-based nature tourism

- **Activities:** High-biodiversity, high-community-based nature tourism (e.g., birdwatching, wildlife viewing, and revenue from diversification of local economies) can be developed.
- **Benefits:** The establishment of protected areas reduces the risk of invasive species invasion. Protection of forests and other natural resources leads to habitat conservation and biodiversity preservation. Increased carbon stocks equivalent to 10–20% CO2-equivalent.

## 26. However, bio-businesses and the nature of investments related to them pose various challenges in raising capital. Since 2015, the environmental investment sector has been growing rapidly. This expansion includes investment blueprints for sectors in the bioeconomy, expanded capital in private equity funds, and increased interest from institutional investors. The amount of green bonds issued is also increasing. Yet, bio-businesses in general are still not ready to absorb the minimum levels of investments required by international financial institutions. In addition, the technical expertise needed to implement these businesses are often associated to small and medium-sized enterprises and to payback periods that are longer than what investors are willing to take on risks.

### Raising the regulatory space for bio-business development

## 27. Amazon countries do not have dedicated legal frameworks governing the bioeconomy, although there are countries that have recently embraced the concept under different instruments. All target countries have some form of forest law and/or land use planning legislation in place, which can be used as payments for ecosystem services, forest concessions, environmental incentives, supply chain legislation, or other forms of financial mechanisms to support sustainable development and/or scientific research. In countries like Brazil and Peru, sub-national governments have also embedded bioeconomy-related activities into their broader national strategies. However, the majority of these businesses is still at its infancy; based on programmatic objectives and lacks sufficient legal anchorage.

#### A. Investing in the conservation of biodiversity and ecosystem services considers forest concessions.
The sector is dynamic throughout the region, with consolidated legal frameworks and countries exploring new approaches to forest management and private sector engagement (Table 5) [2]. Concessions can incorporate sustainable practices in forest with proper management plans, while also providing economic benefits. This model has been successful for timber products, giving governments a role in private resources to forest conservation [3]. In addition, increasing financial gains from sustainable products, activities or services will benefit not only private concessionaires but also the local communities and countries, especially those associated with poverty eradication and recognition of community forest management.

### B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

#### Problem Statement
In the Amazon basin, its various ecosystems—which include forests (lowlands and montane), peatlands, freshwater wetlands and others—are experiencing changes in their dynamics as a result of climate change-induced increase in temperature, changes in precipitation and its variability, and extreme hydro meteorological events. These changes are leading to increased evaporation rates and reduced rainfall, which is a key component of the surface water flow and energy balance of the target ecosystems. The impacts of these changes are further exacerbated by human activities such as land use change, including deforestation (small and large-scale), land degradation, and wildfires, with CO2 being emitted into the atmosphere as trees die and vegetation burns. These changes in the Amazon basin have significant implications for the local communities that depend on them, especially when hydro meteorological events affect the productive systems and the people and businesses (micro, small, medium and large) that interact and depend on them.


---
**Page 20**
---

3. As described in Section B.1, these climate-change induced warming of the Amazon, with more severe and sustained droughts, will make the forest ecosystems more vulnerable to degradation and loss of its ecological functions. The average temperature in the Amazon is 6.5°C warmer, pump one less than what was above the third tier of carbon and show a considerably higher fire risk than intact forests (Longo et al., 2020). In the last decades, the Amazon region has experienced an increase in temperatures and precipitation variability, which have led to a decrease of its vegetation cover. It is predicted that if more than 20-25% of the Amazon's original forest cover is lost, the entire biome could be transformed into savanna (Lima et al., 2018). The loss of forest cover also leads to a reduction in the amount of water vapor released by trees and plants, which reduces the humidity levels. As shown in Figure 3, deforestation causes climate feedback loops that reduce precipitation and increase temperatures, leading to changes in the albedo and surface roughness, and that reduce evapotranspiration and rainfall recycling.

Shift in the development pathway

4. Cognizant of the social, economic and environmental challenges associated with the loss of natural capital, governments are increasingly looking for effective ways to promote a low emission-high carbon stock, climate resilient, more sustainable and inclusive development. Substantial efforts are being made by governments to address these challenges through various initiatives that are ambitious and somewhat intermittent. Although the investor community at large has already started to contribute to sustainability solutions in the forest sector, there is still much room for improvement. In order to further increase low in emission, climate resilient sustainability solutions, International commitments under the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) have been made by governments and the financial sector in sustainability overall, triggering awareness on the relevance of forest protection, restoration and sustainable use. However, despite these efforts, only a small proportion of resources deployed at scale, and finance for conservation still represents only a small portion of the broader sustainable investment market.

The region's importance of natural capital and its economic relevance offer considerable opportunities to mobilize private investments for bio-businesses. Throughout this proposal, bio-businesses are defined as businesses that generate revenues from the use of natural capital and ecosystem services that lead to reduced emissions, higher carbon stocks, net gains in natural capital and increased climate resilience. These include but not limited to: agroforestry systems, sustainable forest conservation and preservation of ecosystems, recyclability and/or use of recycled materials, accreditation or certifications, efficient use of renewable resources (linked to climate change mitigation) and/or inclusion companies. The study is based on the assumption that there are 10 million hectares of degraded land in countries with poverty levels, proportion of natural-vegetation cover, and exposure to climate change (linked to climate change vulnerability). These areas can be restored and managed as sustainable intensification and/or extensive ecosystem conservation that leads to reduced emissions, higher carbon stocks, net gains in natural capital and increased climate resilience. Programme Goal

36. The overall goal of the Programme is to reduce GHG-emissions and increase the resilience of targeted value chains and ecosystems in the Amazon region by enabling the conditions to increase the flow of low-emission goods and services from the Amazon region.

Fostering private investment in bio-businesses and their value chains can be determinant of the economies to achieve maximum use of the potential of the bioeconomy. The financial sector can play a significant role in this process, through the development of innovative financing instruments with longer grace periods, flexible conditions and preferential interest rates. Both developers and financial institutions need to have access to adequate information on the risks and opportunities associated with projects that help attract private capital[2]. Promoting the bioeconomy also requires boosting processes such as incubation, training, and capacity building for entrepreneurs in the sector.

Access to financing, especially in the early stages and expansion is particularly relevant[3]. This will allow to shift from a paradigm where the Amazon region is seen as a source of environmental degradation and social value, project high emissions and increased climate vulnerability, to a new paradigm where the bioeconomy increases the economic and social value of the Amazon forests and ecosystems in a sustainable way (Figure 4).

Figure 4. Paradigm shift of bioeconomy interventions in the Amazon region

[2] For instance, local infrastructure and connectivity gaps or deficiencies in land tenure and rights across the region.

## Barriers and Risks

38. As showcased in Figure 4.1, this paradigm shift will help address both climate risks and drivers of land use change and conversion. Climate induced risks affect the Amazon ecosystems, productive systems (small, medium and larger producers) and other economic activities in the Amazon biome (including timber production, cattle ranching, soybean production, etc.). As shown in Table 4.1, the climate-induced risks due to changes in temperature and precipitation include the reduction in suitable crop production areas (coffee, cocoa) and interruptions to crop growth cycle of native palms: a decrease in yields of coffee, cocoa, palm oil, bananas, pineapples, avocados, guavas, passion fruits, berries, Brazil nuts, etc. due to inadequate growing conditions; risk of biodiversity loss; changes in ecosystems (e.g., deforestation and forest degradation); increased fire frequency; reduced availability to regulate key hazards (food, drought); loss of carbon stocks, and reduced evapotranspiration and rainfall recycling; increased vulnerability to pests and diseases; increased soil erosion; increased flooding events and decreased rainfall; damage to key tourism hotspots and tourist infrastructure creating higher operational costs.

39. Partly due to the relative novelty of bio-business products, technologies and markets, important barriers exist that prevent funds flow to private investments with strong climate change adaptation and mitigation impacts. These include: (i) a lack of market demand for bio-products; (ii) a lack of a proper value to natural capital used sustainably in business. This is relevant as these investments are expected to generate returns on investment, but the returns are not yet recognized by markets; (iii) nature conservation, sustainable production practices and livelihoods in local communities. In line with this, the following barriers exist that prevent funds flow to private investments:

(i) Weak institutional environment for low-emission, climate resilient bio-business development: The lack of national systems, platforms and tools for the valuation of assets that produce carbon sequestration and other ecosystem services; weak institutions that are not able to build the capacity to attract private investment to activities that protect natural capital. In all target countries, existing policies do not provide a clear framework for the protection of biodiversity in forests and land-use, without assigning a tangible value to conservation, climate mitigation, or increased climate resilience. Changes are needed to address the absence of policies that support

Lack of data, measurement systems, taxonomies, and natural capital and forest asset value accounting systems that can be used by businesses to assess their exposure to risks related to climate change mitigation and adaptation. Measuring and reporting systems, metrics and indicators are key to build credibility.

### (iv) Immature capital and financial markets, which limits supply of funding for bio-businesses: In LAC, the problem is particularly acute because the region has a very low level of financial development. The region may not be very high, micro-businesses in the region do not have access to sources of venture capital, angel investors or other forms of private equity financing that are more likely to scale. About half of the region's banking sector lacks depth, with around 80% of total financial demand unmet. ### Programme structure: Components and Activities

40. Enabling businesses to incorporate low-emission and climate resilient practices while maintaining competitiveness and profitability. The following components will be addressed:

- **Adoption and expansion of adaptation and mitigation activities**: This component will address the intensification or enhancement of value chains in bio-businesses, biochemical, molecular and genetic properties of natural ecosystems, etc., which are most vulnerable to impacts of climate change in those value chains. These activities include agroforestry, irrigation best practices, sustainable forest management, conservation and restoration of native species, improvement of ecosystem services, sustainable forest management and conservation, plantations of native species, and the reduction of carbon emissions that mitigate climate change and strengthen resilience of local producers and ecosystems.

- **Adoption and expansion of adaptation and mitigation activities**: This component will address the intensification or enhancement of value chains in bio-businesses, biochemical, molecular and genetic properties of natural ecosystems, etc., which are most vulnerable to impacts of climate change in those value chains. These activities include agroforestry, irrigation best practices, sustainable forest management, conservation and restoration of native species, improvement of ecosystem services, sustainable forest management and conservation, plantations of native species, and the reduction of carbon emissions that mitigate climate change and strengthen resilience of local producers and ecosystems.

42. First, commercial banks in target countries do not offer credit products specifically to bio-businesses or to the financial sector. Second, there is a lack of information on the adoption and adaptation benefits of these businesses. Third, MSEs are leaner short-term, and financing is concentrated in larger companies. Where formal lending institutions exist, they are often too large to provide adequate support for MSEs, which is mainly the result of: (i) high operating costs to lend at smaller scales; (ii) high transaction costs due to the lack of information on MSEs; and (iii) a general lack of experience in assessing risk by financial institutions might not be unfazed by bio-businesses in many cases face legitimate bankability challenges (see description of incremental risks above) and capacity gaps (see description of incremental risks above). Fourth, there is a lack of innovative financing mechanisms to address the financial value of natural capital is not available. Innovative financing is key to address these issues, including a range of financial models and approaches to close market failures of traditional financials.

B

Innovative financing may use a combination of already established instruments, such as guarantees, loans, microfinance and tailoring, or introduce new products, such as equity, quasi-equity, or convertible bonds to express the value of natural capital in business. These innovative financial mechanisms (such as seedfunding or investmentmatching platforms that can yield results). In any case, these financial mechanisms can help manage risks and adjust incentives to encourage private actors to invest in biodiversity conservation.

42. Second, governments are increasing interest in the issuance of bonds to raise money for biodiversity conservation projects. This is partly due to the fact that the growing number of institutional investors is increasing their concern for environmental and social standards into their investment decisions*. Capital markets indeed provide an adequate platform for new mechanisms to be developed and tested, as well as for existing ones to be scaled up in response to environmental challenges altogether. For instance, with transactions ranging between US$300 million and US$1 billion, the green bond market is still relatively small compared to other asset classes, particularly from large mainstream investors**. Green bonds in LAC are still largely focused on energy and infrastructure projects. However, as the demand for green bonds increases, the supply side portfolio would need to be supplied to support the minimum size of a bond issuance, the potential for bio-bonds is significant. The current market conditions are not yet conducive to the development of a bio-bond in the context of the bioeconomy being large. In turn, improvement in capital markets for the bioeconomy would boost the demand for green bonds and other innovative financial instruments that can be used to finance projects under this framework.

43. To overcome the challenges described above, ensuring financing becomes available and projects are suitable for financing, and enabling the private sector to invest in biodiversity conservation is key. The use of innovative financial mechanisms and financial instruments that enable investments that protect and recognize the value of natural capital in business is expected to help offset the incremental risks and costs of these ventures for the private sector. This will allow them to compete with other projects or investments, which may be more cost-effective or/also less sustainable alternatives. Targeted instruments designed to meet bio-business needs can make financial markets more attractive to investors, while also helping to increase their profitability, addressing the "immature capital and financial markets" barrier. Knowledge and institutional mechanisms are needed to ensure that these innovative financial mechanisms are used effectively and, lack of standardized frameworks can help ensure a continued flow of quality projects that can be financed by private actors. Well-structured bond issuances to address the immature capital and financial markets barrier can also help to increase the profitability of investments for private sector actors through concessions. Well-structured bond issuances to address the immature capital and financial markets barrier can also help to increase the profitability of investments for private sector actors through concessions. The development of standardized frameworks to monitor biodiversity and climate vulnerability impacts, can increase the flow of funds to sustainable portfolios and pursue other issues - public and private – to undertake this type of investment.

45. Overall, the Programme can help create the foundation for markets that value the fundamental role of natural capital in business. This will be achieved by supporting the development of new production models in which private entities will be more willing to participate. Juxtaposing private actors with public institutions and other stakeholders is key to this process. The programme will support demonstration efforts, highlighting biodiversity and resilience as an intrinsic element of economic success. In turn, this may well enrole more private actors to enter these markets in the future and in the longer term. This will also help to increase the profitability of investments for private sector actors on activities that support GHG-emission reduction and lead to reducing vulnerabilities to climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL2.1 | PAGE 23 OF 82

## B

### Background

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a global mechanism for climate change mitigation and adaptation that aims to provide financial resources to developing countries in order to help them reduce their greenhouse gas emissions, adapt to the impacts of climate change, and build resilience. The GCF was established by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 2010 as a multilateral entity with the mandate to mobilize and disburse funds for projects and programs that contribute to the global goal of addressing climate change.

### Proposal Structure

The proposal is structured into three main components:

#### Component A: National greenhouse gas emissions reduction
- **Objective**: To support countries in reducing their greenhouse gas emissions through the implementation of national low-emission development strategies (LEDS).
- **Activities**:
  - Developing and implementing LEDS.
  - Strengthening institutional capacity for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

#### Component B: Climate-resilient development and adaptation
- **Objective**: To support countries in building resilience to the impacts of climate change through the implementation of national adaptation plans (NAPs).
- **Activities**:
  - Developing and implementing NAPs.
  - Strengthening institutional capacity for climate change adaptation.

#### Component C: Financial and technical cooperation
- **Objective**: To provide financial and technical assistance to countries in order to support their efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and build resilience to the impacts of climate change.
- **Activities**:
  - Providing financial resources for projects and programs that contribute to the global goal of addressing climate change.
  - Providing technical assistance to countries in order to help them develop and implement LEDS and NAPs.

### Implementation

The proposal is implemented through a combination of national, regional, and international efforts. The GCF works closely with governments, civil society organizations, private sector entities, and other stakeholders to ensure that the projects and programs it supports are effective and sustainable.

### Monitoring and Evaluation

The GCF monitors and evaluates its projects and programs in order to ensure that they are achieving their intended outcomes. This is done through a combination of quantitative and qualitative methods, including regular reporting by project implementers, independent evaluations, and stakeholder consultations.

### Conclusion

The Green Climate Fund proposal aims to provide financial resources and technical assistance to developing countries in order to help them reduce their greenhouse gas emissions and build resilience to the impacts of climate change. The proposal is structured into three main components: national greenhouse gas emissions reduction, climate-resilient development and adaptation, and financial and technical cooperation.

| Component | Description |
|-----------|-------------|
| A         | National greenhouse gas emissions reduction |
| B         | Climate-resilient development and adaptation |
| C         | Financial and technical cooperation |

### References

[1] World Bank. (2015). Green Climate Fund: Overview of the Fund's Structure, Governance, and Operations. Washington, DC.

[2] United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. (2010). Decision 3/CP.16: Establishment of the Green Climate Fund. Bonn, Germany.

[3] GCF Secretariat. (2018). Annual Report 2017-2018. Washington, DC.

### Notes

* The proposal is subject to approval by the GCF Board and may be amended as necessary.

## Eligibility Criteria: Activities/positive use of activities, investments and use of resources for value addition

### Renewable Energy (RE)
- **Solar energy**
  - Solar photovoltaic (PV) systems
  - Concentrated solar power (CSP)
  - Solar thermal systems
  - Solar water heating systems
  - Solar drying systems
- **Wind energy**
  - Onshore wind farms
  - Offshore wind farms
- **Hydropower**
  - Small-scale hydroelectric projects
  - Medium-sized hydroelectric projects
- **Biomass energy**
  - Bioenergy production from agricultural residues, forestry residues, and other biomass resources
  - Biogas production from organic waste (e.g., livestock manure)
  - Anaerobic digestion of organic waste
- **Geothermal energy**
  - Geothermal power plants
  - Geothermal heat pumps
  - Enhanced geothermal systems

### Energy Efficiency (EE)
- **Buildings and urban infrastructure**
  - Building retrofits with high-efficiency windows, doors, and insulation
  - Building retrofits with high-efficiency lighting fixtures
  - Building retrofits with high-efficiency heating, ventilation, air conditioning (HVAC) systems
  - Building retrofits with high-efficiency water heating systems
- **Transport**
  - Electric vehicles
  - Hybrid electric vehicles
  - Plug-in hybrid electric vehicles
  - Battery electric vehicles
  - Fuel cell vehicles
  - Low-emission buses and trucks
  - Low-emission rail transport
  - Low-emission maritime transport
- **Industry**
  - Energy-efficient industrial processes
  - Energy-efficient manufacturing equipment
  - Energy-efficient production facilities

### Sustainable Land Management (SLM)
- **Soil conservation practices**
  - Conservation tillage systems
  - Cover cropping and crop rotation
  - Agroforestry systems
  - Soil amendments to improve soil health
  - Water harvesting techniques for improved water management
- **Tree planting and afforestation**
  - Tree planting programs
  - Afforestation projects
  - Reforestation projects

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 26 OF 82

#### B

49 With a view to using a multi-stakeholder approach (including investors, banks, project developers, producers, specialized advisory institutions, and RAD institutions) the Programme focuses on enabling the conditions to increase the flow of funds to bio-businesses in the bioscience of the Amazon. The Programme will be implemented through three components: (i) Sovereign investment loans; (ii) Sovereign investment grants; and (iii) Concessional financing solutions for bio-businesses.

50 In order to achieve Programme goals and in line with the theory of change, a multi-component structure is proposed. Since not all components and activities might be relevant or applicable in all participating countries, the Programme will include an indicative framework that aims to provide a list of possible –not mandatory– uses for the funds at the country level.

### Program Components

#### 1 Concessional financing solutions for bio-businesses: Support to private bio-businesses and their supply chains through dedicated financing solutions designed to help overcome the prevailing financing challenges in the Amazon region. The Programme will provide concessional financing solutions that meet both the supply and demand sides of the financing, using a set of tailored instruments to finance projects in various stages of development. Concessionality is particularly relevant for companies establishing new operations or expanding existing ones, as it allows them to reduce their financial costs and risks, reducing the cost of finance and increasing its tenure enables amortization profiles that better suit bio-businesses’ cash flow characteristics.

1. Sovereign investment loans ($US153M) and investment grants ($US16M) will be provided by IEE to EEs to finance private projects via on-balance sheet earmarked financing lines and/or debt-for-equity swaps. The Programme will also provide sovereign loans (funded with GCF and/or IDB resources) and investment grants (funded with GCF resources) to financial institutions that would extend credit to beneficiaries.

a) Loans ($US153M): allow IEEs to provide lending in various modalities to beneficiaries directly or via local financial institutions (LFI) that would extend credit to beneficiaries.
b) Targeted Sovereign Investment Grants ($US16M): will be provided by IEE to LFsIs, matching the needs of LFIs in each country-specific context to help them properly manage increasing demand for financing from bio-businesses. The Programme will provide grants to support the type and types of businesses they finance—with the objective of enhancing and expanding their credit offer for investments eligible under this Programme. The management of incremental risk may materialize through a combination of: (i) longer grace periods; (ii) lower interest rates; (iii) more favorable amortization schedules; (iv) longer grace periods (more attuned to the revenue profile of some bio-businesses); and/or (v) other terms that are more favorable for the LFI. The Programme will provide grants to LFsIs with a higher risk profile earlier stage, less mature companies, those with less robust financial statements, or those in countries where the business environment is more vulnerable to climate/agricultural risks, etc. Investment grants will allow the provision of risk-

## 2.2 Equitable access to finance for bio-businesses in the Amazon

Equitable access to finance is a key element of the GCF’s strategy to increase global investment flows to early-stage bio-businesses in the Amazon region in the form of direct equity, equity-like instruments and other financial products. The GCF will provide funding through its own funds under administration by the IDB (such as IDB Lab) and other co-finance. These schemes are considered effective tools to maximize the potential impact of financing operations in early-stage high-potential bio-businesses. They can be used for both equity investments and equity-like investments while offering more value for innovative companies. Equity will be used for more mature bio-businesses (i.e., those that have already demonstrated a proof-of-concept or are at an earlier viable product phase), have positive revenues, and are looking for gaining market traction. Grants, in the form of seed funding, can be provided to early-stage bio-businesses (i.e., those that are still in the pilot phase to start selling a determined product or service). The GCF will also provide co-finance with other IDB funds (such as IDB Lab) for equity-like investments. Activities under Component 1.2 will support ongoing IDB Lab initiatives, including:

a) Regenerative Agriculture ($53MM equity and US$4M grant): a pilot fund that invests in high-potential bio-businesses in the Amazon region with a focus on regenerative agriculture in the Amazon. The EEW is Kaeite Investments, a local and reputable fund management company with experience in early-stage investments. The GCF will provide seed funding to support due diligence and deploying the best financial products for companies and investors.

b) Direct funding for climate-tech and biotech companies ($US4.5M equity): IDB Lab has identified 10 high-potential bio-businesses that are developing startups that develop innovative products and services that result in better climate change resilience for local households, communities, or regions. The GCF will provide seed funding to support these investments based over 20 years of experience in financing impactful start-ups in LAC.

Eligibility criteria for beneficiary bio-businesses under Component 1.2 may only be used to directly support projects meeting such eligibility criteria, but also consider the following: (i) they must have a strong business model and a clear plan that will help overcome critical barriers for their development (for example, to enhance market access and product or service adoption), (ii) they should be able to demonstrate a proof-of-concept or early viable product phase, (iii) they should be able to demonstrate the ability to scale up their products or services in the short term with international standards and norms (including the adoption of certifications), (iv) access to local and international markets, and (v) technology upgrading in priority value chains.

## 1.3 Technical cooperation ($US5.6M): will finance activities under the following categories:

### a) Bio-business pipeline development and supervision ($US2.5M): Advisory directed to giving the development of bio-businesses in LAC the capacity to identify, develop, and mitigate activities in bio-business that will reduce the negative impacts of climate change.

#### i. Supporting capacity of potential beneficiaries (US$138K)
(i) advisory and extension services for developing countries to improve their capacity on greenhouse gas measurement and monitoring systems (including participatory monitoring systems with local indigenous communities), (ii) technical assistance in the development of bio-businesses that comply with international standards and norms (including the adoption of certifications), (iii) access to local and international markets, and (iv) technology upgrading in priority value chains.


---
**Page 33**
---

### Supporting capacity of LPFs (public or private) (US$3M): (i) governance, management and fundraising of different financial structures, including SPV, where applicable; (ii) monitoring and evaluation of LPF performance against agreed indicators, including data collection systems and indicators; (iii) coordination systems seeking to articulate actions among NGBs, financial intermediaries and other relevant institutions; and (iv) optimization of resource allocation.

#### ii. Supporting biosciencepro, knowledge dissemination and R&D (US$2M): (i) creation and strengthening of a global network of biosciencepro hubs for advancing open science, collaboration, and knowledge exchange for selected accredited R&D institutions; (ii) development of biosciencepro strategies and dissemination of results (open science); (iii) establishment of an international biosciencepro fund to support R&D and innovation projects by bio-businesses and R&D institutions jointly.

##### b) Toolkit for scaling up: This initiative will provide a set of tools to help LPFs in the prototype phase or already operating with a minimum viable product to (i) better connect with stakeholders and match their projects with investors; (ii) provide greater access to markets opportunities; (iii) identify and address market barriers; and (iv) develop strategies that support their transformation into scalable businesses, capable of attracting and absorbing funding offered by LPFs. This initiative will also promote the inclusion of women, indigenous, Afro-descendant and peasant communities among producers and companies with a focus on the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity in Amazonian peoples in bio-businesses." This activity will support the following ongoing IBD Lab initiatives:

- **Amazonia 4.0 (US$1M):** Support to local communities (including indigenous) start-ups focused on the development of innovative products based on the use of non-timber forest products. NGO that has a long track record in developing solutions to create economic opportunities through the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity, with a focus on the reduction of deforestation in four Brazilian Amazon communes that depend on the cocoa and coffee sectors.
- **Leticia Platform (US$3M):** Executed by the Worldwide Fund for Nature (WWF), which finances the creation of a local multistakeholder platform aimed at Geographic Information System (GIS) data collection, analysis, and dissemination to support decision-making, promote and link targeted bio-businesses to potential investors.
- **Amazon Bioeconomy Accelerator (US$7M):** A two-track business ecosystem in Amazonia that will start-up entrepreneurs and more mature small and medium-sized businesses can increase access and connect with investors. This project will run in two complementary tracks: Track 1 will develop the transactional marketplace to support emerging bio-businesses and connecting them with investors. To do so, it will (i) identify and connect bio-businesses with potential investors; (ii) provide a platform for bio-businesses to participate in a transactional marketplace; Track 2 will develop the transactional marketplace to support more mature small and medium-sized businesses. It will also: (i) market analysis (local, regional and global) to identify bioeconomy products being exported or imported by Amazonia; (ii) provide technical assistance to bio-businesses with this track to strengthen the skills and competitiveness of the producers; (iii) local partner engagement and capacity building to help strengthen the skills of local producers of selected

Amazon biocconomy products, and increase their international competitiveness; (9) business matchmaking mechanisms to generate export deals for new or existing Amazon biocconomy products; (10) a dedicated transactional marketplace for cross-border transactions of the future online Marketplace and; (v) design and implementation of a dedicated, transactional marketplace for cross-border transactions, that includes an application that allows buyers and sellers to connect directly. The platform will be built on top of a crowd-equity financing platform). The marketplace will be designed, developed, implemented and operated by the Amazon Biocconomy Alliance, the Global Forests Initiative Alliance, The Nature Conservancy, ICB Lab, the Natural Capital Lab and could collaborate with global platforms such as eBay, Alibaba, Mercado Libre, etc. It is expected that both initiatives herein proposed by IDB Lab: Amazonia 4.0 and Leticia Platform; Amazonia 4.0 will be a key enabler for the transactional marketplace and Leticia Platform will be the marketplace in the future. The Leticia Platform can also benefit from the Marketplace given that it will allow to connect buyers and sellers of forest products and other ecosystem services undertaken by NGOs that may be featured in the bio-business marketplace in the future.

### Institutional and regulatory environment (US$19.1M): While policy frameworks are in place at all levels, there is a need for strengthening them through a more integrated and coherent regulatory approach to natural capital valuation, access to genetic and biological resources, and incentives for sustainable forest management. The project will support the development of grants that will finance advisory for and/or training of government entities in the following areas:

- Strengthening national systems in valuing natural capital/forest assets (US$ 2M): (i) strengthening national capacity on valuation of natural capital through policy, strategy, and taxonomy; integration of natural capital accounting practices in decision-making both at the national level and within forest management units; (ii) integrating ecosystem services mechanisms, landscape-level monitoring and management systems (e.g., REDD+); (iii) developing a national system for environmental impact assessment and risk management planning; (iv) integrating land-use change and forestry management in public budgets; (v) developing country's System for Environmental Economic Accounting (SEEA) to support the valuation of natural capital assets, including forest resources. This will also include capacity building at the national level as well as with neighbouring sub-national entities to tackle specific issues, and vii) articulating regional and national policies and strategies that promote the participation of indigenous peoples, Afro-descendant, fishing and peasant communities) and their territories, in order to promote the conservation and sustainable use of natural capital assets, including forest resources and specific actions carried out by women.

### Developing and implementing innovative schemes for private participation in the sustainable management of forests and other conservation areas (US$15.1M): appropriately frame forest concessions and Public-Private Partnerships (PPP) as efficient policy instruments to promote private sector investment in forest conservation, with local communities. Forest concessions—both private and community-based—have shown their potential for promoting the conservation of natural resources and land speculation out. They can also contribute to the instauration of sustainable forest management practices and the promotion of biodiversity conservation within protected conservation areas, including legally protected areas but also other effective area-based conservation measures (AECMs). Concessions are a mechanism that allows active role in active role* Contractual structures designed will seek to promote economic efficiency, value-for-money, fiscal sustainability and improved conservation outputs and results. These mechanisms should be designed with the objective of promoting forest conservation when local communities and indigenous territories are involved, including areas where they have the right to decide on their own use and management of natural resources or land or that they can sell their own carbon credits.

Complementary roles of thematic bond issuance and other financial instruments (e.g., NBSAs, NRIs) to leverage capital market financing through the issuance of thematic bonds (those earmarked for specific use of funds, such as green or sustainable bonds that include biodiversity conservation goals) can be complementary in several ways: (i) they can provide a broader social and environmental financing programs, with better financial conditions relative to other sources? Novelty lies in the fact that thematic bonds are not only aligned with the SDGs but also have concrete performance conditions required by investors. To address the aforementioned cost barrier, IDB will use CIF grants for guarantees to reduce the cost of issuing bonds. The CIF grant will cover up to 50% of the issuance costs (including underwriting fees) in accordance with IDB policy and as per documentation of the guarantee funded with IDB resources. This is a new approach that allows IDB to provide additional support beyond its usual resources to assist the guaranteed debtor in reducing the financial charges it has to pay to IDB during the life of the bond, thereby making the issuance more attractive for investors.

The CIF grant will be used to cover the difference between the cost of issuing the bond and a firm financing cost offered by the bonds’ credit to bring funding to bio-businesses at viable terms. ## B

### II.A Small Grants Program (SGP) (US$10M): Improving the design and execution of the whole Programme by including a meaningful and organic stakeholder engagement with quantifiable results for all actors involved, from the SGP itself to the Amazon SMEs and their communities in the Amazon region. It also aims to revitalize and activate the Amazon's key stakeholders' inclusion by widening a range of stakeholder profiles, perspectives and perceptions, contributing to improve meaningfully the Programme's impact on the ground. The SGP will be implemented at three levels: public sector, private sector, civil society, and other development actors, with quantifiable results. The SEP will be implemented in two levels: technical cooperation, public consultation and dialogue, and partnerships.

#### II.A.1 Small Grants Program (SGP) (US$10M): This activity seeks to empower and enable local stakeholders—smallholder farmers, indigenous people, Afro-descendants and campesinos—to take on initiatives and enable bio-businesses to enhance and influence development outcomes with a biocentric approach. The SGP will be implemented in two main components: (a) Investment Grants, including clarification of funding priorities will be designed together with a SGP steering committee of key stakeholders; (b) the SGP Steering Committee will develop a prioritization matrix for selection of SGP activities to make the selection process more effective. Activities under the SGP, composed of both Technical Cooperation and Investment Grants, are divided into three categories: (i) programme management activities (administration, knowledge and project lifecycle management); (ii) catalytic grants for projects that involve several communities focused on productive activities and conditions to facilitate community participation in bioeconomy value chains ("Productive Projects"); and (b) catalytic grants for projects that involve several communities focused on enabling conditions to facilitate community participation in bioeconomy value chains ("Enabling Projects").

The Amazon 4.0 concept is a technology-driven methodology with the aim to develop a green circular economy that takes full advantage of the value of a "standing and permanent productive forest" so that it can reduce deforestation and capitalize on the Amazon biodiversity, and its ecosystem services. It will be implemented in three main components: (i) the development of a biocentric approach for SMEs and their communities; (ii) the creation of an enabling environment for SMEs and their communities to access market opportunities leveraged by other initiatives such as those led by the IDB Lab, such as Amazonia 4.0, Letica Platform, Regenerative Accelerator and Bioeconomy Marketplace; and (iii) a local mapping exercise will be performed to create a database consisting of potential providers and recipients of services and products in the region. The SGP is being implemented through three main actors: (i) programmes and institutions providing traditional peoples and communities and their bio-businesses with biocentric knowledge, skills, and tools that are adapted to their specific socio-economic and cultural characteristics, as well as their main lines of economic activity; (ii) other local partners (public and private sector); and (iii) local market opportunities that can stimulate bio-business growth and resilience. The SGP is being implemented in partnership with the IDB Lab, which will be responsible for implementing the Programme together with the SMEs and their communities. IDB Lab is planning for Conexus to be the regional coordinator of this subcomponent, considering its expertise in business development and innovation, as well as its experience in working with indigenous peoples.
