#### B.2. Implementation arrangements: (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

As provided in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), signatories to the AMA and the GCF Facility Access Arrangement (FAA) will be responsible for the transfer of funds and use of GCF funds for the financing of this Programme. The IDB will ensure all necessary management and supervisory arrangements are in place, including the establishment of a Sub-Programme under the Programme and will be solely responsible for the management and administration of GCF resources in accordance with the terms and conditions set forth in the AMA and the FAA.

The Subsidy Agreement (SA) that is activated as part of this agreement will establish the terms and conditions for specific IDB operations to be considered for funding from this Programme. Consistent with its regional and multi-component structure, detailed arrangements—including between the IDB and counterpart countries—will be established in a separate document for each combination of component activities and third parties involved (see Table 6), as well as intended final beneficiaries.

During the implementation period, following the entry into effect of the FAA for this Programme, GCF resources will be transferred to the IDB. The assessment of the institutional capacity and eligibility of EEIs will be individually undertaken as part of the preparation process for each Sub-programme. This will include a review of the EEI’s policies, procedures, and due diligence standards. Only after a Sub-project has been approved by the IDB, this Sub-project will be eligible to receive GCF resources.

Following a request from a specific country to the IDB, a Sub-project will be prepared, assessed and approved as an individual IDB operation financed with GCF resources under this Programme. If particular circumstances exist that make it necessary for the Sub-project to be implemented in phases, each phase shall be consistent with the objective, scope and activities established in Section B.3 of this proposal and aligned with the overall objectives set forth in Section E.5 of this proposal. All IDB operations shall also be included in IDB programming and strategic

67. In its capacity as AE, the IDB will administer resources received from GCF for Component II.2. The implementation of the green bond issuance will be the responsibility of the public entity issuer in accordance with the terms agreed upon between the public entity issuer and the IDB. Upon receipt of the approval of the relevant guarantee operation (i.e., Sub-project), the IDB will conduct an institutional credit assessment on the Sub-project, and if approved by the IDB, the Sub-project will be entered into the GCF portfolio. 68. GCF guarantee grant resources will be allocated for reducing the cost of the financial charges of IDB funding operations. For this purpose, the IDB will make payments from the GCF Account to the GCF Account prior to the effectiveness of the guarantees. GCF grant resources will be disbursed by the IDB as a grant to the public entity issuer in order to reduce the cost of the guarantee. If the public entity issuer pays in full to the IDB but has received a grant, it defrays its cost. In this way, the guaranteed debtor benefits from the grant and the GCF Account is credited with the amount of the grant. If the financial charges paid at the time of the guarantee, and the charges paid by the guaranteed debtor up to that moment are less than the amount of the grant, a partial refund of the grant must be made.

Experience with each record of the AE and EEs

79. The IDB is the main source of multilateral financing for LAC. Since 1961, the IDB has provided almost US$248 million for projects to reduce poverty, raise standards of living, spur economic growth, protect natural resources, and foster integration and trade. The IDB is well-positioned to support Amazon countries in their efforts to achieve these goals. This experience with each record of the AE and EEs draws on the experience gained during design and implementation of several operations related to the promotion of private sector development in LAC. These include the IDB’s role as a lead agency for the Global Environment Facility, mobilizing public capital in partnership with a vast array of public and private institutions. Only within the last decade has the IDB begun to focus on climate change financing, but it has already committed US$3.1 billion in green investments in 19 countries. Additional support for thematic bond issuances with NABs is also provided.

80. All this experience entails long-term relationships with governments and partner institutions, including over 40 NGOs throughout the region and in target countries, as well as through understanding of the financial landscape in each country. The IDB has a strong track record of supporting public sector reform by supporting innovation in various sectors across the region and developing innovative financing mechanisms to support these reforms.

81. Appropriate coordination between public and private parties, as well as among the different financing mechanisms, is essential to ensure success when implementing the structure the Programme. The IDB will work with the Government of Colombia (GoC) and other relevant stakeholders in the sector, including ministries and public agencies involved in the bioeconomy. The Programme will be guided by IDB focal points for each country, who will provide technical support and guidance to the GoC and other stakeholders.

82. Due to its internal structure, the IDB is able to sustain this multidisciplinary approach through the implementation of the Programme, adding value and offering knowledge based on regional and international experience in the areas of climate change adaptation and mitigation, biodiversity conservation, and sustainable development. The IDB has a strong track record of supporting the intervention in each participant country, including technical feasibility of the proposed customized solutions, as well as financial viability of the projects. The IDB will also work with other sources of funding, and engagement of corresponding counterparts at a country and regional level.

83. NABs acting as EE for Component I have a public mandate to promote inclusive and sustainable private sector development in their respective countries. As such, they play a fundamental role in the design and implementation of projects that are aligned with national priorities underserved by local markets, offers several financial support mechanisms to companies of all sizes to enable them to access finance, and has a proven track record of supporting innovative enterprises. In most cases, these entities will be those with which the IDB has a relevant track record, based on prior investment loans or other cooperation agreements (see Annex A and Annex B).

B.8. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words; approximately 2 pages)

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 42 OF 52

#### B

**64. Attracting the private sector to participate in bio-business investments is essential to support Amazon region-wide strategies related to climate change mitigation and adaptation of forest ecosystems. By nature, private capital flows are necessarily reliant on return expectations (the existence of and possibility to deliver returns). If private investors are not able to achieve a level of financial returns adjusted for risk that are attractive in comparison to other investment opportunities, private funding will be limited. This is particularly true when the natural value of forest assets is not properly accounted for in their business models. In addition, there may be a lack of awareness among private sector actors about the involvement of private actors that are fundamentally willing to make efforts to support sustainability in their business but have yet to see them as financially attractive.**

**65. Encouraging private sector participation requires a system that facilitates access across markets if private sector participation is to be encouraged. The system needs to be comprehensive enough so that the dynamics of financial flows can be managed and understood, and so that the private sector can play an important role in allocating resources directly to the development of the bioeconomy, especially since it is so relevant to the government's development goals. But overall fiscal constraints in targeted countries are substantial, and there may not be sufficient public funds available for all the challenges that governments need to address. Particularly amid the recent COVID-19 crisis, restrictions have become larger, and the ability of governments to provide support has been limited. The GCF can help by leveraging limited own and international resources to provide incentives so that private funds flow more easily into the bioeconomy. This is particularly true when it comes to concessional funding as this enables this transaction to happen at a faster pace.**

**66. Concessional financing and grants are an integral part of the set of mechanisms used by development finance institutions (DFIs) in their efforts to support private sector participation in bio-business investments, especially since they should bring additional value without displacing other financing sources. Extending incentives with sufficient scale and scope will help to unlock private capital flows for bioeconomy projects that have positive externalities that would not be properly accounted for otherwise. When the concessionality is effective, it can also help to reduce transaction costs and risks in the development of bioeconomic impacts by leveraging private sector participation that would not happen without support.**

**67. As a general premise, the justification of the use of GCF concessional funds in this Programme is based on financial instruments that are designed to provide incentives for private investors to participate under conditions where they might otherwise be reluctant to do so. These include: de-risking and leveraging expansion and diversification of the bioeconomy. GCF's participation and concessional finance will allow for the private sector to take on more risk, while also providing a buffer against potential incremental costs and risks undertaken by private developers/porsons, banks and investors to make these projects happen. In this way, the private sector actors that are expected to leverage resources from other developers, lenders and investors.**

**68. Concessional loans, equity and grants under Component I, aim at delivering more adequate financial incentives for private sector participation in bio-business investments and increasing their ability to amortize profit more suitable to bio-business investments. De-risking support can catalyse positive lending conditions that are more appropriate for the development of bioeconomy projects (i.e., reducing losses for financiers (in turn reducing loss provisions and improving profitability). First risk, loss, risk sharing, and guarantees are provided by GCF in order to reduce transaction costs and risks. This will help to unleash the potential impact of leveraging equity from private investors, helping address the "valley of death" for innovative companies. GCF participation is critical to:**

- **supporting the overall ambition and development reach and impact of the Programme (more than twice as per IDB estimations); this includes an estimated private sector leverage potential of over USD 10 billion only in Brazil alone.**
- **achieve the required level of concessionality and other critical financing terms (e.g., tenure, risk-tolerance).**

B. Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

95. Successful implementation of Programme activities should translate into increased confidence on and engagement with green business models by households and firms, leading them further investing in structuring and implementing bio-businesses without donor support in the future. The microfinance institutions and other financial intermediaries will be encouraged to provide more robust connections to demand and markets. These relationships and mutual beneficial transactions are strengthened through the Programme. In addition, the exit strategy of the Programme has been designed to induce investment and credit in private actors, with a view to ensuring that such activities produce results that are sustainable over time. These mechanisms are intended to ensure that the financial intermediaries will be able to provide financing for bio-businesses when market conditions can be improved to match the risks and costs of bio-businesses.

96. For this purpose, in addition to the use of guarantees and insurance schemes, concessionality is essentially used to incentivize a systematic and effective financing system for formal bio-businesses, based on their potential for development. By tackling first-mover risk, the Programme will help build strong portfolio of bankable projects that can be scaled up by the actor of finance and businesses alike, encouraging other actors to participate in these markets later in time. The Programme will make use of a combination of financial instruments, including guarantees, insurance schemes, and large alike, without requiring further subsidies, as well as to expand their lending practices and conditions to include green and sustainable bonds, which are increasingly being used by both local public and private institutions, the use of certifications and sustainable principles, and the development of new markets.

97. Second, regarding the work with thematic bonds, besides securing sufficient investment in bonds earmarked for bio-businesses and their direct contribution to scaling up financing for these ventures, the programme will also contribute to the creation of a market for green and sustainable bonds through demonstration, since supported bonds will offer another platform to demonstrate the viability of these assets. In this regard, it is important that the Programme will be able to provide guarantees and standards on the issuer side (for issuance, monitoring and reporting) that at the local level will remain in place, even after the end of the Programme, as well as to ensure the creation of a market for potential issuers. Within the context of the global drive toward green and sustainable bonds, the instrument will be able to contribute to the development of this market by providing guarantees previously applied by major international financial institutions. Above and beyond the introduction of the instrument in developing markets, resource mobilization will expose institutions and impact investors in developed countries to new opportunities for investment, which will also contribute to the development of domestic capital markets, which will eventually also facilitate the participation of international investors.

98. Institutional and specialized knowledge barriers are addressed by technical cooperation activities under each component structure. These have been crafted to incorporate capacity transfer and training on the part of financial intermediaries, as well as the creation of networks and partnerships and networks involved in the bioeconomy, better gauge the risks involved and overcome the information asymmetry that may exist between the actors of finance and businesses alike. The activities will replicate the activities financed by the Programme, as well as strengthen the delivery of associated development services (e.g., extension organizations or incubators), know-how should be inherited by others that are also

More concretely, these are related to IDB’s and EEI’s operational and administrative costs, as well as the cost of any other third-party services from the Programme, which will be determined on a case-by-case basis, depending on various factors such as the type of intervention (i.e., the specific characteristics of the project), the level of complexity, required allocation, and the timeframe for implementation of each operation**.

101. The Amazon region is one of the priority regions in which the IDB focuses its support to the Amazon region and the support for the bioeconomy. These include activities at the regional, national and subnational levels, including those that contribute to the sustainable development of the region. For instance, the IDB has supported the creation of a regional framework for the Amazon region with IDB support from the Natural Capital Lab, such as the Programa para el Desarrollo Sostenible de la Amazonía (PDSA) and the Amazonia Regenerativa Accelerator program, which are part of the Amazon Initiative.

102. In the context of its internal Amazon Initiative financing strategy, and in line with its broader engagement in partnerships and mobilization, the IDB will map out a portfolio of projects that will be implemented by the IDB and other society actors, working in the same or complementary areas of actions proposed by CI and IDB. The IDB has already worked closely with CI and Conservation International (CI) and IDB have a very good track record of collaboration on forest conservation and sustainable development initiatives. For this proposal and to ensure that all investments maximize opportunities to achieve multiple benefits, including biodiversity conservation, climate mitigation and resiliency goals, an ad hoc working group for the Amazon region which will include all three organizations and is part of the Stakeholder Platform for the Amazon Initiative.

103. Complementarities between proposals of these organizations have been analyzed in detail by the IDB. For example, CI’s proposal to apply the model applied in the Bhutan for Life Project (FP 050), in the projects Natural Legacy of Peru (CI 21330) and Colombia Heritage (CN 22863). WWF will also be supporting the creation of a regional framework for the Amazon region, and the mitigation and adaptation benefits they provide through improving governance and management of protected areas. CI is also proposing to establish sustainable financing mechanisms for national networks of protected areas. The IDB will support this proposal by providing technical assistance to the Programme by securing sustainability of public land, while increasing economic opportunities in the region through the development of eco-tourism for forest products (such as Brazil nuts) as well as valuable ecosystem services such as carbon sequestration and water regulation.

Scaling up regional action to reduce deforestation and increase resilience to climate change is a priority area for CI’s work in the Amazon region. Colombia, Ecuador and Peru, CI will also be supporting the creation, strengthening and scaling up of enterprises and value chains which reduce vulnerability to climate change. In addition to its focus on institutional support for forest conservation and sustainable development, CI has proposed the management of at least 35 million hectares of forest in the region; CI’s initiative

### 106.

The proposed innovative financing mechanisms and incentives embedded in newly developed schemes, the need to address the complex institutional arrangements and models and identify the complexities involved in coordinating the various actors efficiently, calls for specialized technical cooperation support for capacity building and technology development/transfer. This includes providing technical assistance such as extension services and coordination mechanisms for bio-businesses, investors and producers, as well as other stakeholders (see Section B.2). The GCF will also provide support to develop a reliable natural capital valuation, innovation in public-private contractual schemes in forestry, standardization and harmonization of standards and certification systems, development of an innovative bio-business pipeline, monitoring and verifying environmental impacts of bio-business (see Section B.3).

### 107.

Awareness raising and dialogue will be an important transversal element of the Programme to maximize the impact of the GCF funding. The awareness raising activities will target a wide range of stakeholders: community, domestic and international institutional investors and government relevant agencies. The transfer of knowledge and lessons learned from the Programme will be supported by the GCF through a built-on-over time (see [105]). As a standard practice, the IDB is also committed to making knowledge and lessons learned from the Programme available to the International community, including financial and development institutions, to enable further replication and upscaling of the approach.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

108. Due to the diverse nature of bio-businesses that may ultimately benefit from the improved business environment and financing enabled by the Programme, and the characteristics of the proposed scheme – in which private sector actors are expected to provide the bulk of funding for the programme (through private actors) - estimations of potential impacts were made based on an indicative portfolio of value chains.

108a. After a characterization of the potential sectors and value chains in the bioeconomy, analysis was undertaken to understand the size and definition of actors in each chain. A more detailed review was conducted for the most promising value chains (Annex 2) with respect to their characteristics and limitations of the existing business models. By applying to each value chain an indicative allocation of Programme funding (US$10 million per value chain, US$50 million overall), the study modelled specific productive interventions. Where possible, these were then converted into a set of policy recommendations on a per basis and translated into mitigation and adaptation benefits (see Section E and Annex 3).

110. Based on the methodology and assumptions used in the model described above, the expected annual potential impact of Programme activities is estimated to be US$2 billion to US$4 billion. The lower bound is $6.2 million and 124.3 million respectively (see also Annex 2). The expected volume of resources to be leveraged by the Programme is US$50 million, with an indicative allocation of US$70 million (US$10 million per case as per Section C.1 plus US$40 million from other sources). The total number of direct beneficiaries is estimated at 238,000 and the total number of indirect beneficiaries at 695,000.

111. It should be noted that in order to maintain a conservative approach on the potential for mitigation calculations for this section consider only the impact expected to be produced by the direct use of the US$70 million of Programme funding. The estimated impact is therefore considered as a lower bound inasmuch as the funds are expected to be used in a revolving manner throughout the maturity period of the GCF loan, and the benefits will be sustained beyond this period through the establishment of a sustaining support framework that will keep funds flowing to bio-businesses that lead to GHG emission reductions. The total impact is therefore likely to be higher than what has been estimated here. This indirect impact of the Programme, mainly the result of the demonstration effect, would be difficult to quantify and is considered to be additional to the values presented. Hence, the potential impact assessment and quantification presented in this section should be seen as being in the lower bound of the range of possible actual impacts attributable to Programme activities.

- It supports ventures that are commercially viable or expected to become so in the short-to-medium-term

# D. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Social and economic co-benefits

At a macro level, investing in bio-businesses in the Amazon is necessary to protect the regional economy from climate change impacts. The projected increase in agricultural productivity and income is expected to be significant (Section B.1). This does not only involve environmental challenges, but also the ability to create jobs and growth, making economies more competitive. The mainstreaming of these bio-businesses will have a positive impact on the Amazon’s economy by creating new economic markets, which leads to positive effects on several aspects, including:

- **Economic competitiveness:** Increased investment in bio-businesses at the macro level enhances competitiveness, productivity, and value creation of firms related to these ventures.
- **Diversification of crop production systems and improvements in the provision of agro-biodiversity services:** This diversification will lead to reduced government expenditures on subsidies (where applicable) or increased tax revenues through improved agricultural productivity.

Increased private investment overall can also trigger ripple effects throughout the economy, which in turn reflects in human well-being and welfare consistent with assessed targets of the Sustainable Development Goals.


---
**Page 57**
---

Development Sustainable Goals (SDGs), specifically those related to poverty, hunger, health, work, innovation consumption and responsibility production, water, cities, climate, oceans, land, and partnerships (PRES, 2019). Thus, loss of biodiversity is not only a threat to the environment but also a social issue. In this sense, Programme activities are expected to contribute to co-benefits related to:

- increased sources of income for agricultural and non-agricultural communities in rural areas
- direct and indirect employment creation
- technology development

Gender equality and women's empowerment impact

120. The Programme will strive to support gender equity by incorporating mechanisms and a strategy that targets and monitors the participation of women as beneficiaries. For instance, technical training or financial services can be provided to women in rural areas. In addition, the Programme will consider financing gap for this group, especially among MSEs. In addition, investments in projects that support MSEs are usually more likely to benefit women than men because women tend to have a higher share of ownership and control over resources as well as in local enterprises that can deliver services or become part of the supply chain. Programme implementation will also consider the potential for women's participation in decision-making processes and the potential to incorporate specific measures to ensure gender equality in alignment with DDB’s Operational Policy on Gender Equality (OP-075). The Programme will also seek to promote equitable access to the benefits of investments supported by different social groups, including women and indigenous communities. Whenever possible, the potential to promote equitable access to benefit from investments is considered in terms of land tenure, participation in decision-making processes, and activities. In addition, another crucial outcome is the improved articulation of indigenous, afro-descendant, and other vulnerable populations’ rights and interests through the implementation of the Operational Policy on Indigenous Peoples (OP-765), the Strategy for Indigenous Development (ON-CRITI), and the National Plan for Afro-Colombian People (PNAF). The Programme will also respect and promote cultural forms of conservation and territorial management. That is why the activities included in this proposal are designed to address gender equality, social inclusion, financial analysis, strategic legal, and other challenges. The Programme also promotes intercultural communication between indigenous communities and the State, as well as their participation in international markets and with different governmental institutions or economic sectors. Finally, this Programme includes the lessons of previous IBO interventions that attempted to strengthen indigenous, afro-descendant, and vulnerable populations’ rights and interests through the implementation of strategies that guarantee the social, environmental and cultural visibility, as well as the sustainability of the Programme.

### D

#### 120. None of these countries have either a proper regulatory environment or specific mechanisms to address the challenges associated with the development and implementation of bioeconomy strategies in the context of climate change adaptation and mitigation.

#### 123. On the other hand, all targeted countries have financial systems that show low levels of depth, are inherently risk-averse and offer very limited financing alternatives for these ventures. Adequate financial instruments to support the transition towards a more sustainable bioeconomy and innovative business models are still lacking. In general, the risks associated to these projects, especially in the absence of proper legal frameworks, make them unattractive from an investor perspective.

#### 124. GCF resources are necessary for deploying mechanisms that can attract private capital, particularly from local financial systems and impact-driven investors to bio-businesses that can deliver climate benefits while also generating economic returns. This will require a strong enabling environment and capacity within local governments, along the finance and production value chains.

#### D. Country ownership (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

##### 125. Country-level ownership is crucial to ensure results over the long-term. This involves meaningful participation of all relevant stakeholders in the decision-making process, including governance support and alignment with nationally devised climate change strategies, and forest and bioeconomy related legal frameworks. The GCF will provide technical assistance to the respective countries to assist them and the respective country public authorities to ensure alignment with their existing and upcoming programs and policies. This will be supported by a dedicated knowledge management and capacity sharing knowledge. A strategic steering committee will be organized at the country level with the respective national government as chairperson, and GCF as co-chairperson to provide periodic strategic input, while the day-to-day execution is conducted by the designated EE.

##### 126. All targeted countries have well-established climate commitments and plans – both at national and sub-national levels. These include Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and National Adaptation Plans (NAP) (see Table 7). Many of these countries are also developing or have already developed REDD+ strategies that recognize the private sector's role in deploying investments to support their goals, and its potential to contribute to the paradigm shift to a low-emission and climate-resilient development path.

##### 127. Amazon countries have recognized the importance of the bioeconomy as an important tool to contribute to addressing climate financing and implementation gaps. Approaches to REDD+ and forestry policies offer opportunities for co-benefits in terms of biodiversity conservation, poverty reduction, and other strategies. Meanwhile, while most Amazon countries do not have dedicated legal frameworks governing the bioeconomy, several countries have recently embraced the concept under different instruments

Brazil has incorporated bioeconomy strategies in national documents such as the National Plan for the Promotion of Socio-Biodiversity Value Chains (2009), National Strategy of Science, Technology, and Innovation (2013), and the National Programme for Biobased Agricultural Inputs (implemented by Decree No. 10,375/2014).

- In Ecuador, the government has stated its vision to pursue the bioeconomy as a strategy for recovery and development of the country's economy, with the aim of diversifying agricultural ecosystems, and diversifying the country's production matrix to become more independent from fossil fuels. The National Strategy for the Development of Bioeconomy in Ecuador (2015) is a tool for sustainable growth. Ecuador has also adopted a National Pact for Sustainable Bioeconomy and a Law for Agro Biodiversity, Seeds and Promotion of Sustainable Agriculture.

The bioeconomy concept is also present in other national documents such as the Colombian Low Carbon Development Strategy, the Green Growth Strategy, the Circular Economy Strategy, and the National Plan for the Conservation and Restoration of Forests. In 2018 Guidelines to authorities regarding the application of available economic valuation methodologies, to promote the generation of information as a technical criterion to support the designation of natural heritage areas in Colombia.

- Bioeconomy concepts are embedded in multiple pieces of Peruvian legislation and development plans such as the National Strategy for the Development of the Amazon Region, the Law Promoting Investment and Development of the Amazon region. Concerning the accounting for biodiversity values in Peru, the Ministry of Environment has approved the Guide for the Economic Valuation of Natural Heritage, which aims to provide guidance on the matter so that decision makers can use this concept in the face of the need to make decisions regarding investments or projects.

- The Guyana's Low Carbon Development Strategy (LDC) establishes the country's intention to transition to a low-carbon economy, focusing on reducing emissions and degradation and investment in renewable energy sources. This includes the sectoral and sub-sectoral strategies.

- Suriname’s Policy Development Plan objectives give a strong indication of the country’s priorities related to climate change mitigation and adaptation. The policy development plan identifies those that contribute to a sustainable bioeconomy by strengthening environmental components in bioeconomic activities, such as agroforestry systems, forest-based industries, and other sectors that are prioritized in this regard include processing and/or use of residual wood and waste, non-timber forest products, and environmentally related use of the forests, including compensation for forest preservation.

12b. In general terms, all Amazon countries have sectoral or general policy frameworks that cover some aspects of climate change mitigation and adaptation, such as biodiversity conservation, supply chain legislation, organic agriculture, sustainable fisheries, ecosystems, or technological research.

- Brazil's NDC establishes that the country will enhance its national capacity in the conservation and sustainable use of forests and other terrestrial carbon sinks. The Brazilian government has also committed to the overall objective of contributing to the mitigation of climate change through the eradication of illegal deforestation and forest degradation. This is achieved by promoting the development of a low-carbon, sustainable forest-based economy, generating economic, social and environmental benefits for all stakeholders involved in the process. Brazil's NDC includes commitments on mitigation and adaptation that aim among others to prevent and control deforestation in the Amazon, prevent and control fires, and promote reforestation and afforestation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 58 OF 82

## Ecuador

Ecuador's NDC places strong emphasis on climate targets for the AFOLU sector. The NDC embraces a gender-sensitive approach and aims at promoting sustainable, resilient, inclusive, and poverty-reducing land use systems. It focuses on reducing GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+), as well as increasing carbon sequestration, greater food security, sustainable water-resources management, and an integrated approach to climate change adaptation and mitigation in the context of sustainable development.

## Colombia

Colombia's NDC sets emission reduction targets for 2030 and a carbon budget for its National Development Plan (NDP) 2014-2018. The NDC focuses on three goals in the AFOLU sector: i) halting the accelerated growth of deforestation, ii) promoting sustainable land use systems, and iii) increasing the resilience of ecosystems through management of natural forests, silvopastoral systems, and other forms of productive land restoration by over 20 million hectares. Colombia's NDC also includes a carbon market and an emissions trading scheme as economic instruments including a carbon tax and an emissions trading scheme. They also have a National Forest Policy, the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (SNA), and the National Climate Change Vision Program. Colombia's National Plan for Adaptation to Climate Change (PACACC) seeks to reduce the vulnerability of communities in the face of climate change by 2030.

## Peru

Peru's NDC focuses on reducing GHG emissions from deforestation, forest degradation, and land use changes in forests, as well as in mitigation in the land-use, land-change and silviculture sectors. Under current policy, land-use, land-use change, and forestry (LULUCF) activities are responsible for 54% of total GHG emissions, with a reduction target of 84% between 2012 and 2030. Peru is committed to reducing this source of GHG emissions, has declared the intention to reduce its emissions by 67% below business as usual (BAU) levels in 2030.

## Guyana

Guyana's NDC focuses on climate change adaptation and sustainable forest management, including the extension of 2 million ha of national forests under a new Forest Management Act. The NDC is aligned with the National Climate Change Policy Action Plan 2020-2030 as the main policy framework for addressing climate change in Guyana.

## Suriname

Suriname's NDC focuses on reducing GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, promoting a variety of adaptation measures in the forestry sector, and promoting and encouraging local community-based conservation. The NDC includes three key elements: 1. Implementation of effective management plans for primary and secondary forests; and degraded forest lands (AFOLU). 2. Promotion and encouragement of local community-based conservation activities on lands with economically valuable species which are resilient to the impacts of climate change, where communities have a strong interest in maintaining these resources. 3. Development of innovative financing instruments is one of them. The plan seeks to introduce financing instruments for the management of forests and other land uses that are consistent with national policies and strategies, including international cooperation and funding, and investments from the private sector and public budget.

## D

### Section 3.9: Regional cooperation is also key.
As previously described, the Leticia Pact signed by representatives from all target countries (Section B.1) lists various points on which they intend to collaborate, including committing to a common vision for the Amazon region and its conservation; strengthening women and indigenous peoples and the creation of educational campaigns on the importance of the Amazon conservation. Although the Leticia Pact is a good starting point for an overall vision for the Amazon region, it does not specify how this will be achieved. The IBRD will work closely with governments and accompanying their efforts to integrate climate change adaptation into broader development strategies, while also setting targets in their agendas as a priority. As described in Section B.4, the IBRD will also capitalize on its long-standing experience of working with countries in the region and a long history of collaboration with an extensive network of NDB and government agencies in LAC.

### 3.10: Perspectives of various local stakeholders have been considered for the development of this proposal.
Including indigenous peoples, civil society organizations, and other relevant actors. Specific implementation will ensure close consultation with local indigenous people and communities, as well as civil society organizations, including those that are part of the Amazon Pact, NGOs, CSOs, organizations and cross-national networks, such as Pangea pela Amazônia, Amazon 4.0, Fundação Terra, among others.

### Section 3.11: All operations carried out within the framework of the Programme will be targeted to countries' national priorities related to climate change and land degradation.
And their commitments under the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), the Ramsar Convention, the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and its Kyoto Protocol, the Paris Climate Agreement and their NDCs, NAPAs, the Sendai Framework and the SDGs. They will also be aligned with those undertaken by these countries under the amended Amazon Cooperation Treaty and the Leticia Pact.

### Section 3.6: Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Section B.5 describes how QOF resources are needed to structure financial instruments that can help overcome barriers to private investment in climate change adaptation and mitigation concessionally.
Besides the provisions thereby described, the risk of crowding out other private and public investment will be mitigated by some additional factors:
i. As previously described, micro-enterprises and small businesses (particularly MSEs) are also large financially underserved, both in terms of availability of risk capital for start-up and of adequately term debt from financial institutions. The Programme will provide a range of services to help these firms with their financial support. In doing so, the Programme strives to develop a market that is currently far from tapping its full potential. As this market develops, opportunities will be created (rather than diminished) in terms of private sector participation.
ii. The Programme implementation model will extend solutions for LFs and other relevant actors already acting as intermediaries between financial institutions and small businesses. These include microfinance resources; these entities are best positioned to reach poor, property assets and support beneficiaries. Partnerships with such organizations will be key to the success of the Programme in providing financing solutions, as well as various types of technical cooperation (see Section B.3) that will enhance their capacities to properly identify, assess and structure financing solutions that can effectively manage the risks inherent to these businesses and attract more investment.

**Page 62**

134. Based on our economic model and with a conservative approach (see Annex 3), the Programme is estimated to deliver an average GCF cost of US$26 per ton of Co2e abated. This expected GCF cost efficiency will be achieved by scaling up the implementation of various projects in the Amazonia (Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador) under the Pilot Program for REDD-plus results-based payments (ranging between 4 and 5 US$/ton of Co2e) as well as other GCF forest projects. From an effectiveness point of view, the Programme is projected to achieve a leverage ratio of 1:2.6 (i.e., US$1 million in co-financing will be leveraged for every US$2.6 million in GCF investment). In total, an investment of US$279 million investment is expected to leverage about US$719.1 million in additional public and private co-financing.

135. The Program's Economic Rate of Return (ERR) has been estimated at 49%. The estimation is based on financial flows stemming from an assumed portfolio of supported investments and the value of abated Co2e emissions, discounted to reflect the time value of money over a 7-year period. Internalizing carbon value increases the basic financial rate of return of the investments at the asset level (i.e., the ERR) by about 10 percentage points compared with the ERR calculated without internalization, reflecting the significant additional value of natural capital externalities when properly considered (in this case, through the inclusion of abated Co2e emissions). The ERR calculation is also conservative, as it does not incorporate other expected environmental and social benefits (including those stemming from enhanced climate change adaptation/resilience) into the model.

136. Availability period for the structuring and deployment of some of the financial solutions proposed at a year prior to the start of the Programme (i.e., in 2017, with an implementation period starting in 2018 and ending in 2024). This is required given the complexity of structuring some of these solutions. The availability period will be extended by one year for each additional year period, considering that: i) multiple LFTs will need to be involved to reach target bio-businesses, with each of whom a design, approval and deployment process will need to be followed; and ii) in some cases, the required obligation capacities will only be in place after some technical assistance activities are completed.

## Assumptions

The indicator measures annual CO2eq.

* The baseline estimate is 20 years on average.
* Total expected forest abatement over the lifetime of investments (20 years) is 1.2 million CO2eq.
* There are sufficient local public actors (public and government entities) willing to and capable of engaging in projects with capital natural assets.
* Private sector agents are willing to participate if they can make a profit from capital natural assets.
* Specialized service providers are available at competitive costs.
* Bio-businesses in each of the sectors adopt best abatement and mitigation practices and technologies.

The monitoring for impacts, outcomes and programme specific indicators will be conducted according to the Monitoring and Evaluation Plan specified in Annex 11.

All estimates are based on a range estimated at between 10-20% of final values, as the preparatory and appraisal process of all country projects is still ongoing. The baseline estimate is 20 years on average.

* The final value is based on a typical value chain in each of the sectors assumed to be financed, as per feasibility study. For detailed assumptions please refer to Annex 3.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

On account of the Programme design, which includes various countries, components, EEs and subprojects, it is not possible to monitor all activities in detail for each project. Therefore, only projects approved and financed – totally or partially – with resources from this Programme, and for which the EE has provided a monitoring plan will be subject to monitoring and periodic reporting to the GCF (see Annex 11).

Monitoring and evaluation of subprojects approved with funding from the Programme will follow the GCF’s M&E policy. This includes a requirement to define monitoring indicators at the project and results level, which shall be consistent with one or more of the activities and expected outcomes defined in the Project Document. Monitoring indicators may also be ex ante, based on existing knowledge at the time of preparation and approval of the Sub-project by the GCF. The EE will provide a monitoring plan to the GCF as part of the project proposal. Monitoring indicators will be integrated annually by the BGF in its internal monitoring systems.

Following IBE standard practices, agreements between the IBE and EEs will establish monitoring and reporting requirements for each approved subproject. These agreements will specify how to compile and maintain all information necessary. In some cases, additional projections, estimates or assumptions may need to be made based on the available data. Monitoring indicators shall be defined in collaboration with the EE and will be based on the information provided by the EEs in the periodic reports or with supplemental support provided by the IBE.

All REs are required to inform the IBE of the performance of activities under their execution at least on an annual basis. Annual reports generally include an in-depth examination on the performance of each approved subproject, including (i) a comparison between actual and planned results (including local counterpart and other sources, when applicable), eligible expenses financed, and the remaining budget; (ii) identification of lessons learned from the project implementation; (iii) evaluation of indicators on the results framework, including at the investment level.

In addition to the annual reports, BGF mid-term and final evaluations of all approved IIB sub-projects will be conducted. These evaluations are designed to assess whether the expected results in the result framework have been met; (i) identify lessons learned and provide recommendations for future projects; and (ii) determine if the project has achieved its intended impact.

These evaluations will be used as inputs to develop a final assessment.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk assessment and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### 148. Selected Risk Factor 1: Insufficient transformation of identified demand into fundable projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description
Business owners and developers must face several needs at the same time, and resources are limited. While the existence of a viable model is necessary for the process of investment decision-making, it is not alone sufficient to ensure that projects will be fundable. The risk of project failure due to lack of demand has increased in recent years as a result of recurring crises, including the most recent COVID-19, may have made private actors less inclined to invest in infrastructure projects. This is especially true for projects with high upfront costs and low returns, which are more likely to be affected by such events.

The probability of not reaching at least 37% of the total potential demand (US$50 billion) via CAPEX estimated to be supported by equity and debt instruments enabled by the Project’s “green” veto clause is 28%. The risk of a bill being passed in the next two years that would reduce the amount of funding available for projects on approval and implementation periods in each country increases the probability to medium. Impact is considered high if this risk materializes, under no circumstance should it affect the totality of Programme funding.

### Mitigation Measures
149. Technical cooperation activities under each component include activities to support the demand side of each component. Bio-business pipeline development and business development activities under Component I are examples of such activities.


---
**Page 77**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 73 OF 82

## Selected Risk Factor 2: Impossibility to deliver monitoring data on all supported projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description
150. Because the bioeconomy is a relatively new concept that involves various specific technical skills, monitoring the results could become challenging. In most cases, monitoring will depend on secondary sources of information such as project reports or other data. For Component I, these institutions may have good monitoring systems in place for their overall credit operations and can be expected to apply similar practices to the bioeconomy. However, if they do not follow best IBP practices, the probability of this risk is considered high based on the scope of the Programme, and additional measures will need to be put in place to ensure that the required monitoring data are available among relevant institutions, as identified in some target countries, which leads to numerous databases and dashboards. In addition, there may also be a lack of harmonization between these systems, so it is not always possible to guarantee that all investment follow strict eligibility criteria.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
151. As part of the implementation activities for each IBP operation using funds from the Programme, specific monitoring and reporting arrangements will be established, including templates and indicators, means of verification, and a schedule for submission of reports. The terms and conditions of these arrangements will be agreed with the IBP prior to the disbursement of funds as per IBP policy. In addition, transversal technical cooperation activities include support for overall supervision and coordination at the country level, including the establishment of an integrated monitoring system that includes a set of monitoring systems that are adequate to incorporate the majority of beneficiary projects to the overall impact assessment.

## Selected Risk Factor 3: Unexpected changes in political will

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

### Description
152. Given that future developments around the COVID-19 pandemic are still uncertain, a risk has been identified in potential delays on implementation at country level, as a result of governments in target countries prioritizing new emergency measures that may arise in the coming years. So far, government funding through public procurement is not considered to be an appropriate instrument for supporting bio-businesses with the participation of the private sector as they consider it key to promote the sustainable development of the sector and its competitiveness. However, the Government has recognized that the sector can play a significant role in the post-COVID recovery phase. Hence, this risk is considered low.

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Misuse of materials or technology procured for unauthorized, improper or illicit use

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description
Due to the diverse nature of the bio-technology sectors, models, practices and technologies, use of funds for purposes that are aligned with the project’s objectives can be difficult to standardize. Hence, it is risk identified in a possible misuse of some elements procured with loans or grants from the Programme.

The risk is considered low as IDB policies and procedures are deemed sufficient to guarantee checks and balances to prevent this.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- EES eligibility and disbursement of funds to them will be contingent on their compliance with institutional capacity assessment and formal evaluation. These assessments include specific guidelines for use of proceeds, whether it is reimbursable or non-reimbursable money. The IDB supervises compliance of all requirements as per the terms and conditions of the loan agreement (see Section 4 and G.3) and carries out supervision of all beneficiary entities and projects.

## Selected Risk Factor 7: Exposure to foreign exchange risk of beneficiaries or LFsIs as a result of financing under Component I.1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forex | High | Low |

### Description
Foreign exchange (FX) risk (or currency risk) exists in financial transactions under Component I.1, as loans are denominated in USD and loan borrowers are likely to be disbursed in a currency other than USD (i.e., the local currency in each country), especially in the case of smaller firms for which transaction costs may be prohibitive. The FX risk is also associated with the volatility and fluctuations between the local currency and the USD before the loan repayment is completed, as the association between these two currencies can be volatile.

In some countries, particularly in Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) region, countries are generally prone to undergo sporadic unstable economic conditions, but NDBs acting as EES generally do not have the capacity to hedge this risk (i.e., place a USD or the market and manage profits). As such, borrowers may be exposed to FX risk. To mitigate this risk, it is recommended that they increase their capacity to hedge this risk (i.e., place a USD or the market and manage profits) or import goods and services are also subject to FX risk but can take measures to manage it as well. If all of these measures fail to reduce the exposure in the target countries is high, it is important for them based on the mitigation measures below.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- If the analysis at the country-level identifies that a substantial portion of the loans will be required in local currency (the IDB will ensure that the country-level analysis informed about the FX risks and sufficiently converted to financial institutions interested and willing to hold USD positions locally, and that the size of the market is sufficient for them to do so) or if the loan is denominated in a currency other than the particular country. As for bio-businesses exposed to FX risk, hedging strategies to mitigate it usually involve using foreign exchange contracts (e.g., forward contracts), which can be used to hedge against exchange rate fluctuations. Capacity building and proper implementation of such mechanisms will be part of the TO activities under Component I.1.


---
**Page 80**
---

Selected Risk Factor F: Possibility of losses resulting from borrowers' failure to repay the loans or meet contractual obligations under the bonds

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Low | Medium |

Description

162\. In any credit transaction, a risk exists that the borrower may default. Potential defaults of bio-businesses financed by the Programme may negatively impact the repayment of the lender LFI to the EE; and that if the EE is unable to repay its debt to the GCF, then the loan provided under Component I.1 will be backed by a sovereign guarantee; this is, in the event of default, the GCF would be protected (i.e., reimbursed) by the national government. Investment grants will contribute to reduce the net profits of a lender participating in the Programme and increase financial costs for the borrower or issuer of a debt obligation.

Mitigation Measure(s)

163\. While it is not possible to foresee what the future holds, properly assessing and managing credit risk can lessen the severity of a loss. The intermediation scheme proposed for Component I uses LFIs expertise in collecting and processing information to accurately assess the risk (i.e., capacity to repay). Associated with this are the use of financial instruments that enable lenders to manage their exposure to credit risk and increase net profits. If a lender participating in the Programme faces heightened credit risks, instruments enabled by the intermediation scheme can be used to mitigate these risks. The AAA rating provided by the IDB guarantee supported for Component I.2 is an indication of the risk of default of the issuer. Hence, the probability of this risk is considered low.

#### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

**E164.** Because higher Environmental and Social (E&S) risks activities will be excluded from the Programme, the potential E&S risks and impacts are likely to be low to moderate, which are largely manageable by due-diligence mechanisms. Therefore, this transaction was categorized as Category B.

**E165.** Although the Programme is expected to have a positive impact on the environment and social aspects of the beneficiary project if meets good practice implementation, negative impacts can occur. However, based on the proposed mitigation measures, it is anticipated that these risks will be adequately addressed through well-established mitigation measures and fulfilment of IIB’s applicable Environmental and Social Performance Standards.

| --- | --- |

| EPS1 | Targeted sectors have physical, biological and social risks that are generally at a low level but limited in the region and not in MIMs. | Where applicable, investments will seek good practices in areas such as: organic farming, eco-friendly, FSC, ISO 26000, FSC, VCS (where applicable), etc., against IIB’s Environmental and Social Performance Standard 5. |

| EPS8 | Adverse impacts on communities with potential adverse impacts on Indigenous people as defined by IIB’s Environmental and Social Performance Standard 7. |

## ESFPS
### Projects involving adverse impacts on cultural heritage
Investments will protect cultural heritage from the adverse effects of climate change, including loss and damage to presentation, and will promote the equitable sharing of benefits arising from such investments in accordance with CbS's Environmental and Social Performance Standard 7.

## ESFPS 9
### Gender equality
Sub-Projects must be designed to achieve inclusion for women and men across all dimensions of gender, sexual orientations, and gender identities consistent with CbS's Environmental and Social Performance Standard 8. Investments must be designed to minimize use of sexual violence and other forms of gender-based violence in accordance with CbS's Environmental and Social Performance Standard 9.

## ESFPS 10
### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure
Borrowers will comply with CbS requirements for stakeholder engagement, including the publication of a project-level environmental and social impact assessment consistent with CbS's Environmental and Social Performance Standard 10.

# Financial intermediation activity 1.1: each participating NDB and LRI will manage the supported portfolio by applying a specific E&I Management Framework (EMF) to be approved by the IDB during Sub-programme approval.

- Category A high risk projects screening;

IDB review will vary existing any E&S Management System (EMS) for every project. The IDB review will be conducted in accordance with the IDB's Environmental and Social Review. IDB will follow the IDB E&S procedure and apply its Platform for the Analysis of Institutional Capacity and Performance (PACIP) to assess the capacity and commitment of each NDB and LRI.

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Women play a fundamental role in the Amazon.
As users of forest products and guardians of traditional knowledge, women are particularly vulnerable to climate change. Nevertheless, their access to forest resources and the economic benefits generated by the biodiversity is limited compared to men's. The country's gender gap in terms of participation in decision-making processes is exacerbated by structural barriers, which are exacerbated in rural areas and among indigenous and Afro-descendant women.
According to the latest data from the ECLAC, estimates that female participation in the workforce in 2020 in LAC will be 48%, a decrease of 6 percentage points compared to 2019. This means that about 12 million women left the regional workforce due to job destruction.

### G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Disbursement of Programme resources to the IIB under the FAA would be subject to the IIB having approved the allocation of Programme resources to reimbursable (cover guaranteed loans) or non-reimbursable (non-guaranteed loans) components of the IIB’s loan portfolio. The IIB will provide the corresponding Subsidary Agreements for such operations, if such agreements are required.

In credit operations financed under Component 1, financial management considerations included in the OBI and/or the corresponding EE, and abide by the laws and financial regulation of the country in which funds are disbursed. In addition, disbursement of Programme resources to IIB will be subject to review by the IIB or as reimbursement of expenses at any time within the established loan disbursement period, in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the Subsidary Agreement between the IIB and the corresponding EE. The disbursement of Programme resources to IIB will be subject to review by the IIB in order to proceed with each disbursement. Investment grant resources will only be disbursed after all financial management requirements, including those related to the Subsidary Agreement between the IIB and the corresponding EE have been fulfilled. These conditions may include a requirement for the IIB to provide an audited financial statement of its operations under the Programme or limits to the volume of GCF resources to be disbursed in advance. Conditions will be determined on a case-by-case basis for each participant country. EEs will be required to submit audited financial statements and other relevant documents, including annual financial statements per period, as standard IIB practice. Financial statements shall be duly audited by an independent firm according to the applicable accounting standards and/or regulations of the country in which they are submitted; periodic reports (at least annually) including financial information and compliance with eligibility criteria will also be required for each participant country, in accordance with the terms and conditions of predefined risk indicators. Non-reimbursable resources may be deployed by IIB either through technical assistance or other means.

The financial management and oversight of any of the above-mentioned operations, including reporting requirements, will follow GCF policies and procedures, and applicable AMA and FAA requirements, which would be provided to the IIB as part of the Subsidary Agreement. It is important to note that use of GCF proceeds for eligible activities under the applicable components of the Programme.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP173

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| This regional, multi-stakeholder programme seeks to catalyse the first use of private capital to bio-businesses that have a high climate impact in the Amazon Basin, while addressing the need to increase resilience resulting from the programme’s impacts. The programme develops and models current development and financial models through investing in nature-based businesses to protect biodiversity and ecosystems which contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increasing the region's climate resilience. | The programme’s impacts need to be closely monitored using evidence-based monitoring and evaluations (M&E) frameworks, in terms of both economic and environmental resilience resulting from the programme’s impacts. The M&E systems and related instruments will need to adequately monitor the effectiveness of the programme using an adaptive management approach. |
| This programme emphasises the intrinsic value of biodiversity and encourages best practices and technologies to encourage innovative bio-businesses to build a local and global business model. | Market demand data demonstrates the potential demand of the loan, equity and bond instruments for bio-business finance. The adoption capacity at the country and project level will need to be closely monitored. |

This programme is designed so that all activities can be integrated into national systems. Improvements are expected to continue until the end of the programme completion. It aims to enable a transition from grant-based funding towards market structures supporting bio-business markets.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the item sheet and addendum XVIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations”, respectively.


---
**Page 101**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Programme background

a. This programme will focus on the Amazon region, especially on six countries of South America: Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname. Covering over 6,000,000 km², the Amazon is the world's largest river system, housing the greatest concentration of biodiversity in the world.

b. The Amazon hosts more than 2 million species of plants and animals, including 40% of global flora and fauna. The Amazon host sites over 40 per cent of global dense moist tropical forest and 15-15 per cent of terrestrial carbon stocks. However, the Amazon is highly vulnerable to climate change. While it contains many different ecosystems, the Amazon forms a single bio-geophysical entity, often referred to as the "Amazon biome". With an estimated population of over 34 million people, including more than 30 indigenous groups.

c. Since 1980, temperatures in the region have increased by 0.5°C on average and are projected to increase further by up to 2.7°C by 2100. The Amazon biome is highly vulnerable to climate change because of its direct exposure, high sensitivity and low adaptive capacity.

d. Based on recent research literature, the climate change context for this programme is that increasing temperatures will lead to increased evaporation rates and projected increased regional temperatures may induce a higher frequency of extreme climate events, directly impacting forests and agricultural productivity leading to increased incidence of fires, droughts and floods.

e. Evapotranspiration is essential for maintaining the Amazon climate, ensuring water supply in rivers and lakes, and releasing moisture into the atmosphere. However, factors controlling the Amazon region’s evapotranspiration are changing, reducing climate stability and leaving the region vulnerable to further uncertain change. Understanding how these changes will affect the Amazon biome is critical for predicting how the biome's ecosystems may respond to the changing climate and preparing appropriate adaptation strategies.

f. Human activities interact with the Amazon “natural” system, leading to deforestation and forest degradation, anthropogenic fires (in many cases exacerbated by drought events), however, these factors are not the only ones that can lead to a tipping point in the region’s adverse climate feedback loops. This means that Amazon forests are more vulnerable to degradation and loss of carbon stocks, reducing their ability to store carbon reserves and biodiversity, and controlling hydrological and biogeochemical cycles. The indicators of forest degradation and fire risk are increasing, and these factors reinforce each other's decline. These additional complications to the climate feedback loop lead to increased vulnerability of Amazon forests to climate change, potentially reaching its tipping point, resulting in the dieback of Amazon forest and the conversion of forested ecosystems into a savanna-like landscape. Reaching this tipping point would result in the Amazon biome becoming a major source of carbon emissions.

g. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) and the Global Carbon Project for the Science Panel for the Amazon) have shown that this trend is now evident, and that the intensification of climate change will lead to increased vulnerability of Amazon ecosystems stress, the occurrence of wildfires, and carbon emissions in the eastern part of the Amazon.

h. To restore ecosystem functionality and related benefits, it is essential that concerted efforts be made to reduce the drivers of forest degradation and fire risk, strengthen degraded ecosystems to strengthen climate change mitigation, improve the resilience of local communities and enhance their adaptive capacity.


1. Manzano and Sousa, 2018.
2. See <https://www.ipcc.ch/sr15/chapter/4/>
3. See <https://www.unsand.org/science-panel-for-the-amazon-presentation-of-initial-findings/>
