# Green Climate Fund

## GFZ.30.PN.74.VAM.05

### Page 1/2

The concept of "biomass" refers to any economic activity based on the use of natural renewable biological resources, from both land and water, to obtain food, plants and energy in a sustainable way without causing significant environmental damage or degradation. Biomass is used for various purposes such as biofuels, biogas, bioelectricity, development, production and use of biological products and processes. For the purpose of this programme, the biomass is understood as an economic activity that generates natural capital, resulting in positive impacts on biodiversity and encouraging sustainable land use practices.

### Page 2/2


---
**Page 103**
---

18. The programme consists of three components with eleven subcomponents. They will be implemented by the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB) in collaboration with the Governments of Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname. The programme is categorized at the national level of intermediation (SI) based on the Environmental and Social Policy of the GCF.

| Component I. Financing solutions for bio-businesses Subcomponents | Component II. Bond structuring and issuance Subcomponents | Component III. Transversal technical cooperation Subcomponents |
|---|---|---|
| I.1 Sovereign investment loans and investment grants | I.I Technical cooperation for thematic bond issuance | I.II Inclusion mechanisms for women and diversity in the financial sector |
| I.2 Equity investments and innovative financing | I.2 Grants for guarantees | I.III Integrated support and the strengthening of the capacity of micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) and their communities |
| 1.2a Regenerative agriculture | 1.2b Direct funding for climate-tech and agri-tech projects | I.IV Stakeholder engagement programmes |
| 1.2c Bio-businesses pipeline development and acceleration | 1.3 Tools for connecting bio-businesses with domestic and international markets | I.V Small grants programme |
| 1.3a Institutional and regulatory environment | 1.3b Financial instruments and guarantees | I.VI General advisory services |
| 1.3c Financial instruments and guarantees | 1.4 Programme monitoring and evaluation |


The financial instruments and mechanisms for this programme include grants (USD 175 million), loans (USD 115 million) and equity (USD 6.5 million) amounting to a total GCF contribution of USD 297 million. A total of USD 598.1 million is committed, mostly from IADB and grants from other organizations, including private entities.

### Component-component analysis

**Component I: Financing solutions for bio-businesses (total cost: USD 307.7 million; GCF cost: USD 213.1 million; co-financing: USD 94.6 million)**

- Sovereign investment loans and equity (USD 51 million): Sovereign investment loans and investment grants will provide funding to national development banks (NDBs) or special purpose vehicles (SPVs), which in turn will lend to the NDBs, that offer financing for bio-businesses. This will be conducted directly by the IADB or through the IFC (which will also provide guarantees). Financial instruments tailored to the scale and risks of bio-businesses.
- Strengthening capacity development: Strengthening the capacity development of the financial, legal and operational entities of the relevant entities. For this component, the entities must have already been assessed by the accredited entity (AE). Entity strengthening and capacity development will be conducted through a combination of direct support to the AE, including fiduciary structures, over an extended period, allowing for cross-country learning and exchange.
- Financial instruments: The IADB will provide financial instruments from different sources with varying risk appetites and tolerances. This component offers an ample range of financial products to support projects of all sizes, either directly or indirectly.


---
**Page 104**
---

with high levels of flexibility and efficiency. For each country, this means that different financing structures can be offered to meet the needs of different market segments. The AE aims to support and leverage the capacities of NBIs so that they can also provide second-tier financing to create a more efficient and effective system for mobilizing public and private resources (EEs). Following the examples of GCF-funded programmes FP09, FP063 and 064, LFs are not EEs because they have no contractual relationship with IDB.

For example, in the case of the GCF-supported USD 16 million loan to the lender to sub-projects under component I could be any of the following: (1) the NBIs, (2) tier-in/tier-out operations via dedicated financing lines or SVPs, and (3) a third-party intermediary financial institution, in second-tier operations, structured via either dedicated financing lines or SVPs, as follows:

Note that LFs have discretion to allocate funding. They can lend on direct or through LIs, at their per official mandate and subject to the requirements in the GCF’s Operations Manual (OM). The lending terms are determined by the NBIs with discretion on the operation type. In each case, the Subsidiary Agreement will require that an Operations Regulation [OR] be approved in terms acceptable to IDB as the AE. The operations must also comply with the GCF’s eligibility criteria and accreditation process for LFs (with applicable).

When a loan is made directly by the NBIs or through LIs where NBIs are involved, it is the executing NBI that is contractually responsible for deciding which projects in the credit portfolio of each LF are presented to IDB for funding, based on the eligibility criteria and accreditation process. The NBIs are also required to be responsible for supervising the use of proceeds or post.

The lending terms must be consistent with the GCF’s OM and should include four elements: (1) the characteristics of each project; (2) the entity financed; (3) the ultimate use of proceeds, and (4) prevailing financial market practices. 

Overall, the NBIs are required to take all reasonable steps so that financing remains viable and projects profitable, but markets are not distorted. The mismatch between the terms of a loan and the actual use of funds is reflected in the revolving use of funds if the programme monitoring shows positive results.

The rationale for the targeted investment grants (GCF USD 16 million) is to overcome the higher risk associated with investments in small-scale projects, which are often outside the scope of business models or/and introduction of more sustainable practices. The respective NBIs will implement these investments through LFs.

Use of an investment grant will need to comply with (i) the eligibility criteria; (2) the methodology for concessionary pass-throughs; (3) the maximization of impacts, mobilization; and (4) the risk management framework. These are described in detail in annex 19. The use is highly dependent on circumstances, as indicated in the programme’s Operations manual (annex 21).

Three de-risking instruments can be structured and offered by the EE with support of an investment grant, with aim of reducing risk/exposure/or and/or enhancing the collateral profile of certain investments. The three are:

Part-pays credit guarantees where payments are distributed proportionally between the guarantor and the creditor financial intermediary, who is the beneficiary of the guarantee.

Portfolio guarantee instruments (or risk-sharing facilities), especially useful for some segments (micro/small businesses) that face significant limitations in terms of collateral available; and


---
**Page 105**
---

Risk transfer schemes operating as guarantees for integrators that provide liquidity, suppliers or other resources to companies in their supply chains.

23. The AIF provided (in annexes 25a) different alternatives to the investment grants for achieving the required level of de-risking. During the review process, IIB explained that a GCF guarantee would be provided by the national government and/or by a sovereign counter-guarantee. The sovereign counter-guarantee would not effectively share the risk with the private sector (i.e., the private sector would still bear 100% of the risk). A GCF guarantee the GCF would be reimbursed by the national government), thus substantiating the need for an AIF.

24. Subcomponent II: Equity investments (GCF USD 6.5 million) and innovation grants (GCF USD 4 million) has two subcomponents: I.2A Reproduce Accelerator (GCF USD 10 million in equity investments and USD 3 million in grants); and I.2B Invest with companies (GCF USD 10 million in grants). Subcomponent I.2A aims to target disruptive solutions that can be scaled up by private sector entities, including start-ups and small and medium-sized enterprises. It will be key to developing market sectors where climate-tech skills are crucial and better-informed consumers. A focus on the consumer side will need to be considered in order to ensure a more sustainable and inclusive transition, while acknowledging the heterogeneity of the entrepreneurship-ecosystems dynamics among the Amazon countries.

25. Subcomponent I.3 Technical cooperation (GCF USD 53.6 million) intends to enhance readiness for bankable projects under subcomponent I.1. Supervision costs are excluded from the GCF contribution, and will be covered by the national government. The pipeline development and supervision subcomponent (I.3.1) comprise [1] support to potential beneficiaries in preparing their project proposals; [2] technical assistance in developing a business case for projects; and [3] capacity building and training on climate-smart agriculture incorporating research and development and innovation.

26. The EBR for this subcomponent, IIB's Reproduce Laboratory (Lab Lab) and Converx, will take the lead in supporting the following initiatives: (1) Amazonia 4.0 (e.g., in fabrics for cocoa and cayusca value chains); (2) the Letras Platform* currently being piloted for Colombia with the support of IIB; (3) the Amazonian Agroforestry Innovation Network (AAN), based on the Hacienda Colombia Project, and (4) the Amazon Bioeconomy marketplace, co-created by Converx, the EBR, and the Colombian government. The AAN will be hosted in the Converx Lab, and potentially collaborating with global marketplace platforms such as Mercado Libre.

27. These initiatives are underpinned by the development of tools for connecting bio-based value chains to markets (e.g., the Letras Platform), which will support the design and implementation of business models that can be scaled up; the development and strengthening of the institutions and regulatory environment (3.x.), including the creation of a national framework for agroforestry, the establishment of capital forest assets and mainstreaming the valuation processes into national economic accounting systems; and the promotion of public-private partnerships to support the transition towards private sector participation in forest and conservation concessions management schemes.

Component II: Bond structuring and issuance (total cost USD 105 million; GCF cost: USD 35 million; IIB cost: USD 70 million)

28. Component II aims to support public entities to leverage finance from capital markets through the issuance of green and sustainable bonds. IIB has recently demonstrated experience supporting such issuances, including in Colombia (e.g., the Colombian National Bank FNB) in the issuance of green bonds related to activities leading to emission reductions and natural resource conservation. The objective is to support public entities in structuring and strengthening their regulatory frameworks for sustainable bonds such as the Peru Sustainable

Bond Framework, which has been developed to follow the International Capital Market Association principles.

28. The programme’s bond component comprises two subcomponents: (I.I) Technical cooperation for thematic bond issuance (GCF: USD $5 million) to provide specialized advisory services on the design and implementation of bonds; (II) Financial instruments consisting of the legal and financial structuring of the bonds and certification; and (II.2) Grants for guarantees (GCF: USD 10 million) to support the second component through IIB guarantees structured for bonds that include bio-businesses, as well as other thematic bonds aimed at financing bio-businesses.

30. As stated in the funding proposal and its annex 25(a), the proposed use of reimbursable investment grants under component II.2 is to enhance viability and expand the reach of IIB guarantees. The GCF has determined that the cost of providing IIB guarantees for thematic bonds aimed at financing bio-businesses and is expected to improve the results all in financing offered by the bond, which is a critical aim of providing bio-businesses with viable terms.

31. In response to a request from the GCF Secretariat, a comparative modelling exercise was performed on the cost of IIB guarantees versus investment grants for thematic bonds aimed at financing bio-businesses instead of GCF guarantees (annex 25(b)). Leverage factors under various scenarios of bond issuance and guarantee costs were applied to the indicative cost of IIB guarantees and found to range from 7.7 to 31%. For the programme proposal, the AE has assumed terms that result in a conservative 7.7x leverage, at the lower end of the above-mentioned range. This suggests that the proposed use of USD $25 million in investment grants under component II.2 will lead to an increase in potential bond issuance of USD $193 million in bio-business investment. This is also reflected in the estimations of leverage potential in section 4.

32. The bonds will be issued by public entities (including national governments and NIIbs). 34. The bonds require funding with funds raised directly via GCF or IIB loans/grants supported by the programme, as well as through other sources of financing. A sensitivity analysis demonstrates that the size of the demand is large enough to justify both mechanisms. As the feasibility study demonstrates, the demand for thematic bonds aimed at financing bio-businesses via loans/grants in component I, eligibility criteria for the use of component II funds shall be the same as the eligibility criteria for financing under component I.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

This is a cross-cutting programme with 50 per cent of the impact potential allocated for the forest and land use results area (mitigation), 25 per cent for ecosystems and ecosystem services, and 25 per cent for adaptation and climate change policy and governance (adaptation). The programme's direct beneficiaries are estimated at 191,952 people (around 50 per cent of whom are women) who will benefit directly from the programme. An additional 485,375 people (around 50 per cent in women) in the region are expected to benefit indirectly from the programme, from the new economic opportunities and improved livelihoods that will be created as a result of the value chains that will be supported by the programme. An additional 192,486 people (around 50 per cent in women) in the region are to be used to realize programme benefits. These criteria are detailed in the programme’s investment case and in the GCF project design document and operations manual (annex 21). The criteria for programme benefits are deemed to be appropriate and are in line with the GCF’s operational policy.

The number of beneficiaries (direct and indirect) is estimated as follows:

(a) Direct beneficiaries are estimated based on the total size of the programme (both GCF and co-financing) and the number of bio-businesses that the programme can finance. Using the average investment per bio-business, the average investment for each value chain, market segment [micro, SMEs, medium, large] and country, and the total number of bio-businesses in the region supported by the programme, the total number of faces that can be financed by the programme is estimated (annex 21). The total number of direct beneficiaries is then estimated using the number of bio-businesses multiplied by average employment per bio-business. In some cases, this may not be possible. In this case, it is assumed that the direct beneficiaries correspond to the owner of the bio-


---
**Page 108**
---

business and the respective family members in the household supported by the income from the bio-businesses.

(b) Indirect beneficiaries are assumed to be employees working in the bio-businesses (excluding the owner) and their respective family members who benefit from the income generated by these businesses. The number of indirect beneficiaries is the bio-businesses multiplied by employees (excluding the owner) multiplied by the average number of family dependents per employee (including spouse, children, and other direct or actual employees).

61. The economic and financial analysis (annex 3) provides details of the data and assumptions for these estimations. The AE will follow the criteria described in the Operations manual (annex 21) for all value chains. Thus, at a minimum, the direct beneficiaries are assumed to be those who benefit from the income generated by the bio-businesses through the adoption of practices and technologies that improve their climate resilience. This follows the agent-based approach as described in the Operations Manual (annex 21). The AE will also include the new employees and their dependents that benefit from the improved financial performance of the bio-businesses. The adaptation impacts, in terms of direct beneficiaries, are conservative and objective.

62. The feasibility study (annex 2) contains the climate problem statement, which includes a well-developed description of the climate change risks for the countries, regions, basins, and regional and national implications, as well as the dynamic nature of changes behind these implications. In addition, the theory of change includes a detailed description of the climate risk for the bio-businesses in the basin. The feasibility study (annex 2) also generally includes a characterization of the climate risks overripe at the Amazon basin level and presents country-specific information.

63. The expected impact magnitude of the programme is within a reasonable range in terms of cost-effectiveness, and the assumptions underlying the estimates for each value chain are appropriate. For the bio-businesses, the avoided emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (FDD) are estimated in 123 MGHG reduced or avoided (including increased removals) in the forest and land use sector. The methodology used to estimate these values is described in the impact calculations and related models as described in the GHG calculations (annexes 22 and 24 based on the description of the methodologies provided in the economic and financial analysis [annex 3]).

64. The programme applies the Six-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (Six-AT) developed by the Forest and Land Use Programme of the Organization of the United Nations (FAO), which uses the appropriate default values in the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) guidelines. Default values are used for all countries except Brazil, where country-specific data have been documented [annex 21)]. For the timber value chains, the values have been adjusted in the model. Country-specific information is provided in annexes 23 and 24). The forest reference emission level (FREL) and national GHG inventory of each participating country; and the carbon fraction, being consistent with the IPCC default value for tropical regions as well as with most of the FRELs used by countries in the Amazon region. It is important to note that the programme is conservative because it does not include removals from the dead organic carbon pool (DOP).

65. In terms of the baselines, all countries considered for the programme have submitted their FRELs to the UNFCCC; most addressed only the activity "reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation" in the forest sector or the conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks" (e.g., through restoration or reforestation). Moreover, the information

# Green Climate Fund

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

The programme is strengthening regional integration among Amazon countries by supporting a new paradigm of sustainable development and climate change mitigation.

This holistic and systematic approach will not only increase productivity and green employment in the Amazon region, but also promote inclusion and sustainable business models that increase climate resilience and reduce poverty.

There are several elements showing that this programme is more than a sum of interventions at the country level and has a clear and demonstrated innovation and impact at programme level.

(a) The proposed programme consists of a set of interlinked individual projects, unified by one overarching objective: to create conditions for the development of new business models in targeted value chains and ecosystems in the Amazon region by enabling the conditions to increase funding flow to bio-businesses in the Amazon's interconnected. Interlinked individual projects are designed as building blocks that can be combined with other projects or stand alone. The programme is structured around three main components, each with deliver sustainable climate results and impact in the GCP mitigation and adaptation result framework. These three components are interdependent and mutually reinforcing: (1) they are coherent, creating synergies between components and sub-projects and reinforcing the intended outcomes of the programme; (2) they are designed to be flexible enough so that they create greater impact (increased sustainability, higher cost-effectiveness, and deeper integration); and (3) they are designed to be scalable.

(b) The programme stimulates two key shifts: (1) moving away from an economy based on forest and natural resource exploitation, cattle raising and annual agriculture, to a nature-based economy consisting of diverse and sustainable livelihoods; and (2)

*Red Amazónica de Información Socio Ambiental Gerenciada.


---
**Page 110**
---

changing values that see the Amazon region as an infinite source of natural resources and agriculture land for capitalist consumption to a diverse environment of global importance, made from standing forests and related ecosystems and its local communities, which has an inclusive wealth paradigm based on the Amazon biomes' natural capital.

The programme's bioeconomics approach is a financial innovation. The focus on socio-biophysical (SBI) models (see annex 1) provides a framework for how to attract conventional investment, despite having viable business models and a clear climate adaptation strategy. This innovative approach is particularly relevant to the Amazon region, as it sub-categories provide an innovative approach to programmatic structure and clearly demonstrates added value compared to stand-alone projects.

The proposal is technically sound. Specific theories of change are provided for each of the value chains. The programme's design is robust and consistent with the complexity of the programme’s climate rationale and result areas. The interventions proposed for each value chain are described in sufficient detail and references are provided to relevant research and practical experience. The programme has been peer-reviewed by a panel of independent, peer-reviewed articles. The feasibility study (annex 2) provides detailed analysis of the national context and the potential impact of the programme on the target countries. The proposal also includes details of the eligibility criteria to be used for assessing the proposals of bio-businesses in each value chain. The proposed approach is innovative and has not been tried before.

The programme has potential for demonstrating the economic utility of climate-resilient practices in the target countries, as well as for providing a framework for how to attract public finance and additional private and public sources. Leveraged investments will benefit both the value chains and the projects within each value chain through a series of interventions which may contribute more broadly to overall programme goal as described in annex 1.

54. IIB will work with the programme's NDBs as its main partners. The existing networks and leverage mechanisms that have been established by the NDBs, particularly those where mainstream or commercial banks are initiating in the region. Most of these commercial banks do not have direct branches in the intervention sites of the region. The programme expects to use the IIB’s network of development finance institutions (DFIs), microfinance, rural finance and cooperative financing institutions and networks working in each of the target countries as well as its own network of DFIs.

52. While countries have made great efforts to estimate and monitor their national income accounts to evaluate economic performance and assess the effectiveness of their policies, traditional approaches such as Gross Domestic Product (GDP) or Gross National Product (GNP), are limited because they do not consider the use and consequent depletion of the natural capital of a country. The inclusion of the value of ecosystem services in the calculation of GDP is a crucial step towards including the value of natural capital into their national accounts, to be able to make better economic decisions. This approach, which is known as the System of Environmental-Economic Accounting (SEEA) and is particularly relevant for Amazonian countries. The system follows the principles of the System of National Accounts (SNA), which has been used by the AE for this programme, has demonstrable experience (e.g., through the Integrated Economic Information and Monitoring System (IEIMS)) in including natural capital-related projects into accounting in the national accounts of the country and evaluating the economic impacts of the projects, and would be a strategic choice for this programme.

53. The IIB is committed to supporting sustainable use of specific land uses, such as green or sustainable biofuels. Recently, the IIB launched the Green Bond Transparency Platform, which captures all the issuer-reported and external reviewer data in a free-of-charge one-stop shop.

# Sustainable development potential

## Scale: High

### The programme creates economic, environmental, social and gender co-benefits beyond the typical SDG targets for sustainable development. The programme directly contributes to targets 13.1, 13.2, 13.3 and 13.A of Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 13 (climate action and contributing to the achievement of all other SDGs). It also contributes to the following SDG targets: 8.2, 8.4, 8.5, 8.9 and 10; SDG 15 (to end land degradation), 15.1, 15.2, 15.3, 15.5, 15.A, 15.9, 15.A and 15.B; SDG 2 (zero hunger), achieve food security and promote sustainable agriculture) targets 2.a and 2.b; SDG 3 (good health and well-being); SDG 4 (quality education). Specifically, the programme contributes to SDG 3 (no poverty), SDG 5 (health and well-being), SDG12 (responsible consumption and production).

### In terms of environmental benefits, the programme will promote the conservation, restoration and sustainable management of 3.9 million hectares of natural forest ecosystems. This is achieved through a combination of activities such as agroforestry, silvopastoral systems, namely non-timber forest products (2.9 million hectares) and nature-based tourism (1 million hectares). The programme will also promote the sustainable management of 305,000 hectares of timber sustainably harvested (165,000 hectares), and land uses such as agroforestry and native pollinator-friendly crops. These activities will generate economic co-benefits through the sequestration and emission reductions of 123 Mio tCO₂ over 20 years.

### Beyond the climate mitigation co-benefit, environmental protection and biodiversity conservation are also key co-benefits of the programme. The Amazon is one of the most biodiverse regions on the planet. The extreme species richness and local knowledge wealth amounts to an enormous potential for sustainable development in the region. This high biodiversity acts as an intrinsic element of economic success, in two main ways:

- **The paradigm shift to a more biodiversity-based economy not only promotes the local ecosystem services that are essential for human well-being but also provides new opportunities of biodiversity management. These benefits include fair access and benefit sharing, protection of traditional knowledge and practices, and increased research on sustainable use of biodiversity. Through this, the programme contributes to strategic goals B, D and E of the Aichi Biodiversity Targets.**[10]


---
**Page 112**
---

(b) The protection and enhanced management of the biodiversity, soils and ecological processes of the natural forests deliver significant benefits related to the enhancement and maintenance of key ecosystem services such as water provision, pollution reduction, carbon sequestration, soil conservation and the production of traditional natural medicines. These services are of significant economic, social, health and cultural importance, while not yet adequately included in traditional economic accounting and policy decision-making.

56. The programme's social co-benefits are measured in terms of direct (193,952) and indirect (407,880) jobs created for the benefit of forest communities, including landowners and their families, including existing and newly generated jobs). This includes climate adaptation benefits such as increased resilience to droughts and floods, and greater resistance to the effects of climate change (heat, drought, flooding) compared with the business-as-usual economic sectors (extensive cattle raising, annual agriculture and so on). Other social benefits include the creation of new jobs in forest communities, improved access to education and income per job overall.

57. Other socio-economic co-benefits are generated by programme activities such as the training and communication with the target population, which will strengthen their financial administration and business management skills (70 per cent of all business owners) with the use of resources from the project. This will increase the capacity to generate income overall awarenesses of climate threats and related appropriate responses (10,000 people).

58. In relation to gender issues, the status quo based on destructive forest use, cattle ranching and other activities that are harmful to the environment is being addressed in which other members of the communities, usually women and youth, area able participate only marginally or not at all. The programme will provide training for women and youth in labour (i.e. menial labour in shifts or unhealthy working conditions). Diversification of the local economy through the creation of new jobs and income-generating activities increases the options for different members of the community to have jobs (or household tasks) that match their interests, skills and capacity, reducing the risks of inequity or unhealthy labour conditions.

59. An entire subcomponent [III.3] is devoted to mechanisms to increase diversity and the participation of women in forest management activities. The programme will cost $1 million. In accordance with national gender strategies, the programme will encourage women's participation in forest management through training and access to microfinance and other formal employment schemes. This will be done through the development of strategies, explicit measures and/or services to promote equitable access to the benefits of investments supported such as:

* Technical training or financial services tailored to women (taking into account collective land ownership)
* Better and more accessible routes to information and technologies;
* Equitable ownership over productive factors such as land, animals and capital;
* Including specific criteria for the use of proceeds obtained from the issuance of sustainable bonds in favour of activities led by minorities, indigenous people and women.
* Access to formal economic; and
* Better promotion and marketing strategies for bio-products in local, national and international markets, including with good production practices.

60. The programme will apply gender-segregated data collection, and analysis of country-level gaps and opportunities: it targets a minimum of 50 per cent female participation in all

### Needs of the recipients

#### Scale: High

The AFOLU sector is a major contributor to GGR emissions in countries in the Amazon and a key area for climate change mitigation. However, efforts to reverse deforestation and land degradation efforts so far have been insufficient to reverse trends. A shift in local productive structures and practices cannot be achieved on public efforts alone. Good policies and investments are needed to support private sector actors in driving lasting change, specifically via encouraging an increase in the level of investment and investing in business models that can deliver climate benefits.

In the programme's targeted countries, natural capital is substantial, and the development of bio-businesses pose an important opportunity to address AFOLU-related GGR emissions. The programme will support the development of innovative financing mechanisms to better connect relevant actors within these markets and for them to realize the value of their investments in natural capital. This will be achieved by constructing capital flows prevalent under the status quo by focusing on two main areas:

(a) Strengthening and/or stabilizing a proper regulatory environment, or specific mechanisms that can support the development of bio-businesses.

(b) Attracting private capital, particularly from local financial systems and impact-driven investors to bio-businesses that can deliver climate benefits with the maximum leverage.

GCF resources are necessary for these actions for two primary reasons:

(a) Advances made in regulatory and institutional frameworks vary among the six targeted countries. In some cases, they have not yet been fully implemented or are at an early stage. Overall, legal and policy frameworks are in place for the conservation and sustainable management of natural capital. However, there is a need to further strengthen their participation of private actors in related business needs to be developed.

The programme will support the development of innovative financing mechanisms with corresponding actions for safeguarding their natural capital in this complex sector.

All six targeted countries have financial systems that show low levels of depth, are vulnerable to systemic risks, and are not well suited to supporting innovative or venture-based ventures. There remains a lack of adequate financial instruments that can crowd in the private sector to support the development of bio-businesses in these countries. In general, the risks associated with these projects, especially in the absence of proper natural capital valuation, hinder credit and investment by private actors. In addition, support for innovation is limited due to the lack of access to adequate financing, expertise and advancement in technology development associated with the innovative nature of bio-businesses.

The environmental and social management framework (ESMF) (annex 6(a)) and stakeholder engagement plan (annex 7) set out the approach for achieving continuous inclusion and regional representation. The ESMF will be used to ensure that the programme is fair for indigenous and traditional communities and a public consultation series. When applicable, the eligibility criteria will be adapted in order to take into account the specificities of peoples' considerations, such as participation and consent requirements. The programme will

# 3.5 Country ownership

Each of the countries targeted by the programme have climate, forestry and biodiversity policies in place. These countries have well-established climate communities and active plans – at both the national and subnational level – related to reducing GHG emissions and increasing climate resilience. They also have a range of forest sector strategies and/or national adaptation plans. The countries also widely recognize the private sector’s role in deploying investments to support their goals, and the potential of the private sector to contribute to the paradigm shift needed for sustainable development.

In terms of forestry and biodiversity policies, approaches to forestry policies differ among countries. These differences are reflected in the variety of forest policy options that form part of their strategies to address climate financing and implementation gaps. The funding proposal document presents a detailed overview of all country-specific sectoral and general policy frameworks that cover the following topics: (a) land use planning; (b) fiscal instruments, including environmental incentives, supply chain legislation, organic agriculture, sustainable fisheries, ecotourism, or technology transfer agreements; and (c) other policies.

(a) Brazil has incorporated bioeconomy strategies in several strategies, including the National Plan for the Promotion of Socio-Biodiversity Value Chains and the National Programme for the Sustainable Use of Forest Resources.

(b) Ecuador has stated its vision to pursue the bioeconomy as a strategy for recovering from the socioeconomic impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic, preserving its threatened ecosystems, and ensuring that its population is more resilient and less dependent on fossil fuels.

(c) Peru has recently approved the Law on Societies of Collective Benefit and Interest. The bioeconomy concept is integrated in several Colombian national development documents such as the Green Growth Strategy, the Circular Economy Strategy, and the Innovation Policy.

(d) Guyana and Suriname also have some policies in place that contribute to a sustainable bioeconomy.

The proposed Amazon Bioeconomy Fund is a key tool to promote regional collaborative implementation of the Amazon Cooperation Treaty. The text of the Fact lists various points on which signatory countries intend to collaborate, including (1) promoting the conservation and sustainable use of resources; (3) strengthening actions to strengthen the rights of women and indigenous people, and (4) promoting the development of a bioeconomy.

Guided by the spirit of the amended Amazon Cooperation Treaty and the Leticia Pact, all operations carried out within the framework of the programme will contribute to the target countries’ efforts in implementing their climate change strategies (nationally determined contributions and national adaptation plans), commitments under the Convention on Biological Diversity, the Ramsar Convention, the CBD Cartagena Protocol, and international treaties such as the Paris Agreement, the Sendai Framework, and the SDGs.

During the development of the programme proposal, relevant national government agencies were consulted to ensure that the proposed activities are consistent with


---
**Page 115**
---

climate, energy, and bioeconomy-related legal frameworks. In addition, perspectives of various local stakeholders have been considered, including NGOs, government ministries, bio-business developers, LFs and investors. Focusing on the Finance sector, national financial institutions and private banks are involved in the programme. The AE has also decided to consider EEs as a key part of the programme's delivery model as EEs of many of the programme’s financial instruments will be required for implementation. These include indigenous peoples and local communities, as well as civil society organizations, in all cases. Also, the AE will engage with universities, scientific organizations and cross-national networks, all of which are considered key stakeholders.

72. During the implementation phase, strategic decisions will be coordinated with national designated authorities (DNAs) to ensure alignment between the programme’s goals and national complementarity with their existing and upcoming initiatives in the Amazon with a focus on the scalability of approaches, and the promotion of knowledge sharing. A strategic steering committee will be established under the leadership of the AE, comprising representatives from the authority, the EEs (where applicable), and the respective Ministry of Finance to provide periodic strategic input, which will be used by the AE to guide programme implementation. The AE will work closely with governments and accompany their efforts to consider, evaluate and implement concrete actions, sectoral agreements and establish targets in their agendas as a priority.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

73. From the perspective of both mitigation and adaptation, the programme exhibits high efficiency and effectiveness. The programme’s mitigation cost-effectiveness is USD 24.1/CO2e (GCF financing) which is below GCF's portfolio average in Latin America and the Caribbean region and international AIs. Similarly, the programme’s adaptation cost-effectiveness is USD 30.5/USD 1000 (GCF financing), which is below the GCF’s portfolio average for Latin America and the Caribbean, direct access entities and international AIs. The programme also demonstrates high efficiency in terms of its ability to generate significant mitigation benefits, for a carbon price of USD 40/CO2e, and 14 per cent without carbon benefits. This is well above the GCF’s portfolio average for Latin America and the Caribbean region and country-level poverty analysis. It provides analytical comfort on the overall programme intended impacts.

74. The funding amount requested by the AE is thoroughly justified. One of the main strengths of the programme is its focus on a limited number of value chains, with economic and financial consideration of the value chains prioritized. The selection of these value chains is well defined in terms of their potential for scaling up and replication. The programme targets 105 businesses (24% of total) across four sectors: agriculture, energy, bioeconomy and sector. It also describes all these elements not only as aggregate values at a programme level, but also for each country.

75. The financial gap analysis shows that the financial gap for bioeconomy businesses in the programme amounts to more than USD 2 billion. This gap is split into USD 834 million for microenterprises, USD 691 million for small and medium size businesses, and USD 571 million for large businesses.

76. From a sectoral perspective, the AE’s analysis estimated funding needs in the agriculture sector to be USD 2 billion (USD 1.1 billion), followed by the forestry sector at USD 899 million, and wildfires at USD 43.7 million.

77. The country allocation of the financial demand adequately reflects the extension and spatial coverage of the programme’s activities. The analysis also provides a summary of negotiations between the AE and each of the countries during programme formulation and provides a reflection of the heterogeneity of the economic and political situations in the region.


---
**Page 116**
---

The voice of financial instruments by the AIF directly stems from the financing gap analysis. The funding proposal adopts a programmatic approach that targets the de-risking of biocomy business at all the relevant levels.

(a) Combining concessional loans with the innovative use of reimbursable grants for credit enhancement purposes, the GCF loan facility allows the programme to target companies of all the relevant sizes ranging from micro businesses to SMEs with potential access to bank financing. The proposal also targets larger enterprises seeking capital market funding through loans.

(b) The AIF proposes to use the GCF concessional loan to de-risk LFI investment in bio-commodity projects by providing a risk-sharing facility for investors through financial demonstration effects. The AIF is requesting a long-concessional senior loan (0.75 per cent per year) and a short-term concessional loan (1.25 per cent per year) on top of the senior loan provided by the AIF (LIBOR + 0.80 per cent pricing, 25-year tenor; 5-year grace period). The proposal is based on the GCF’s financial analysis and demonstrates that the GCF will contribute to enhancing the bankability of biocomy projects by increasing average interest rate of return for investors from 13.4% to 16.7%, with a corresponding increase in the average payback period for the activities under consideration in the proposal is between 7 and 20 years. The GCF will provide concessional financing at a cost of 5.8 per cent, which is lower than the cost of capital associated with LFI investment in biocomy projects. This type of financing with such long tenures because they are not familiar with the credit risk associated with these types of investments and the associated transaction costs. The proposal is therefore justified on the basis of the financial analysis and the feasibility study results.

In addition to directly providing concessional loans, the AIF will use GCF reimbursable grants for credit enhancement purposes in order to de-risk LFI investment in biocomy projects. The rationale behind this instrument is described in annexes (24a). This risk-sharing facility will be provided by the AIF on top of the concessional loan provided by the GCF’s USD 135 million senior loan and IBF’s USD 135 million senior loan. The funding proposal includes a total of USD 60 million in grant financing, which is to be co-financed with IFC financing and L2A contributing additional USD 400 million. This includes USD 49 million from L2A own resources and USD 81 million in debt/equity bond investors, and USD 175 million in equity from the GCF.

The delivery model selected by the AIF, through NDIIs as EEs for component I also increases the likelihood of successful disbursement of the programme resources by leveraging the NDIIs’ network and existing distribution channels. It builds the capacity of NDIIs and EE in assessing risks related to biocomy projects and provides them with a better understanding of the GCF’s terms in innovative and strategic use of their own resources. Discussion on the choice of EEs is described in annexes (24b). The delivery model also increases the likelihood of successful disbursement of the programme and increase the likelihood of improved access to finance for biocomy businesses, which will be perceived as a positive development by investors in the value chains under consideration.

The AIF also includes a component aimed at catalyzing use of market capital to finance biocomy projects. This is targeted towards the largest business under the programme. It is a realistic target because the programme includes Brazil, which is both the most sophisticated and largest country in terms of biocomy production and the country that has the largest share of the Amazon, as well as Colombia, another sophisticated capital market. The component will include a USD 10 million grant to provide credit enhancement for bond issuers, increase the coverage of IBFC own guarantees, and ultimately create the additional credit establishment that will lower the cost of capital, in addition to transaction costs, for the green bond issuers.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

**Programme background:** The programme aims to increase private investment in bio-businesses that will contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation in the Amazon, through innovative financing solutions, strengthening the business environment and providing capacity building for financial institutions. This is expected to lead to a significant increase in private sector investment in the region.

- Increased investment in bio-businesses includes diversification of current crop production systems, improvements to the provision of agro-ecosystem and forest ecosystem goods and services, and increased access to markets for smallholder farmers in remote areas.
- This will contribute to enhanced biodiversity conservation, improved livelihoods, growth, species diversity and ecosystem function.

**Implementation:** The programme is implemented by financial institutions. The programme is classified as category II (medium level of intermediation) because the potential E&S risks and impacts are likely to be low for most end-users, and are largely manageable by implementing appropriate measures at the project level. These risks fall within the medium category and that this is within the ESF's accreditation level. High E&S risk activities (category A) will be excluded from the programme. The ESF has prepared an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP). As part of the ESMP, the AE will follow its E&S risk procedures and use the Environmental and Social Safeguards Capacity (ESSC) tool to identify the needs of local financing institutions to manage the E&S risks.

**Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (GSSS) standards:** The discussion below summarises the programme’s compliance with the GSSS standards.


---
**Page 118**
---

### ES2: Labour and working conditions

The programme may have potential for child and forced labour practices that occur in the region (including supply chain-related practices) as well as other forms of exploitation such as bonded or debt bondage. To mitigate these concerns, the programme will support the development of sectoral labour practices and working conditions guidelines consistent with ILO conventions and standards, including environmental and social impact assessment and the implementation of a due diligence framework.

### ES3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The eligible activities under the programme may have potential to cause water pollution as a result of their implementation. There may also be potential for air emissions from land acquisition and other investments. To mitigate these risks, the programme will ensure that implementers acquire the necessary environmental health and safety (EHS) training and certification in accordance with international environment, health and safety (EHS) practices and guidelines. Nevertheless, the programme will not include those investments that demonstrate significant risk of induced deforestation or which present significant risk of increased pollution.

### ES3: Community health, safety, and security

The implementation of the programme may also have potential to cause adverse impacts on community health, safety, and security (physical and chemical) including concerns on access to resources and benefit sharing. To mitigate these risks, the programme will ensure that implementers acquire the necessary EHS training and certification in accordance with international environment, health and safety practices and guidelines. The programme will also support the development of EHS checklists for communities and guidance assessments consistent with the AEI environmental and social performance standard.

### ES3: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The programme will not support activities that will involve involuntary resettlement and these investments will not be eligible under the Green Climate Fund (GCF) criteria for eligibility, including land acquisitions and investments. Should land acquisition occur, this will be conducted on a negotiated settlement and will be in a “selling-seller-buying buyer” basis.

### ES3: Land use change and sustainable management of living natural resources

The programme may include land conversion and deforestation risks that could affect present or future livelihoods and the environment. To mitigate these risks, the programme will exclude activities that will require deforestation, or which present significant risk of induced deforestation, including those in the supply chain. Monitoring of land use and land-cover change will also be conducted.

### ES3: Indigenous peoples and the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy

The Pan Amazon region where the programme will be implemented is home to 10 million indigenous people (around 4 per cent of total population). To mitigate potential risks to indigenous peoples, including Afro-descendant and peasant communities, the programme will exclude sub-projects


---
**Page 119**
---

that could have potentially adverse impacts on communities and for indigenous populations, including involuntary resettlement. The AE also notes that any potential risks to indigenous peoples will be managed in line with its latest new environmental and social policy framework, which has been developed based on lessons learned from previous projects. This framework requires prior and informed consent of affected communities. It also excludes the possibility of projects that may have potentially adverse impacts on indigenous peoples, unless they are part of a larger program or "in initial context". The AE has included an outline for the development of socioecological analyses and indigenous peoples plans. While additional due diligence on indigenous peoples will be conducted as per the GCF’s environmental and social policy framework, this will be engaged by the AE.

The AE also aims to ensure consultation and stakeholder engagement with indigenous peoples and has conducted socialization activities through round-table meetings that include indigenous organizations. Due to the COVID-19 pandemic, dialogues have been conducted mainly virtually. The AE notes in the ESMS that, as a result of these dialogues, elements to strengthen engagement will be included in the project design. These include the development of an indigenous grants programme for indigenous and traditional communities, continuous stakeholder engagement, and the inclusion of indigenous peoples in the dialogue process on topics such as indigenous knowledge, capacity-building of women and empowerment of women. The AE has also developed a gender action plan that targets the inclusion of women in territorial management and local livelihoods. An action plan that targets inclusion of indigenous peoples and local communities to increase their business capacities is part of the general action plan.

### 95. ESS B: Cultural heritage

The implementation of the programme could have adverse impacts on cultural heritage, including traditional sites and landscapes, as well as efforts to protect cultural heritage from the adverse impacts due to project activities and initiatives related to preservation. The sub-projects will also promote the equitable sharing of benefits from the use of cultural resources and ensure that indigenous peoples are involved in decision-making.

### 96. Implementation arrangements

The AE will monitor the environmental and social performance of the specific sub-projects implemented by the programme through supervision by safeguards specialists, loan officers, and other relevant staff members. The WOrls on compliance with environmental and social safeguards. Supervision missions will be conducted at least once a year to assess progress towards achieving environmental and social compliances. Financial institutions will be supported in the development of each sub-project implementation plan (SIP) for the specific project activities. Progress towards achieving environmental and social objectives will be part of the legal and contractual agreements with financial institutions.

### 97. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

During the development of the programme, consultations and dialogues have been conducted with representatives of traditional communities to identify potential adverse impacts on indigenous peoples' concerns raised during these consultations that were considered by the programme include issues on extensive land use for agriculture, loss of biodiversity, lack of access to water resources, exclusion of indigenous peoples’ concerns: potential for deforestation; land-tenure issues; and exclusion of small organizations in decision-making activities. The programme has also developed a stakeholder engagement plan to ensure that all relevant measures are taken with affected and interested parties. Stakeholder engagement and consultations will be conducted throughout the project.

### 98. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM)

The programme will have a GRM for communities and individuals who may be affected or potentially affected by the activities under the programme. The AE has developed a three-tiered approach to address grievances through the AE’s GRM, and the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism. This three-tiered approach will be


---
**Page 120**
---

established early in the stakeholder engagement process, in a culturally appropriate manner and its decisions will include inputs from locally affected stakeholders. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF indigenous peoples' focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### 4.2 Gender policy

101. The gender assessment provides the context of the enabling environment in the six countries covered by this document and its findings are based on the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women and its optional protocol and the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence Against Women. It also takes into account the principle that there is a respect for equality between women and men, and prohibition of discrimination based on sex. Each country has different levels of progress in implementing, promoting and protecting the rights of women and other marginalised communities. This assessment highlights some of these differences with regard to implementation of women's policies (Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador), gender action plan (Suriname), gender and climate change policy (Guyana) and gender equality strategy (Peru). Despite the institutional support, legal frameworks and policies, the ability for women to access resources and opportunities remains low and women are persistently in a disadvantaged position compared with men.

102. The gender assessment was completed based on a desk review and it is expected that the programme will be further informed by additional data collection through stakeholder consultations. This is to be done at the initial phase of programme implementation. As per the findings of the gender assessment, women across the Amazon region are engaged in reproductive activities such as childbearing and breastfeeding, but also in productive activities including the rearing of livestock, and engagement in agroforestry and non-timber forest products activities. Women play a major role in the management and conservation of the natural environment. In addition, women are also active in the productive activities of agriculture, fishing, hunting and gathering. They have access to a wide range of productive assets (land, animals, capital), access to formal markets and economies, information technologies and more decision-making power over financial and other resources, including natural resources.

103. Cultural and social norms continue to create barriers for women's engagement in leadership roles at all levels of society. Women are less likely than men to have a high level of education or to be literate. Few women in leadership roles in local and national governance and few women entrepreneurs in the formal economy. The main barriers that female entrepreneurs encounter is forming formal business associations, lack of access to credit and other financial services, responsibilities preventing them from working longer hours outside the home not being informed about financial services and products available for women, lack of awareness regarding legal ownership or guarantee; and a general inequality in entrepreneurial opportunities, including access to land and natural resources. Women are also less likely than men to be employed. Existing financial service providers and their products also generally ignore the particular needs of women entrepreneurs and do not provide them with adequate information. These barriers also limit opportunities for indigenous peoples and people of African descent.

104. Land and land tenure are resources distributed unequally among men and women, because of gender-based social norms that restrict women's access to land. This is exacerbated by women having less access to state identification or visibility processes that prevent them from participating in formalization processes. Although Amazonian women

## 4.3 Risks

### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The funding proposal requests USD 135 million in senior sovereign loans, USD 6.5 million in equity and USD 37.5 million in grants (of which USD 20 million is reimbursable). The programme aims to increase the flow of funds from public and private financial institutions into natural resources and ecosystems in the Amazon region by enabling the conditions to increase the flow of funds to bio-businesses in the bioreconomy of the Amazon. Countries included in the programme are Brazil,


---
**Page 122**
---

# Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname

The AE is providing co-financing of up to USD 155 million in senior loans, up to USD 145 million in guarantees and up to USD 6 million in equity. IDB together with other donors will also provide USD 14 million in grant funding.

## Component I GCF will provide USD 135 million of senior loans to EEs for on-lending to eligible borrowers, subject to the terms and conditions set out in the loan agreement, including a guarantee provided by the borrower or its sovereign. Additionally, USD 16 million in reimbursable grants will be provided to support early stage projects. The IDB will provide USD 20 million in grant funding for component I. USD 4 million of this will be used as a guarantee benefiting the LFs. The AE shall hold the funds from the members and other donors in trust, and disburse them only upon receipt of the loan application. To support (early stage) bio-biodiesel USD 4 million reimbursable grants and USD 6.5 million in equity will be provided. A total of USD 54.6 million is allocated for technical cooperation under component I.

## Component II will provide support to public entities to leverage capital market financing through the issuance of green bonds. The AE will provide USD 10 million in grant funding for technical cooperation and USD 30 million in grants to deploy credit enhancement instruments for green bonds issued by public sector entities. The USD 30 million in grants will be used to remunerate the guarantee provider, which will provide guarantees on behalf of the public sector entities to bondholders to investors. The guarantees by the AE aim to increase investor appetite for thematic bond issuance. The component II GCF will provide USD 25 million for transversal technical cooperation.

### Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The AE, IDB has an extensive track record in the region amounting to nearly USD 166 billion for projects. The AE has experience in providing climate finance demonstrated by six approved projects with the GCF and approved green credit lines for over USD 1.5 billion in the past five years. The AE is rated AA+ by Moody’s and AAA by Fitch.

The AE will be supported by a team of experts from IDB, NDBs, private sector fund management company, other private sector entities and the AE. The AE will assess the capacity of each EE as per the Operations manual (Annex 21) prior to entering into any Subcontracting Agreement with the EE. The AE will also provide technical assistance to participating governments of the programme countries and may include NDBs or government agencies. The EE receives technical assistance from the AE in the areas of project preparation, implementation, and for component II LGE the EE will be public sector issuers. The EEs for technical assistance can also include private sector entities.

GCF will rely on IDB to adequately assess the capacity of the EEs. Given the limited visibility on the EEs for GCF at this stage and the potential number of entities involved the AE/GCF team will need to be flexible in its approach.

IDB and the designated EEs (including NDBs) clearly have the necessary institutional and financial capacity to manage the programme and to select, assess and monitor sub-project implementation. The IDB has a well-established project management system with an assessment methodology and criteria, including for technical quality supervision, which gives sufficient confidence in the ability of the IDB to ensure project quality, which is also backed up by the level of technical detail provided in the feasibility study.

### Programme-specific execution risk (medium risk)

Sovereign credit risk: IDB shall enter into sovereign loan agreements on behalf of GCF with the countries under the programme. The loans will be either a direct sovereign loan or a


---
**Page 123**
---

loan to AE with a sovereign guarantee. Therefore, for USD 105 million in loans GCF will be exposed to credit risk of these countries. The level of credit risk differs between the countries in the programme: Peru has an investment grade rating Bfbb (Fitch), while Brazil and Colombia are regarded as non-investment grade with BBB- (S&P) and BB+ (Moody’s) respectively. This is due to an uncured default. The borrowing countries are member countries of the IDB, and the IDB will provide a credit enhancement in the form of a stand-by facility of USD 105 million and the beneficiary country is expected to reduce the sovereign credit risk. IRRBBs are presented in the funding proposal are contingent on approval of the IDB board and could be subject to change. The AE has agreed that GCF will have full alignment with the financial alignment of GCF with the IDB. Allocation to a single country of sovereign loans is set at up to 60 per cent of the total amount of the loan, while allocation to a single country of IRRBBs is set at up to 40 per cent. The AE will commit to co-financing the loans by its own resources and on a pari-passu basis.

### 11.2 Private sector investment risk: Risks for the investments undertaken on behalf of GCF in the private sector are divided into two categories. First, there is credit risk which gives the risks inherent in the financial instrument as well as early stage at which GCF will provide funding to the project. Second, there is equity risk which arises from the fact that co-financing by GCF under component I.II is contingent on the repayment of private sector loans guaranteed by these projects. The AE has agreed that GCF will have full alignment with the financial alignment of GCF with the IDB. Allocation to a single country of sovereign loans is set at up to 60 per cent of the total amount of the loan, while allocation to a single country of IRRBBs is set at up to 40 per cent.

#### Credit risk: Risk arises from the inability of the AE to repay the AE. Lack of co-financing could delay the programme or reduce the size and therefore impact achieved in a timely manner. There are no specific credit ratings for GCF loans, but there may be appetite for thematic bonds issuance but the issuances are not tied at this stage. Some comfort can be derived from the fact that the AE has a rating included on the sovereign risk index (which includes resources outside of the AE) and has an estimated financing ratio of 1:48 for the grants under component I.II.

#### Equity risk: The programme will focus on enabling conditions to increase the flow of business in the bioeconomy. Lack of demand for such financing or lack of economic opportunities would reduce the impact of the programme. Mitigating factors for this risk are the financial commitment from the AE and the fact that the loans are co-financed by GCF, which includes non-formal demand declaration by the AE.

### 11.3 Foreign exchange risk: Risks under component I.II will be in USD. Currency risk will be absorbed by the EE or the intermediary LFI. Shocks in exchange rates could impact the EEA ability to repay, potentially resulting in the need to invoke the sovereign guarantee.

#### HEDGING: The AE shall commit to hedge 100 per cent of the foreign currency (FC) proceeds in accordance with the goals set out in the funding proposal and the initial eligibility criteria. The AE will be required to enter into a hedging agreement with GCF or another intermediary in accordance with the investment criteria or repaid to the AE and GCF. The AE shall contractually require that ELIs for the duration of the programme will invest proceeds of GCF in eligible sub-projects. Any foreign currency proceeds generated from the sale of carbon credits from GCF grant for guarantee fees shall remain invested in eligible sub-projects for the duration of the programme.

### 12. Carbon credits: Although the sale of carbon credits is not an objective of the programme, the term sheet includes no requirements to collect or retire carbon credits generated by the projects. The AE has agreed that GCF will have full alignment with the financial alignment of GCF with the IDB. Allocation to a single country of sovereign loans is set at up to 60 per cent of the total amount of the loan, while allocation to a single country of IRRBBs is set at up to 40 per cent.

# Results monitoring and reporting

The programme is a facility supporting bio-businesses in six countries in the Amazon basin, focusing on the improvement of adaptive capacity of various beneficiaries and increased resilience as well as the development of innovative products and services along the bio-business value chains.

The programme is expected to reach a total of 191,352 direct beneficiaries and 485,375 indirect beneficiaries (of which around 50 per cent are final in all six countries). The estimation of the beneficiaries follows a clear logic and is linked to the provision of adaptation capacity and increased resilience as well as the development of innovative products and services.

The programme also expects to lead to approximately 121,639 jobs over the programme lifetime of 27 years. The estimates are conducted using clear assumptions and peer-reviewed methodologies and tools, such as the E4eXt tool. The additionality of the emission reductions is ensured by the fact that the programme does not provide any financial support to projects ensuring that the activities supported by the programme would not take place without GCF funding.

The theory of change clearly establishes the impact pathways of the programme, including the expected changes in the institutional and policy environment as well as the socio-economic context.

The impact and outcome indicators have been correctly selected and included in the logical framework in alignment with the GCF results management framework and performance measurement methodology. The use of a common set of indicators across all six countries will facilitate efficient monitoring of the programme results, outcomes and impacts.

Annex 7 has been completed to show the GCF requirements, clearly describing the timelines of the individual activities and the concrete deliverables.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP173

Proposal name: The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund: Unlocking private capital by valuing bioeconomy products and services with climate mitigation co-benefits in the Amazon

Accredited entity: Inter-American Development Bank (IDB)

Country/ies: Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname

Project/programme size: Large


## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

#### 1. Concept
The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund is a large-sized programmatic funding proposal for the Amazon region in Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname. It covers six Amazonian countries: Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Peru and Suriname and is based on the principles of the Leticia Pact for the Amazon Region signed by the governments of Bolivia, Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Venezuela in 2015. The Fund represents a significant and important step towards implementation of the Pact as adoption of the action plan. The Pact is seeking cooperation with the IDB to provide financial resources that will create new opportunities for the sustainable development and well-being of its population. Among the central concepts of the Leticia Pact are: strengthening the conservation, recovery and resilience of biodiversity and ecosystems; and establishing a framework for financing the transition towards low-emission and climate change resilient bio-businesses in the Amazon region. The Fund aims to support countries to promote the financial mechanisms, with the collaboration of the IDB, to implement the Pact and increase private investment in bio-business. Funding for the implementation of the mandate of the Leticia Pact will be provided through a combination of grants from regional or subregional projects to different international funding sources. The funding proposal is based on the assumption that the Fund will provide financial resources strengthening private investment flow into bio-business.

#### 2. Low-emission and climate change resilience bio-businesses in the Amazon Bioeconomy Fund
In the funding proposal 50 per cent of GCF proceeds if it motivates activities in land-use changes and the forest result area and then returning the 50 per cent to the GCF. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) has reviewed the sample projects provided in the funding proposal, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) has reviewed the sample projects provided in the funding proposal, and in most cases the adaptation measures are not in fact direct responses to well demonstrated climate change impacts. In some cases, the reasons for low resilience of ecosystems are unsustainable anthropogenic activities such as unsustainable land use that causes land degradation. However, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) has noted that where these activities are eliminated or mitigated in a sustainable way could strengthen the ecosystem’s resilience, increase farmers’ incomes and
