### GCF Financial Support

GCF provides three types of financial support: IUSD 137.5 million grant contribution including reimbursable grant, which is 49 per cent of total cost; IUSD 208.6 million loan (IUSD 6.5 million interest); and IUSD 279 million (47 per cent) from GCF. The accredited implementing entity (AIE) for the funding proposal is the World Bank Group. The total cost of the funding proposal is IUSD 597 million including IUSD 279 million [47 per cent] from GCF. The accredited implementing entity (AIE) for the funding proposal is the World Bank Group.

### Implementation Arrangements/Financial Modalities

Implementation of the funding proposal will operate through the executing entities (EE). Prior to entering into any subsidiary agreements, each EE must enter into a memorandum of understanding with the AIE. The EE will provide technical capacity to implement and monitor climate change-related projects. The funding proposal is structured in three components: Component 1.1 sovereign loans; Component 1.2 equity investments; and Component 1.3 investment grants.

#### (a) In the Execution of Sovereign Loans and Investment Grants

Component 1.1 EE will be selected by the AIE through a competitive bidding process in accordance with the subsidiary agreement. Eligible EE will be identified jointly with national authorities, based on their experience in implementing climate change activities, experience in finance structuring and fiduciary management, track record, including experience in managing public financial instruments, and experience of being business entities. EEs may include National Development Banks (NDBs) or other government agencies, such as FINDECOM (Colombia), CONAFOR (Ecuador), COFIDE (Peru). Suitable EE will be selected by the AIE through a competitive bidding process.

Final beneficiaries of individual subprojects implemented through this component will be private entities. The final beneficiaries may include small and medium enterprises operating in the region. Final beneficiaries may be individual investors, associations/structures (cooperative or similar structures), and/or other public or private entities. In addition to the above, the final beneficiaries (farmers) will be involved in monitoring the results of this component at the level of collective action groups. The AIE will provide further information on the respective modalities for EE. Details are provided in annex 11 to the funding proposal.

#### (b) For the Execution of Equity Investments

Component 1.2 eligible EE includes an entity identified by the AIE through a competitive bidding process as an anchor company with experience in identifying sustainable early-stage biotechnology businesses with low emission intensity and high potential for climate change mitigation (e.g., cleantech start-ups) to be defined and assessed by DFI’s Innovation laboratory (IDB Lab) that will receive equity investments. The activities under this component are equity investments.

This component has the type of final beneficiaries and structure and reporting of results as Component 1.1.

#### (c) For the Execution of Investment Grants

Component 1.3 EE for management of the guarantee element of GCF funds, IDB will transfer GCF grant resources to a public entity


---
**Page 130**
---

Issuer acting as EE. This will defray the financial charges associated with the portion of the guarantee funded with IDB resources. As part of its pre-project preparation cycle, and prior to approval of the relevant guarantee operation, IDB will conduct an institutional capacity assessment (ICA) for each EE to assess the EE’s technical capacity to develop and manage climate change related projects.

Direct beneficiaries will be public entities, including national or subnational governments, as well as private sector entities that are market-facing through the issuance of thematic bonds aimed at supporting bio-companies, which will be the final beneficiaries. The IDB will support those companies with a combination of technical field data and those that do not have sufficient capacity will be supported by other resources.

For all technical cooperation activities (Components I, II, I) III) implemented with grant, IDB may be the EE and/or it may enter into a sub-agreement with other EEs. Sub-component 1.1 is the execution of sovereign loans (total loan USD 290 million with USD 135 million from GCF and USD 155 million from DB) and investment grants (total grant USD 46 million with USD 8 million from GCF and USD 38 million from DB). Funds will be channeled by the IDB to EEs via: (a) loans to enable EEs to provide lending to bio-companies, including financial intermediaries and development institutions (FIs) that would extend them to beneficiaries; and (b) targeted investment grants to support early-stage bio-companies. The loan terms will be tailored to the needs of LPs in each country-specific context to help them properly manage incremental risks. Products offered by EEs to beneficiaries that have received loans or investment grants under Sub-component 1.1 are: (a) loans funded by the loans; and/or (b) de-risking instruments structured with the investment grant. The structure of resources for Sub-components I.1 will be IDB and LF). The structure under which resources for Component I.1 may be channeled financing lines and de-risking financial solutions.

Sub-component 12 is the execution of equity investments (USD 5.0 million from GCF and USD 4.8 million from DB) and investment grants (USD 4.6 million from GCF and USD 5.2 million from DB, (Karei Investments)). This sub-component will be used to provide financial support for the development of early-stage bio-businesses in the Amazon region in the form of direct equity, investments or investment grants. The IDB will also provide technical assistance. Sub-component 12 will support ongoing IDB Lab initiatives, such as Regenerate Accelerator a pilot fund that invests in high-potential regenerative bioeconomy

# Amazon small business relief fund: Amazon Relief Fund

The Natural Conservancy (TNC) is an organization with a proven track record of using the best available science for conservation and has extensive experience in developing nature-based solutions initiatives.

## The Amazon Relief Fund Concept

The Amazon Relief Fund concept is a technology-driven methodology with the aim to develop a green circular economy that takes full advantage of the value of a "meaningful and permanent genetic forecast" so that it can reduce deforestation, improve livelihoods for local communities, and provide long-term benefits for biodiversity.

## Components of the Amazon Relief Fund

### Component E2.1
Component E2.1 is initially targeted at Brazil, Colombia, and Ecuador only. Nonetheless, final allocations will be made to all countries in the region where the project described in annexes A and B.A is relevant. The country targeting in this text is only indicative.

### Component E2.2
The Amazon Relief Fund concept is a technology-driven methodology with the aim to develop a green circular economy that takes full advantage of the value of a "meaningful and permanent genetic forecast" so that it can reduce deforestation, improve livelihoods for local communities, and provide long-term benefits for biodiversity.

### Component E2.3
Component E2.3 is initially targeted at Brazil, Colombia, and Ecuador only. Nonetheless, final allocations will be made to all countries in the region where the project described in annexes A and B.A is relevant. The country targeting in this text is only indicative.

(vi) Sub-component III.H.1 considers inclusion mechanisms for women and gender diversity in bio-business (USD 5 million, GCF). These actions will be carried out in accordance with national gender strategies;

(vii) Sub-component III.H.2 considers strengthening the ecosystem for Amazonian forest and non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and their communities (USD 5 million, GCF and USD 1 million, IIB). This sub-component creates and consolidates a network of actors in the Amazon region that will develop connectors that are synergistic with the Amazonia+4.0 concept and that will strengthen the role of indigenous peoples and local communities as forest maintainers and generators of a bio-business ecosystem;

(viii) Sub-component III.H.3 considers strengthening the stakeholder engagement platform (USD 1 million, IIB). This sub-component increases levels of engagement: information, dialogue, public consultations, collaboration and participation.

(ix) Sub-component III.H.4 is a small grants program (SGP) (USD 10 million, GCF). This activity seeks to empower and enable local stakeholders - especially women and indigenous peoples and local communities - to take on initiatives and bio-businesses that will enhance and increase development opportunities in the Amazon region. The funds will be used to strengthen mechanisms for inclusive business, accountability and participation;

(x) Sub-component III.H.5 considers general advisory services (USD 5 million, GCF) for facilitation of Let's Part regional dialogue among signatories.10 High-quality knowledge products will be developed on the basis of lessons learned with the Amazon Cooperation Treaty Organization (ACTO) among others. The IIB will fund this activity.

**Eligibility criteria:** During the review process the AF provided additional clarification on bio-business and eligibility criteria for this funding proposal. It was clarified that for the Let's Part initiative, projects must be implemented in a manner that responsibly integrates biodiversity into its core processes, incorporates strategies and tools for climate change adaptation and mitigation, respects the local communities, indigenous peoples and traditional knowledge and internalizes costs for the sustainable use and conservation of natural resources.

The eligibility criteria for investment projects as provided during the review process are:

(a) Reduce emissions and increase ecosystem carbon stocks by expanding the forest-based economy;

(b) Support ecosystem protection, regeneration and adaptation, increasing social, climatic and economic resilience;

(c) Promote a more productive matrix through more efficient and sustainable use of natural resources, channelling private capital to the conservation, restoration and sustainable use of ecosystems, including soil; and/or

10 Among traditional peoples and communities are indigenous peoples, quilombolas, traditional communities of African origin, extractive workers and other local communities. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Pastoral council, but specifically contributes to its objectives 1, 8, 13 and 15, related to the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity and ecosystem services, including the development of market systems, including value chains, fostering technological transfer to encourage sustainable business development, and strengthening financial programmes and mechanisms, including with support from multilateral banks.

### Contribution to a more sustainable and resilient post-COVID-19 recovery with socio-economic benefits for the local population and indigenous peoples and seek the participation of women in all financial components.

The independent TAP considers that the criteria are too narrow, and that the focus on these criteria sample projects presented in the funding proposal. For greater transparency TAP recommends developing more specific eligibility criteria. In addition to the investment eligibility criteria, the independent TAP concluded that perhaps not all species considered for conservation under this programme will be suitable for the direct benefits of project implementation more Amazonian friendly species should be selected which could significantly contribute to the achievement of the programme’s objectives. The TAP noticed that eligibility criteria should not only account for the direct benefits under sustainable management and conservation, but also consider the effect of the programme in terms of structures established and institutions that could contribute to reducing the fire dry corrugates of Amazon.

It is estimated the impact of the programme to be 121.400.953 tCO2 emission reduction for the 27 years of the project’s lifespan. Annual greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reduction is estimated as 6,170.480 tCO2eq. Direct beneficiaries of the adaptation measures are the bio-businesses and their employees. Indirect beneficiaries include the local population. The first direct beneficiaries of the programme are those bio-business owners and their families whose adaptive capacity will be enhanced by the project. The second direct beneficiaries are the communities resulting from the programme (see annex Xa to the funding proposal) and the number of bio-businesses that will be established in the future. The number of indirect beneficiaries could be doubled if the conservation component is added to the investment criteria.

The independent TAP asked the AE to provide its opinion on the added value of this programme for the achievement of the SDGs. In response, the AE provided the following opinions:

- Demonstration of profitability of the bio-economy through creating a diverse portfolio of sustainable products. This is not always possible at the country level, particularly for countries with a small economy and limited markets.
The programme will also provide an opportunity to demonstrate the viability of these investments in terms of financial returns and other economic benefits over time. The programme will also oversee the level of engagement of local public and private sector actors in the development of the bio-economy at the national level, as well as the development of technology to support the creation of a regional pipeline and market.
- Issues and challenges for thematic bonds. As regards the thematic bonds, besides securing sufficient investment in funds earmarked for bio-businesses and their direct contribution to the achievement of the SDGs, it is important that these funds make some additional contributions with longer-lasting effect (i.e asset demonstration) since supported bonds will offer another platform to demonstrate the viability of these aspects. The thematic bonds should also be designed to support the development of capacities and standards on the issuer side (for issuance, monitoring and reporting) that may be required for the future issuance of other types of bonds addressing successful issuances in the future by either the same or other potential issuers;

## Development of green employment in the region

The programme is strengthening regional integration among Amazon countries, having common issues, by supporting the regionalization of bioeconomy value chains and integration initiatives. This holistic and systemic approach aims to promote sustainable development in the Amazon Region, promoting inclusion and sustainable business models.

### The proposed programme considers a set of interlinked individual projects, unified under a single funding proposal

The programme is designed to enhance the resilience of target value chains and ecosystems in the Amazon region by enabling the conditions for their long-term sustainability. Interlinked individual projects contribute to common objectives and strategies that are aligned with the priorities of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) mitigation and adaptation result areas of efficiency and effectiveness at an scale.

### Create and consolidate at a scale network of projects, technical assistance providers and market actors

The programme will create and consolidate a network of projects, technical assistance providers and market actors in the Amazon region. This will enable the scaling up of best practices and lessons learned that strengthen the role of traditional peoples and communities as forest maintainers and generate new opportunities for their inclusion.

### Provision of general advisory services related to:

- (i) facilitation of Leticia Pact regional dialogue among signatories;
- (ii) knowledge and dissemination across the region on the development, implementation and operation of bioeconomy value chains collection systems; and
- (iii) analysis of limitations and opportunities of business informality in target value chains.

### The independent TAP fully agrees with the AEV's view on the added value of this regional programme and would like to particularly highlight the benefits which could be gained by scaling up the project at a regional level

The independent TAP’s opinion is that the funding proposal has high impact potential but at the same time recognizes the complexity of this funding proposal for implementation. This implies that the programme will require a strong commitment from all signatories, including: a proper management structure, effective eligibility criteria, including a conservation agreement and resource use permit; an appropriate monitoring system to ensure compliance with what is developed and in the funding proposal though the independent TAP still has reservations related to the inclusion of the adaptation projects. The independent TAP stresses these criteria as medium to high and recommends the AE to focus on the relevance of subjects and their climate change impacts.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: High

As mentioned in paragraph 1, this assessment, the programme considered in the funding proposal is constructed on the principles of the Leticia Pact signed by seven countries for the Amazon Region. This initiative recognizes the significance of cooperation for the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity and natural resources as a basis for the sustainable development and well-being of its population. The Pact reaffirms the sovereign rights of signatory countries over their territories, including the management of these resources; including the development and sustainable use of these resources, declares the willingness of signatory countries to move the region onto a sustainable development pathway, renouncing annexationism and colonialism, and recognizing the importance of cooperation among all conventions and initiatives regulating the sustainable management of biodiversity. Including the UNFCCC climate change convention.

Annex 2a to the funding proposal provides the analysis of progress in implementation in the Amazon countries of three interdependent processes regulated by the UNFCCC (climate

## Sustainable development potential Scale: High

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

Sustainable development is the key principle of the Leticia Pact and the basis of the funding proposal. The sustainability of the Pact was expressed by the signatory parties through adoption of the action plan, which further developed the mandates of the agreement into specific actions to be implemented in the region. The action plan sets out a roadmap towards implementation of the Leticia Pact and a step towards regional cooperation for sustainable development. It is important to note that the funding proposal does not address the causes of deforestation and illegal activities that are taking place in the Amazon region, with 52 key actions organized in 5 thematic areas: promoting the conservation and sustainable development of forests; protecting biodiversity and ecosystems; improving livelihoods and local communities living in this strategic ecosystem, including their cultural heritage and traditional knowledge.

The funding proposal contributes to Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 15 committed to protect, restore and promote sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems, to sustainably manage forests, combat desertification, halt and reverse land degradation and halt biodiversity loss. In particular, the funding proposal contributes to: target 15.9 considering the integration of ecosystem-based approaches into national strategies and policies for sustainable development and poverty reduction strategies; and goals 15.a and 15.b related to mobilizing and increasing financial resources for conservation and sustainable use of ecosystems and protecting terrestrial ecosystems at all levels. The sample subprojects considered in the funding proposal could contribute to SDG 15.2 which calls to promote the implementation of sustainable management

*For example, the leading project activity includes the promotion of native species, among other activities. Considering that climate patterns are being changed beyond their traditional variability, these native species and associated ecosystem services may be more resilient than introduced or exotic species. Therefore, it is important that only “climate-resilient native species” should be supported and promoted.*

The action plan was adopted at the 10th Conference of the Parties (COP10) of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), held in December 2019 in Madrid, Spain.

of all types of trees, halt deforestation, restore degraded forests and substantially increase afforestation and reforestation globally.

### Under mandate number (9) the action plan considers the strengthening of cooperation which should contribute to regional sustainable development. The funding proposal plans to make significant contributions in the areas of capacity building, knowledge sharing, planning knowledge and strengthening capability throughout the region. For all components the funding proposal is committed to a gender perspective and to the inclusion of youth.

In particular, all five sub-components of Component III are dedicated to transversal technical cooperation:

- Grants for technical cooperation in Component I.3 will provide critical support to build capacities, innovation, business resources and regulations, among others, to help overcome barriers to the implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation activities in bio-businesses that will reduce the negative impacts of climate change.
- Grant for specialized advisory services for bond issuers will support the participant public entities and will be dedicated to development of the bond framework, including eligibility criteria, technical assistance, capacity building, harmonization and standardization of bond reporting following international standards, particularly through the use of a common template.
- USD 20 million grant allocated for Component III should contribute to operationalization of mechanisms for women's inclusion as well as to create and consolidate synergies with other initiatives and projects in the region that are synergetic with the Amazonia 4.0 concept and that should strengthen the role of Amazonian women in the design, implementation and monitoring of ecosystem activities. This activity seeks to improve the design and execution of the whole process of the Amazon Grantee Program, which is a pilot program with quantifiable results to make it possible to measure both its effectiveness and the value added of this effort for the Amazon region. The Small Grants Programme is intended to empower women in the Amazon region (including indigenous peoples, Afro-descendants and peasants (campesinos)) - to take on initiatives and bio-businesses to enhance their economic independence.
- Activities planned in the funding proposal contribute to the SDG climate target 13.2 committed to integrate climate change measures into national policies, strategies and planning. The funding proposal is also committed to the implementation of the SDG poverty target 1.3 (end poverty) through contribution to the creation of policy frameworks at the national and regional levels based on pro-poor and gender-sensitive development strategies, to support accelerated investment in poverty reduction.
- Programme activities are expected to contribute to increase sources of income for agricultural and rural communities in rural areas; create direct and indirect employment; develop technology and innovation.

Based on this assessment of the funding proposal's contribution to the various SDG goals and targets, particularly the objective of the project to contribute to implementation of the Leticia Pact in a sustainable low emission and climate resilient way.

# Needs of the recipient

## State: High

### Findings from comprehensive analyses of national circumstances and gaps regarding the three interlinked and interfingering processes ongoing in Amazon countries such as: country needs identified by the AE during the survey; gaps and needs identified by the AE during the survey; funding proposal countries involved in REDD+ plus activity along with a full system of REDD+ safeguards in place; and countries lack the financial and technical resources to fully implement their REDD+ plus and sustainable forest management plans.

#### (a) None of the funding proposal countries involved in REDD+ plus activity along with a full system of REDD+ safeguards in place

- Limited technical and financial capacities were also identified as hindering implementation of REDD+ related policies in Guyana, Peru and Suriname.
- Although countries have advanced in creating policies and programmes promoting community participation in forest management and sustainable development that offers large opportunities for the development of bioeconomy projects;
- Several countries have not yet aligned their sectoral laws and policy frameworks with their national REDD+ plus strategy. For example, Colombia's National REDD+ plus Strategy is also a trans-sectorial policy that targets sustainable agricultural development in addition to forest conservation, which is seen as a major problem.
- No of participant country has a full system of REDD+ safeguards in place;
- Unclear and insecure land tenure remains a major issue in all countries, in particular for indigenous peoples and other communities. Conflicts between indigenous lands and commercial forests are a major barrier to implementation of sustainable use and manage forest component. Forests IIIa (Small Grant Programme; USD 10 million) plan aims at addressing this problem by providing financial resources and enabling local stakeholders – especially women, indigenous peoples, Afro-descendants and people living in protected areas – to participate more effectively in decision-making and influence development outcomes with a bioeconomy perspective.
- Weak land use planning laws and lack of enforcement lead to overexploitation of forest uses. In particular, the lack of clear rules on access to forests, even when producing forest products or concessions. This represents a challenge for the execution of bioeconomy projects.

#### (b) Funding proposal countries involved in REDD+ plus activity along with a full system of REDD+ safeguards in place

- Despite the fact that legislation recognises community concessions, forest communities continue to face challenges in exercising sustainable forest management. Forest communities are often not considered as stakeholders by the competent authorities, which often require technical and financial capacity that the communities do not have. The recognition of rights over forest concessions is increasingly being recognised, but financial and support needs to be enhanced.
- Different sub-components of Component IIIa need to address different elements of this problem but the effective implementation of SEPs should be the most important.

### Country ownership

This funding proposal has very strong regional ownership based on the regional initiative to promote sustainable bio-businesses in the Amazon region. The independent TAP believes that demonstration of similar cooperative ownership around a problem considered in programming is an important indicator of the likelihood of success.

Country ownership regarding the programme could be estimated as high for each participant country based on the high degree of ownership of the Latex Pact which is a foundational element of the project. The fact that six countries are participating and engaged in which these six countries voluntarily participate, are also an indication of ownership.

Activities planned in the funding proposal are fully in compliance with these countries' commitments to the UNFCCC, including their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and climate change action plans for implementation of national commitments to the REDD-plus programme.


---
**Page 139**
---

### Brazil, Colombia and Peru have adopted specific legal frameworks governing climate change mitigation and adaptation.

Brazil and Peru also have climate-related frameworks at sub-national level.

#### Few countries have developed specific gender strategies related to climate change.
Although some countries have laws or policies mandating a gender-responsive approach to climate change and the environment. Peru is perhaps the most advanced of the seven Latin American countries in implementing the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change Action Plan, which specifically addresses forests.

The majority of the programme countries are participants in REDD+ plus programmes and have developed national strategies for forest conservation and/or REDD+ plus mechanism. Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador and Guyana are among the most advanced countries globally in REDD+ implementation, with significant contributions to climate change mitigation from international donors.

All the countries have sectors or general policy frameworks that cover some areas of relevance to bioeconomy, such as payments for ecosystem services, environmental incentives, supply chain legislation, organic agriculture and sustainable livestock management. However, these are not always comprehensive in scope compared to other countries analysed. Colombia has a very complete framework that covers areas related to the development of the Amazon region, including the National Forest Policy, Forest Strategy and policy on green growth and circular economy. In Guyana, bioeconomy-related activities are included in the Carbon Development Strategy and Green State Development Plan. Brazil has a number of policies and initiatives under its forest laws focusing on the development of the Amazon region – for instance, the Peruvian Law Promoting Sustainable Use of Forest Resources.

Subnational governments in Brazil and Peru have also embedded bioeconomy promotion in their regional strategies. In Brazil, Amazon states have also adopted different approaches to promote bioeconomy.

Almost all countries have national forest incentive schemes for both restoration and conservation, which are key elements of the funding proposal.
While approach to REDD+ differ, many countries have included bioeconomy-related approaches as part of their strategies.

Payment for ecosystem services legislation exists both at the national and subnational level in most countries. Colombia has a specific law on payments for environmental services while Ecuador has specific schemes that implement payment for ecosystem services.

Legislation and sustainable supply chain policies are at an early stage of development and are often focused on specific products, with national laws covering with voluntary schemes or international certification schemes. Brazil is developing more supportive regulatory frameworks in relation to sustainable supply chain policies.
Most countries have sustainable supply chain strategies focused on their main agricultural commodities (e.g., coffee, cocoa, palm oil, rubber, soybeans, timber and coca) (Colombia, Peru). These products are in the focus area mainly described in the sampling of bioeconomy projects in this programme and represent significant potential.

The concept of the bioeconomy is proposed as a principle of the Article Part 20 in compliance with REDD+ initiatives in which all six countries are involved. Peru and Bolivia are not involved in this programme.

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex B Gender Assessment

# INTRODUCTION

More than 34 million people, including 120 indigenous peoples, live in the Amazon Region (Paraamazonia or Gran Amazonia) and depend on nature for food security, livelihoods, and shelter. The average percentage of Amazonian women varies between 48.3% and 49.7% of the population and differs according to different ethnic groups that are identified in each country. More than half of the Amazonian population are Brazilian, and more than half live in urban centers. There is a total of 10 cities with more than 500,000 inhabitants, with about 2 million inhabitants each.

The six countries that participate in the program "Amazon Bioeconomy Fund" (Brazil, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Guyana, and Suriname) present similar development indicators in education, employment, entrepreneurship, political representation, access to finance and property. However, significant gender gaps continue to exist for women due to cultural norms and traditional roles, which are wider in rural areas and among indigenous and afro-descendant women.

Women play a fundamental role in the Amazonia. As users of forest products and guardians of traditional knowledge, women have always been involved in the Amazonia. Nevertheless, their access to forest resources and the economic benefits generated by the bioeconomy is still limited.

On the other hand, the emergence of COVID-19 has revealed the fundamental role that women play in the first line of emergency and their role as caregivers at home that has interfered with their job performance. The economic recession has hit women more than men because they are more likely to be employed in informal sectors (informal economy crisis (tourist and trade among other) and (ii) the care of children without schools and other dependents has forced many women to leave their jobs, take part-time jobs, work in informal sectors or become unemployed. About 12 million women in LAC left the regional workforce due to job destruction.

Legal, regulatory and institutional framework. The six countries have ratified or acceded to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)² and its optional protocol. They also have ratified the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence Against Women (ILO Convention 190). In addition, they have adopted national laws that protect women's rights.

(Convention of Belém Do Pará)? Their constitutions address equality before the law and no one can be discriminated against on the basis of sex. Most countries have performed a normative reform effort to establish an adequate framework for development. However, normalization is not enough; it must also reflect the cultural and androcentric culture that is reflected in many social and institutional practices.

A. BRAZIL

The 1988 Constitution symbolizes the legal framework for the re-democratization of Brazil. For the formulation of the 1988 Constitution, popular participation was ensured. With regard to women's participation, the Charter of Brazilian Women to the Constituents stands out, which completed the demand of women's movements.

The 1988 Constitution reads out, among other things, by inaugurating its Text briefly fundamental rights and alternatives by Article 5, which establishes equality between men and women in general. Another point of significant changes is the family. The old Civil Code of 1916 contained several provisions that placed women in an inferior role within the family. The new Constitution of 1988 changed this situation. It explicitly stated out that the rights and duties relating to the conjugal society are exercised equally by men and women and that the State will ensure assistance to the family in the person of each one, as well as the protection of the family with regard to its economic and social relationships.

The Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights, former Ministry of Human Rights (1975–2015) is an office attached to the Presidency of Brazil. Its purpose is to implement, promote, and protect human rights, civic rights, and the rights of children, adolescents, and women in Brazil.

National Secretariat for Policies for Women (SNPW) is linked to the Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights and its main objective is to promote equality between men and women and combat all forms of prejudice and discrimination inherent from a gender perspective. The SNPW was created on 1987 by decree of the President of Brazil and has been fighting for the construction of a more just, egalitarian and democratic Brazil through the valorization of women and their inclusion in the country's social, economic, political and cultural development.

The SNPW is part of the Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights and the National Secretariat of Policies for Women to the organizational structure of the Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights

1 The Convention of Belém do Pará is an international human rights instrument adopted by the Inter-American Commission of Women (CIJ) of the Organization of American States at a conference held in Belém do Pará, Brazil on 3 June 2005. It is a human rights binding international treaty that criminalizes all forms of violence against women, especially sexual violence

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund: Annex B Gender Assessment

In 2004, the Nacional Plan of Policies for Women was created to combat gender discrimination and to consolidate educational policies for an equal gender perspective.

Those laws are aimed at people:

* Contributing to the implementation and institutionalization of the Policy Convention for Women approved from 2004, and endorsed in 2007 and 2011: respective conferences.
* As a national plan, it reaffirms the guiding principles of the Convention: (i) respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms; (ii) search for effective equality between women and men, in all spheres; (iii) respect for diversity and cultural differences; (iv) promotion of equal opportunities and universality of services and benefits offered by the State; (v) active participation of women in all public policy phases; and (vi) transversally as a guiding principle of all public policies.

Programs such as "Minha casa, minha vida" ("My house, my life"), created in 2009 to help people with low-income to buy their own house: almost 80% of the beneficiaries are women; and the "Bolsa Família" (Family Packet), created in 2003, through which payments are transferred directly into bank accounts, in the name of the woman of the family, are consolidating the role of women in social life.

| Law N° 12.705 (2012) | Employment discrimination, gender discrimination | Article 1 prohibits any discriminatory and limiting practice for the effect of access to employment, or for the performance thereof, by reason of: origin, race, colour, marital status, family situation or age. | This law allowed women to serve in the Brazilian army. |

B. COLOMBIA

The 1991 Constitution establishes (i) the principle of non-discrimination for reasons of sex, race, nationality or family origin, religion, language, political or religious opinion (article 33); (ii) the right to equality and the prohibition of discrimination in all areas; (iii) the condemnation of any form of violence in the family that is considered destructive of her harmony and their (article 42); (iv) the adequate and effective participation of women in the decision-making process, as well as the promotion of equal opportunities for men and women and establishes that women may not be subjected to no kind of discrimination and that she will receive special assistance during pregnancy and after childbirth.

The Presidential Council for the Equity of Women, dependent on the Vice Presidency of the Republic, has assigned the following functions among others: (i) designing policies, plans, programs, projects and necessary provisions designed to promote gender equality; (ii) establishing mechanisms for monitoring and follow-up mechanisms for complying with internal legislation and treaties, international agreements and accords that relate to gender equality for women; and (iii) establishing strategic alliances between the sector of public administration as well as civil society organizations, women's organizations, international organizations, organizations from civil society (ONGs, universities and research centers), for the implementation of a public gender policy.

In the last three decades, national governments have developed different policies for women: in 1992, the Comprehensive Policy for Women was formulated; later, in 1994, the Participation and Equity and Women's Policy; in 1999, the Plan for Equal Opportunities for Women and Men; and finally, in 2003, the National Gender Equality Strategy (NGES).

the Gender Equality Policy for Women. The formulation of these policies, since then,
has been led by the entities responsible for the issue of gender equity.

- Law 48 of 1993 determines the voluntary military service of women and exceptionally compulsory when the circumstances determine it.
- Law 60 of 1993 where the social security system is created, which regulates the principle of universality of health and social security without any discrimination. It draws attention to the pregnant woman, prenatal control, delivery care, postnatal care, and the child up to two years old
- Law 43 of 1993 which regulates the acquisition, resignation, loss and recovery of Colombian nationality.
- Law 82 of 1993, where the female head of the family is supported to provide better conditions of access to credit, education, employment, housing, microenterprise, among others

- Law 201 of 1996 by means of which the delegated attorney's office for the minor and the family is created.
- Law 248 of 1995 by means of which the Inter-American Convention to prevent, punish and eradicate violence against women is ratified.
- Law 258 of 1996 by means of which the affection to family housing is established and other provisions are issued
- Law 307 of 1997 by means of which domestic violence is sanctioned
- Law 360 of 1997 by means of which the crimes against sexual freedom and human dignity are stipulated.
- Law 482 of 1997 through which prevention and comprehensive care for the displaced population is established.
- Law 581 of 2000, which regulates the adequate and effective participation of women in the decision-making levels of the branches of public power
- Law 731 of January 2002, by which regulations are issued to favor rural women.
- Law 623 of 2003 by which rules on equal opportunities for women are issued"

### C. ECUADOR

The 2008 Constitution acquires a series of commitments to achieve gender equality, including: promotion of parity in the appointment of public positions, elimination of violence in the private and public sphere against women; formation and execution of policies for the protection of women's rights; guarantee of equal access to education, property and in decision-making in the administration of a conjugal society; guarantee of equality in access to employment, recognition as productive work of labor unpaid self-support, and the right to receive compensation for domestic work; and guarantees of sexual and reproductive health and protection under the law of pregnant women.

As of the approval of the 2008 Constitution, the different public policies generated by the State are oriented towards the effective enjoyment of rights to achieve equality between men and women. The National Council for Gender Equality is a permanent institution; and technical assistance is provided for its mandatory application in the public sector.

The National Council for Gender Equality was created in 1970 as the Department of Women. Later, in 1984, it became the National Office for Women. In 1985, it took the category of National Directorate for Women, of a technical operational nature, to promote and coordinate actions aimed at improving women's living conditions, professional, ethical, cultural life and to improve their living conditions and participation within the Ministry of Social Welfare.

It becomes the National Council of Women CONAMU with administrative, financial and technical assistance attached to the Presidency of the Republic, whose objective was to ensure the incorporation of the gender approach in plans, programs and projects, and provide advice and assistance technique for its mandatory application in all public sector institutions. It is also responsible for the implementation of the National Agenda of Equality Community Plans. Its objective is to ensure the full validity and exercise of the rights and duties of women and the LGBTI population.

The National Agenda for Women and LGBTI people 2018-2027 is a mandatory instrument of public policy, and is aligned with the National Development Plan 2017-2021; it becomes the macro strategy to mainstream the principle of equality and non-discrimination due to Gender, both for public management and for the

# Legal or normative instruments with an impact on the rights of women

## Organic Code of the Judicial Function
Determines the competence and jurisdiction of judges of violence against women and the family

## Code of Ethics for Women and Men in the lists for multi-person elections; It establishes as impediments to being candidates the having exercised violence and not complying with the financial support payments.

## Organic Law of the Legislative Function
Transfers the responsibility on women's rights and gender equality, through the creation of the Legislative Technical Unit, whose objective is to accompany the process of drafting laws in accordance with the proposed Commissions and the Plenary with, of a non-binding report on topics such as the use of non-discriminatory language in the legislative text and the adoption of international standards

## Organic Law of the Council of Citizen Participation and Social Control
Guarantees citizens, individually or collectively, equal rights to conditions and opportunities to participate, influence and decide in the public life of the State and society

## Organic Code of Territorial Organization, Autonomy and Decentralization
The Cantonal Councils for the Protection of Rights will have as attributes the formation, mainstreaming, observation, monitoring and evaluation of national public policies for the protection of rights, associated with the public policies of the National Councils for the Equality.

## Organic Code of Planning and Public Finance
It establishes that coordination spaces will be established in the exercise of planning and public policy, in order to incorporate gender perspectives into the disability and mobility approaches.

## The Comprehensive Organic Criminal Code
Typifies different forms of violence against women or members of the family nucleus: physical violence, psychological violence and sexual violence

## Children and Adolescents Code
Establishes the forms of protection from mistreatment, abuse, sexual exploitation, trafficking and loss of children and adolescents. It stipulates visitation rights, parental custody and guardianship.

## Work Code
It specifies the rights of working women and men, maternity and paternity leave, the prohibition of untimely dismissal for pregnancy or breastfeeding, as well as access to social security and social benefits.

**Page 152**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

# Law on Violence against Women and the Family (Law 103)
It makes an explicit approach to violence against women and the family. It includes protection measures for victims and the persecution of the perpetrators of this type of violence. Article 1 provides that it is a crime to violate the integrity and sexual freedom of the woman and members of her family.

# Free Maternity and Child Care Law
Guarantees the right of women to free quality and warm health care, during pregnancy, childbirth and postpartum, access to sexual and reproductive health programs.

# Organic Law of Public Service of 2010
Establishes the principle of co-government that includes people with diverse sexual orientation and gender identity, and those who have different cultural conceptions or concepts of kin in the educational system. It also establishes the nomination and appointment positions in the selection processes and incorporation into the public service.

# Organic Law of Bilingual Intercultural Education
Establishes the principle of co-government that includes people with diverse sexual orientation and gender identity, and those who have different cultural conceptions or concepts of kin in the educational system. It also establishes the nomination and appointment positions in the selection processes and incorporation into the public service.

# Organic Law of Higher Education
Establishes the principle of co-government that includes people with diverse sexual orientation and gender identity, and those who have different cultural conceptions or concepts of kin in the educational system. It also establishes the nomination and appointment positions in the selection processes and incorporation into the public service.

# Organic Law of Communication
Prohibits discriminatory content for reasons of ethnicity, place of birth, gender identity, cultural identity, health status, sexual orientation, age, religion, political opinion or social status.

# Organic Law of Popular and Solidarity Economy
Establishes as popular economic units those that are dedicated to the care economy, among other activities. It defines the people responsible for the care economy, such as the family, the community, the state, the environment, reproduction and sustainability of people's lives, in relation to the preparation of food, human care and others.

## D. GUYANA
The 1986 Constitution with 1996 reforms states that (i) women and men have the right to equal pay for equal work, (ii) women and men have equal rights and the same legal status in all spheres of political, economic and social life and that all forms of discrimination against women on the basis of their sex is illegal and (iii) the exercise of women's rights must be guaranteed by law. It also states that women should enjoy vocational and professional training, equal opportunities in employment, remuneration and promotion, and in social, political and cultural activity, by special labour and health protection measures for women (providing maternity leave during pregnancy and

by legal protection and material and moral support for mothers and children, including paid leave and other benefits for mothers and expectant mothers.

The Women's Affairs Bureau has been housed within the Ministry of Labour, Human Services and Social Security since 1981, and has played a role in policy changes, and legislative and administrative measures to guarantee women equal opportunities in education, employment, and social services. The bureau provides information on counselling services and legal aid, with the addition of training of police officers after the Domestic Violence Act was passed in 1996. In 2000, the CEDAW Committee commented that the Women's Affairs Bureau had not demonstrated effectiveness of the national machinery engaged with gender issues. Of particular concern was the insufficient allocation of human and financial resources, which continues to limit the bureau’s capacity.*

E. PERU

The 1993 Political Constitution of Peru establishes that no one should be discriminated on the basis of origin, race, sex, language, religion, opinion, economic condition or any other nature.

The mission of the Ministry of Women and Vulnerable Populations is to become an integrated, modern and articulating sector, leading the sector's policies in a decentralized manner and contributing to overcoming poverty, inequality and exclusion, aimed at achieving gender equality and non-discrimination for women and men. The objective is to contribute to comprehensive human development and equity, generating equal opportunities for men and women.

The General Directorate for Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination oversees compliance with national and sectoral policies for Gender Equality and Non-Discrimination in the public administration process and within the scope of its competence (Supreme Decree No. 003 - 2012-MIMP).

The Gender and Climate Change Action Plan is a management instrument that seeks to guide the action of different entities of the Peruvian State so that within the framework of their competencies related to mitigation and adaptation to climate change, it contributes to equality between women and men in the areas of: Forests, Water Resources, Energy, Food Security, Solid Waste, Health, Education and Risk Management.

National regulatory framework that impacts on women

Law No. 28983 | Defines a framework for equality between Women and Men. This norm establishes that the State has the duty to adopt all the necessary measures to promote and guarantee equality between women and men in all areas of public life.
---|---
Supreme Decree No. 057-2007-PCM | Defines and establishes mandatory national policies. (Equality of men and women). Annually approves indicators for each National Plan.
---|---
Supreme Decree No. 064-2012-MIMP | Constituted the Permanent Multisectoral Commission, an instance that will monitor and evaluate the overall fulfillment of its strategic objectives and expected results, as well as sustainability over time.

Supreme Decree No. 005-2015-MIMP Formulates the use of the National System of Gender Indicators. It considers this system as an instrument for the follow-up monitoring and evaluation of policies on gender equality.

Supreme Decree No. 006-2015-MIMP Approves the National Gender Equality Policy. Six priority objectives are established in it: (i) Reduce violence against women; (ii) Ensure access to reproductive health services; (iii) Guarantee the access and participation of women in decision-making spaces; (iv) Guarantee the exercise of the economic rights of women, including their right to work; (v) Promote gender equality in the public and private spheres between men and women; and reduce the incidence of discriminatory socio-cultural patterns in the population.

F. SURNAME

Suriname's Constitution of 1987 with Amendments through 1992 states that: (i) no one shall be discriminated against on the grounds of birth, sex, race, language, religious origin, education, political belief, economic position or any other status; (ii) supplying special protection to women and children; (iii) providing special protection for disabled persons and for those who are engaged in work which demands special efforts or who work in unhealthy or dangerous conditions; (iv) working women shall be entitled to paid maternity leave of at least 12 weeks, with full pay; (v) the right to freedom of conscience, religion or political opinions; the right to a remuneration for their work corresponding to quantity, type, quality and experience on the basis of equal pay for equal work.

The Bureau for Gender Policy was established in 1997 and became officially operational as part of the Ministry of Home Affairs. It is headed by the Board of Gender Affairs (BGA). The goal of the BGA is to be the central cog of the national machinery for gender policy, with the objective to develop, coordinate, monitor, evaluate and disseminate policies and programmes that will create a balanced development with equal opportunities for men and women.

Since it was set up, the Bureau has developed two Integrated Gender Plans of Action (IGAP 2000-2005 and IGAP 2006-2010) and installed Gender Focus Points (GFPs) in various ministries as part of the government’s Gender Policy. The Suriname Multi-Agency Sectoral Development Plan 2003 was led by a rights-based approach to development, and identified gender as a cross-cutting issue across all programs, projects and activities. *The development of gender policies for rural areas

* Ministry of Home Affairs. 2018. National Report Situation Analysis of Women and Men in Suriname

The Ministry of Home Affairs, and in particular the BGA, is responsible for the implementation and monitoring of CEDAW. Surname has worked or adjusted the following pieces of legislation since the ratification of the CEDAW:

* Revision of the Criminal Code with regard to trade in women and minors in 2006.
* Revision of the Criminal Code regarding sexual harassment in 2009
* Bill against Stalking in 2012: according to this law preventive measures can be taken by the public prosecutor to protect a possible victim.
* Adoption of the law on Domestic Violence (2014).
* Adoption of the law on Minimum Hourly Wage (2014).
* Adoption of the law on General Pension (2014).
* ILO Convention concerning Equal Remuneration for Men and Women Workers of Equal Value (Equal Remuneration Convention), 1951 no. 100, Geneva, June 01 1951 (2016): equal pay for men and women.
* Law implementing the accession of the Republic of Suriname to the ILO Convention concerning Equal Remuneration for Men and Women Workers of Equal Value (Equal Remuneration Convention), 1951 no. 100, Geneva, June 01 1951 (2016): equal wages for men and women.
* Law approving accession of the Republic of Suriname to the Convention concerning Discrimination in Respect of Employment and Occupation (Discrimination Employment and Occupation) Convention, 1958 no. 111, Geneva, June 25 1958 (2016): policy for equal opportunities to prevent discrimination concerning labour.
* Law on Labour Migration (2016).*

At present, a Law on Sexual Harassment and a bill on paid maternity leave are on draft.

Education. The literacy rate average 93% in the countries of the program and is similar between men and women in countries such as Brazil, Colombia and Ecuador, with gender gaps pronounced in Peru, Suriname and Bolivia where the gap reaches 8 points of difference (M: 86.8 % to 96.5%).

Regarding educational attainment, most of the countries present substantial gender gaps for men present at the three educational levels. In primary education, the largest gap can be observed in Colombia, Ecuador, Suriname and, especially, in Guyana, where a low level of schooling is also perceived in both sexes (M: 76% H: 67%). In secondary education, the number of women enrolled is higher than that of men, except in Peru where there is a three-percentage difference that favors men (M: 81.7 % to 84.9%).

* Item


---
**Page 157**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

tertiary education, all the Amazonian countries present a higher number of enrollments among women with large differences in the number of people enrolled between them, for example, Suriname has 18% of women enrolled in tertiary education and 8% of men, while in Peru 72.7% of women access this type of education compared to 68.7% of men.

Employment: Although women have a higher rate of education than men, the latter face major barriers in accessing employment opportunities. Women represent only 35-40% of participation of women in the labor force, although these gaps are wider in some countries such as Guyana, where only 45% of women participate in the labor force compared to 83% of men; and Suriname, where female participation is estimated at 27.9%, while male participation reached to 87.3% of men. COCIV-19 update: ECLAC estimates that the female participation rate in 2020 was 46%, a decrease of 6 percentage points compared to 2019. This means that about 12 million women left the national workforce due to job deserts.

The percentage of unemployment among women is higher in all countries and varies between 14% in Brazil and the largest gender gap in unemployment occurs in Suriname where 11.45% of women are unemployed compared to 4.38% of men. COCIV-19 update: ECLAC estimates that female unemployment in Latin America and the Caribbean region fell, while the estimated female unemployment in the LAC region rate decreases to 12.0% in 2020.

The part-time employment has one of the widest gender gaps. All the Amazonian countries present large gaps that show women occupying these jobs. For example, in Ecuador: 35.9% of women work part time compared to 23.89% of men. Similar figures can be observed in Colombia (M: 26.1% H: 8.5).

Women dedicate between 3 and 5.5 hours per day to unpaid work while men dedicate between 75 minutes and 3 hours depending on the country, Peru (M: 5 hours H: 2 hours) and Ecuador (M: 4.7 hours H: 1 hour) are the countries with the widest gap.

Political representation: Women are poorly represented in the parliaments of their countries. They represent between 13% and 36% of parliamentarians.

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

The other communities that live in the region are distributed as follows: 82% peasants, 14% Afro-descendants, 2% colonos, 1% riverbanks and 1% fishermen (Panamanian Ecclesial Network - REPAM, 2019). These proportions vary from country to country. For example, in Bolivia, the indigenous population is 58%, while the rest of the region is recognized as indigenous, 59.33%; as mestizo, 3.48% as white; 1.38% as Afro-Ecuadorian and the rest in other categories such as black, mulato and montobo (INEC, 2010). For its part, in the Colombian Amazon, 59.76% of the population identifies with an ethnic group or belong to ethnic group and belong to peasant communities; meanwhile, the indigenous population slightly exceeds 23% and people who identify as black or mulato are over 2% (DANE, 2016).

The division of the Amazonian population by sex has not been accurately identified. However, it is estimated that in Bolivia, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Venezuela can be estimated that for five countries in the Amazon region - Bolivia, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and Venezuela - the average percentage of Amazonian women is between 48.3% and 49.7%. In Bolivia, 50.2% of the population identifies as indigenous; in Colombia, 61.5%; in Ecuador, 50.5%; in Peru, 50.7%, and in Venezuela, 50.2%. However, there are ethnic groups that are identified in each country. In Colombia, 307,280 Amazonian women do not self-identify with an ethnic group, $29,900$ self-identity as indigenous and 9,427 as Black, Mulato and Montobo (INEC, 2010). In Bolivia, 56.3% of the population in the Bolivian Amazon identify as mestizos (210,462) and indigenous (121,463) (INEC, 2010). Finally, in Peru, 735,597 Amazonian women identify as indigenous, $86,098$ of native or indigenous origin and Amazonian men are 109 at Quechua (INEC, 2017) [1]

Most of the population is Brazilian, accounting for more than 70% of the total population. Such population is followed by that of Peru and Colombia with an estimated 13% and 4% of the total Amazonian population, respectively (Cities and Local Governments United - CLUGU, 2018). Brazil has a total area of $5,697,000$ km² and its Amazon region is $5,200$, with about 2 million inhabitants each. In addition, there are other intermediate cities, such as Iquitos in Peru, with a population of more than 400,000 inhabitants (INEI, 2017). Among the Brazilian states that have Amazonian territory are Acre, Amazonas, Roraima, Pará (Colombia), Tabatinga (Brazil) and Puerto Maldonado (Peru), where the population ranges from $50,000$ to $60,000$ people (INEI, 2017; National University of Colombia, s.f).

Gender relations in the Amazonia

According to a paper published by CIFOR (CIFOR, 2015) there are several features of gender relations in the Amazon, however, each of these varies greatly depending on the social and environmental context from specific Amazonian social groups:

- Strong community identity and political commitment among ethnic, occupational and religious groups discourages attention to gender inequality that belies community unity.
- Patriarchal cultural values among some indigenous groups and among the general population in non-indigenous Brazil assume that men represent the family and the


---
**Page 162**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

community in public arenas, leaving women without independent autonomous property rights and representation in decision making related to forest management.

* The division of labor by gender is often linked to different physical spaces and spheres of activity; for example, women may engage in reproductive and productive activities in the homestead/community gardens, swidden gardens, (including livestock) and other domestic tasks. In contrast, men dominate in hunting, agricultural clearing and logging activities for the commercial market. In non-indigenous communities, women’s arenas are often more limited – and include childcare.

* Women's productive work is often invisible to markets and outsiders due to its association with the home, family, and subsistence and because of limited market access.

Physical isolation of many forest communities makes women's collective action more difficult, and limits access to social services and other benefits of citizenship rights.

Programs to support community forest management have influenced community and gender relations in a number of ways. These programs are often implemented by focusing resources on a small group of male timber managers and neglecting NTFPs and broader forest management tasks.

Over the past decade, women in LAC countries have found diverse ways to organize in support of their community struggles for land and other resources, to demand services from government and NGO projects, and to gradually build their skills and leadership within broader community and public arenas.


4. WOMEN IN FINANCE IN LAC

Women-led businesses

There is mounting global evidence of a correlation between gender diversity and superior business performance. A McKinsey study found that companies with greater female representation had an average growth opportunity of US$ 2.6 trillion for Latin America in 2025 in a world in which women participate in the market economy to an identical extent as men. However, gender diversity is not yet a strategic priority among LAC companies. A recent study by McKinsey concluded that publicly listed companies with higher female representation yielded 44% higher returns on investment and 47% higher profit margins. Despite this evidence, 83% of LAC companies declared that gender diversity is not a strategic priority.

In the Amazonian countries, the presence of women on boards of directors is very low, with a participation between 8.4% in Brazil and 15.1% in Colombia. The figures improve


---
**Page 163**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

a little in the companies where women are majority owners, although they do not exceed 20% in any of the 7 countries in this study. Additionally, the number of companies with women in senior management varies between 11.9% in Suriname[10].

Women represent more than 40% of the economically active population in LAC. Only 17% of them are entrepreneurs in the formal economy. The main barriers they encounter to forming and running their own business include (i) lack of access to finance, which affects 80% of LAC women work in this sector[1]) or going beyond microenterprises, reducing their earning potential; (ii) gender barriers that prevent them from working longer hours outside the home; (iii) lack of education and training opportunities, including access to resources, adequate training and business advice[2].

According to a study of 35 entrepreneurs in 15 countries in LAC, a high number of women entrepreneurs in the region feel that they have been discriminated against because of their gender (27% versus 4% for men). 50.8% of the women surveyed affirmed that there is a general lack of access to business opportunities including access to resources, adequate training and business advice[3].

The main reason women-led businesses fail is lack of access to capital. LAC is the region with the second highest rate of bankruptcy of companies run by women worldwide. While many women entrepreneurs report that they have sufficient capital to start their business, they often do not have enough money to sustain it over time and achieve low profitability. Women entrepreneurs point to the difficulty of obtaining financing as the main impediment to continue with their businesses. The credit gap in LAC is $8bn for women-led microenterprises[4].

The Global Banking Alliance ensures that women clients show a stronger saving behavior (16% more likely than men to save for futures), with lower risk (on average, they are more likely to repay loans), with higher growth and lower attrition. Evidence shows that financial intermediaries can design programs that effectively meet the needs of women see excellent financial returns.

Investing in women-led business

Currently, the supply of gender-lens financial products in LAC is limited since, on the one hand, only a few fund managers consider gender in their investment analysis and, on the other hand, no efforts are made to search for the growing portfolio of women-led[5].

[1] World Economic Forum. Global Gender Gap Report 2020.
[2] UN Women. Women Entrepreneurs: The Missing Link in Latin American and Caribbean informal economies
[3] Burkhardt, Lestienne et al. Gender Lens Investing: How Finance Can Accelerate Gender Equality in Latin America and the Caribbean. IADB Invest. 2019.
[4] World Bank. Global Banking Alliance for Women. Credit Gap. Washington D.C., February 2018
[5] p.l. MIMF Finance Gap, 2017


---
**Page 164**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex B Gender Assessment

businesses. Furthermore, financial intermediaries are dominated by men, especially at decision-making levels.

Despite this, a gender-lens investing for women-led business is emerging in various types of assets in LAC. In public markets, bonds are being created for socially responsible investors interested in promoting gender equality. In private markets, the microfinance sector has been a leader in providing financial services to women entrepreneurs in Latin America. Commercial banks have worked with institutional investors, including multilateral development banks (MDBs), to address the small and medium-sized enterprise (SME) credit gap. MDBs are also working with governments and other stakeholders to provide grant and technical assistance to SMEs run by women. In addition, other investments in private markets such as venture capital (VC) and private equity (PE) funds are growing very fast in the region—especially in Brazil, Mexico, and Colombia.

IDB’s program Women Entrepreneurship Banking (web) facilitates access to financial and non-financial services (training, mentoring, networking) for women-led businesses in LAC through advisory services and a revolving fund. The program has been implemented by IDB in 12 countries, approving nearly $800 million in investments and $5 million in technical assistance, to develop and market products and services for women-owned SMEs (WOSMEs). In addition, the IDB’s Women Program are making impressive advancements serving their female customers.

Pro Mujer is a leading microfinance organization in Latin America dedicated to improving the livelihoods of women in Latin America through a combination of financial inclusion and social empowerment. Its integrated approach includes financial services (loans, savings, insurance), training in basic business skills and entrepreneurship, health education (e.g., on gender-based violence, nutrition, family planning, prenatal development, etc.) and health screenings (cancer screening, diabetes testing). Pro Mujer has a strong track record of impact. In 2018 it had USD $393 million in loans and served 276,000 women. Pro Mujer’s services are offered primarily through a communal bank and its clients have an average repayment rate of 95%.

In 2018, IDB issued, structured and subscribed a Gender-focused Social Bond Issuance Banco Davivienda SA (Davivienda). The deal, worth USD $1 million has a 7-year maturity. This is the world’s first issue of a gender-linked bond aimed at achieving outcomes. The bond was partially backed by the issuer to finance the growth of its women-owned SMEs portfolio (WSMEs), as well as the purchase of social interest houses by women in Colombia.
