### ACCESS TO LAND AND WOMEN TERRITORIAL CARE

Although in the region there are some large and medium urban centers - such as Manaus, Belém or Iquitos - the territory is eminently rural. In rural areas, the production farms of land managed by women are usually small plots of land, which are distributed among community members, and private properties including different productive activities. However, the land tenure of these different figures is distributed unequally among men and women, as it is usually Amazonian tradition that the man is the owner of the land. For example, in the Ecuadorian Amazon only 11.85% of women have their own land, while 88.2% of male producers own it (PROAmazonia, 2019). Likewise, in Colombia, women's access to land


---
**Page 166**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

tenure is precarious and their ownership, or even inheritance rights and shared tenure with partners or spouses, is rarely recognized (UNDP, 2011; Zorno, 2015).

There are different social, economic, legal and cultural factors that can explain this inequality in land tenure. For instance, Amazonian women have less access to state identification or visibility processes that allow them to participate in formalization processes. In the Peruvian Amazon, only 38% of women aged between 20-49 years old speak Quechua and 7.2% of those who speak Spanish do not even have a National Identification Document (Villar, 2013). In addition, although there is a shared tenure within collective or community land ownership arrangements, women usually assume mostly the custody and administration of the lands (PROAmazonia, 2019).

However, although Amazonian women do not exercise ownership over land widely, they do play an essential role in territorial management and local forms of environmental practices that are crucial for the survival and well-being of indigenous peoples. This is mainly because women oversee home and family care, which also often encompasses the care of the natural environment. In fact, a PROAmazonia study (2019) shows that in the Ecuadorian Amazon, women spend 3 hours more per day on domestic chores than men, to reproductive work, almost 17 hours longer than men. Although this gap has variations according to different local communities, it has been found that usually all Amazonian women spend at least one hour a day on environmental care.

In addition, women are also active in the productive activities of agriculture, livestock, forestry, and fisheries in which different forms of environmental care and sustainable use of natural resources are exercised. In the Ecuadorian Amazon, 38.6% of women engage in these activities (PROAmazonia, 2019). In the case of indigenous population, it has been identified that indigenous women mainly assume the care of the chacras as scenarios of family agricultural production and also as a means to ensure food security for their families. In many cases, the generation of economic surpluses for the home (PROAmazonia, 2019). In the case of the Peruvian Amazon, it has been found that women are not only active in participation in productive activities but also in decision-making and resource management.

Women are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Some research argued that this is due to their greater dependence on natural resources for their livelihoods effects, for example on productive systems of agriculture, livestock and tourism (Montalvo, 2019). Women family and environmental care activities create a strong dependence on natural resources for their survival, such as water, food, wood, etc. that are essential to their livelihoods as well as on their personal and social wellbeing. More importantly, “in rural areas women have less access to land, water, seeds, tools, credit, information, better soil wealth, on greater clean water, for greater and better forest management, for greater and better seed management, greater chances of subsistence of families in decent conditions, greater opportunities for education and health services, and lower related risks of contagion of tropical diseases, such as malaria, ZIka, and dengue (Montalvo, 2019). Some studies in the Peruvian Amazon find that women are differentially affected


---
**Page 167**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

by deforestation. This is because, while men own land and can take advantage of tree felling for timber sales or the expansion of productive activities, deforestation degrades the fundamental natural resources most women use to ensure the livelihoods of their families (Villar, 2013).

That is why, different organizations stress the importance of including a gender perspective in policies related to land tenure and forest management for conservation and sustainable development (OTCA, 2019; PROAmazonía & ONUMujeres, 2019. Our Future Forests-Amazone Verda, 2021). In particular, there is a growing call to foster a greater recognition of the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities over their ancestral lands and indigenous and community territories. Together, these forms of ordering occupy more than 47% of the Amazon territory (RAISG, 2018; RAISG, 2020). They are located in protected areas and they are estimated to cover a territorial extension of approximately 2,123,000 km² (RAISG, 2018; RAISG, 2020). The country with the highest coverage of protected areas is Brazil, which has 54% of its territory in protected areas. Colombia and Peru are countries with approximately 14% and 10%, respectively (RAISG, 2017). According to the Amazon Network of Georeferenced Socio-Environmental Information, there are two areas (of direct use) where women have land ownership rights and two areas in which women within governance schemes:

(i) Areas of direct use in which natural resources are conserved within the framework of conservation that regulate and control the different uses. These areas can occupy just under 12% of the total territorial extension and more than 50% of the Protected Natural Areas of the Amazon (RAISG, 2020).

(ii) Areas of indirect use where biodiversity protection is promoted in parallel with resource extraction activities, but where direct exploitation or consumption are not allowed. It is estimated that these areas can occupy about 12% of the Land coverage of the Amazon and make up just over 48% of the Protected Natural Areas of the region.

This issue is particularly relevant because there are 3,275 indigenous territories covering more than 2,300,000 km² of the Amazon (RAISG, 2020). The land tenure categories of these territories may vary between: Officially recognized traditional occupation and used territories; Indigenous Land or Territory (PIAT); Indigenous Land or Territory Recognition; Indigenous Reserve or Intangible Zone (for PIAT); Indigenous Reserve Proposal (RAISG, 2020) The countries with the largest number of indigenous territories are Brazil with an area of approximately 1.8 million km² and Peru with an area of almost 657,000 km². Colombia has an area of 262,912 km² Most of these indigenous territories are officially recognized and occupy more than half the land in their respective countries. Studies have highlighted that the power of women in decision-making is low or medium compared to that of men who are mainly responsible for the administration of resources (PROAmazonía, 2019).


---
**Page 168**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex 8 Gender Assessment

However, there are already multiple organizations that have strong representation and visibility of women. In Brazil, the Coordinator of Indigenous Organizations of the Brazilian Amazon (COIAB) and the Articulation of Indigenous Peoples of Brazil (APIB) have a high representation of women in their leadership positions (Lima and Viera, 2019). Amazonian women (both indigenous, peasant and Afro-descendant) have played a central role in the management of different protected areas and indigenous territories, as well as in the organization of rural communities. They are also involved in sustainable production processes and in the defense of their rights to land and resources, and, in recent years, have been actively involved in sustainable production processes (Lima and Viera, 2019). Many of these processes respond to the emergence of bioeconomy initiatives that seek to use natural resources in a sustainable way. These areas have great potential for contributing to the conservation of biodiversity, reducing environmental degradation and ensure the conservation of the biological resources that underpin the various emerging bioeconomy initiatives. In fact, 72% of Protected Natural Areas and Indigenous Territories are located within the Amazon biome (RAISG, 2020). The causes of deforestation, fire, illegal mining, pollution, among other factors (RAISG, 2020)). There are even multiple studies that point to the relationship between the delineation of these territories and the preservation of biodiversity. These areas also have a high concentration of ecosystem services crucial for bioeconomy such as: carbon capture, biodiversity protection and conservation of hydrological systems (RAISG, 2020).

6. BIOECONOMY: CHALLENGES TO WOMEN PARTICIPATION IN VALUE CHAINS

The Amazon faces multiple challenges associated with deforestation and environmental pollution that occur mainly by unsustainable economic activities (RAISG, 2020). In recent years, therefore, there is a growing need and call to generate productive systems that take advantage of biodiversity in a sustainable way, contribute to its conservation and include the participation of women. However, the Amazon biome has a high concentration of small and intermediate cities as well as other members of those social groups. In this context, "bioeconomy" initiatives are understood as those that seek to generate economic activity by using natural resources by retributing and incorporating the knowledge of local communities and understanding the importance and value of keeping the tropical forest standing, no longer deforested or degraded (RAISG, 2020). However, there is a lack of enough data available to quantify gender gaps within current bioeconomy initiatives in the Amazon, but here we present some of the gender gaps that are referenced in the existing literature.

Taking a step forward to include women into bioeconomy efforts is premised upon the central role they play as producers and users of natural resources. Women have been involved in value chains that harness and preserve natural resources such as trees, seeds, medicinal plants, insects, fruits, among others. However, there are still some gaps for women to be able to have sufficient participation in these processes and to include


---
**Page 169**
---

# The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund: Annex 8 Gender Assessment

## Knowledge of the local communities they belong to. Some of these gaps are related to ownership of productive assets (land, animals, capital); distribution of the use of time in productive and household tasks; access to formal economies; available distribution of investments in education, health services, credit, infrastructure, among others. In this sense, there is a call from different international organizations to recognize that in agrofood systems - and in general in sustainable value chains- women have more difficulties in accessing resources and decision-making power than men (World Bank, 2001; World Bank, IFAD and FAO, 2009).

Another of the gender gaps that exist in the current trends of bioeconomy in the Amazon is that women still have little leadership in economic projects. That is why there is a growing interest to foster such women roles for instance in certain initiatives based on the sustainable use of non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and their processing.

One of these experiences is that of the Xikrin women of the indigenous Territory of Trincheira Bajáç in the Brazilian Amazon. Since about 2014, these women have taken advantage of their traditional knowledge to develop a bio-business model based on the sustainable use of NTFP resources management projects, particularly non-timber forest products. One of the products they have been processing is walnut (Lima and Vieira, 2019). They have developed family processes of collecting, washing and processing the walnut (Lima and Vieira, 2019). In addition, they have led negotiations to skip intermediaries in order to generate fairer income for themselves. This has allowed them to increase their income through traditional knowledge and practice as a bio-business model: the processing of babassu oil for cooking and producing cosmetics (Lima and Viera, 2019; FUNAI, 2019). With these - and other products - they have been able to generate an additional source of income while working on the conservation of their cultural practices and their forests.

In general, one of the problems of inequality faced by women in the green economy (or bioeconomy) is reaching working spaces and value chains that have traditionally been excluded or closed for them. In the case of Brazil, the commercialization of part of Brazil nut or chestnut has been an opportunity to promote gender equity in bioeconomy, as it has created more opportunities for women in the use of non-timber forest resources.

This project is based on the experience of the women of Riberata, who work seasonally in Brazil nut harvesting and who have found job and market opportunities to improve their income. The population of Riberata is 105 people. It is worth noting that only in the city of Riberata by the early 2000s, 75% of the population used in chestnut extraction was women (Montoro and Pavedo 2003 in Schmitt and Gómez-García, 2015).

In addition, many Amazonian women face a gap in income generation and access to knowledge and technical tools. A bio-business that has tried to respond to these gaps is


---
**Page 170**
---

The case of more than 240 women in the Bolivian Amazon who process fruits like acai, copoazú, among others (UN Women, 2019). Most of these women are indigenous mothers head of household, although other peasant women are also involved within communities. These women have been able to generate significant economic income for their homes through the sustainable use of Amazonian fruits. In addition, in a constant dialogue with the United Nations, they have gained access to technical knowledge, training and education on forest management, conservation and sustainable use of natural resources, participate in public settings, negotiate with other actors and prevent gender-based violence (UN Women, 2019). The contribution of this bio-business to forest conservation is also significant. It has been used as a tool for reforestation and to prevent tree felling (which generate economic resources while standing), control burning during summer periods and properly manage solid waste (UN Women, 2019).

Another barrier to the participation of Amazonian women in bioeconomy is the weak bonding of peasant and riverside communities living way in informal bio-businesses. One experience that has tried to respond to this barrier is the case of a community of peasants and riverbanks in the northeast of Para in the Brazilian Amazon. After the 1980s this region was affected by deforestation, which led to the abandonment of the land, extreme agribusiness and the Tucuru dam. These factors impacted, among other things, the availability of acai palm in the area (Cunha, 2006). For this reason, a group of local residents decided to create a cooperative for the production and commercialization not only of its fruit, ensure repopulation in the area and generate greater economic income through export of the product. The project was initially led by men but after some years it had collaboration from some women but after a few years of implementation many peasant and riverside women became more actively involved and consolidated the Iguaçu-Mirim Women's Association (AMIRIM). Women have been protagonists of the project as they began to hold senior positions in the bio-business model, generating significant economic income (Cunha, 2006).

On the other hand, some international institutions have identified that one of the main barriers for women’s participation in the green economy is that they tend to spend many hours in everyday practices of family and territorial care which impedes their participation in economic alternatives (OECD, s.t.). In the Ecuadorian Amazon this time-gap is 18 hours, while peasant women in the Brazilian Amazon spend an average of 20 hours per week on subsistence work, while Amazonian men spend only 60 (PROamazonia, 2019). In addition, even if women engage actively in certain activities that may involve monetary exchange, they are not always able to benefit from them. This is because traditionally why (i) re-encourage Amazonian women to family care activities most and (ii) prevent the development of their own economic projects, which turn into bio-businesses, such as education and political participation (PROamazonia, 2019).

Finally, one of the factors affecting women’s participation in bioeconomy is the uneven distribution of means of production and income. As mentioned above, very few Amazonian women have access to land ownership, which affects their decision-making process.


---
**Page 171**
---

power over objects and effects of production (central elements in sustainable production of bio businesses). (Proamazonia, 2019). In addition, this generates an unequal distribution of income, as Amazonian men usually have the power over surpluses produced by their labor, while women are excluded from these benefits because of lack of income (Proamazonia, 2019). For example, in the case of the Amazon department in Peru, there is a wage gap almost 35% between men and women (Villar, 2018). This situation also affects access to formal identification documents (formal identification (DNI) of Amazonian women), access to land and capital, implying that women have less access to the basic elements to consolidate a bio business.

Access to credit in the Amazon is also limited, there is a problem of access to financial institutions for women. In addition, women are not able to obtain loans from banks. For example, in Ecuador, the total amount of credit delivered to the Amazon region represents only 5% of the country's total[1]. The information shows that credit access opportunities for the Amazonian women are limited and they have less access than men to financial services which generally ensure the holding of material resources such as land and a fixed income. The Amazonian women are disadvantaged against men, as they do not own the land and do not have access to formal labor markets; hence, increasing the already existing poverty in women in the region.

In sum, the participation of Amazonian women in bioeconomic alternatives still presents multiple gaps related to pre-existing limiting conditions such as socio-economic and economic factors. In addition, there is a hand gap between men and women in working conditions, as Amazonian women tend to have more precarious conditions than men. Specifically, Amazonian women have less access and stability in the labour market; for example, in the Ecuadorian context, only 15% of Amazonian women are employed (Proamazonia, 2019: 25). In addition, in the work they carry out they have lower social benefits, such as social security: in Ecuador only 21.15% Amazonian women have affiliation to compulsory social security (PROAMAZONIA, 2019: 36). Furthermore, according to the Center for Business Development (CDE), the other barrier women face to better participation in the formal economy is that they are less educated than men and therefore tend to have a lower degree of education than men, making it difficult to transition to the labor market (CDE, s.f.). An example of this is that in the Peruvian Amazon the average literacy rate for women is 50.7% compared with 68.3% for men[2]. These barriers to the economic empowerment of women in the Amazon due to the sexual division of labor and cultural imaginaries exclude them from entering the labor market which deepens their poverty, as they are not able to access resources or credit. This is because the responsibilities of taking care of the family and kids limit women's ability to enter the labor market (CDE, s.f.). In addition, since women are mainly engaged in agriculture, they are responsible for caring for the chicks, but they do not have control over the use of resources and income and do not access funding or credit.


[1] Diagnosis of the situation of Amazonian women, 2019. (INAE, MAG, FINID, GFC, CEF, ONP Mujeres), Ecuador


---
**Page 172**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex B Gender Assessment

These estimates do not include those provinces, states or departments that have small portions over the Amazon region; for example, the Meta department in Colombia, the Department of La Paz in Bolivia, among others.

### 7. STAKEHOLDER SOCIALIZATION PHASE I: GENDER OUTCOMES

#### Summary of the main findings
- **38%** of all expressed concerns in the Amazon are linked to the protection of the Amazon basin.
- Protection-related comments are more common amongst men than women (gender gap).
- Women talk more about citizen initiatives aiming to solve the issues in the region, especially in Brazil. These citizen initiatives could be "Rainforest Guardians," forming themselves into groups and associations.
- Brazilians in the Amazon are experiencing increasing trends of food insecurity, which led to such a solidarity response in the creation of food banks. 100% completed surveys reported that they have experienced food insecurity at some point during their life, caused by their employment informality and lack of tourism.
- Bio-based products are also an important concern for women, most of them having a strong vision of sustainability, applying indigenous cosmologies to their production techniques, in cocoa and coffee agriculture, but also in technologies, due to the collaboration with different organizations.
- Guyanese citizens are showing enthusiasm about different PPPs (public private partnership) aiming to position Guyana as a bio-tourism destination.

The Napo River is an important source of income for people living near Napo flow, as it is the source for their economic activities, including sustainable fishing. If they find snails in the morning in the riverbank, that means that the following day there will be abundant fishing.


Some specific women findings

In Peru, citizen discussion is closely related to women. There are numerous organizations of women that are aimed to defend their territories. For instance, a group of women


---
**Page 173**
---

The Amazon Bioeconomy Fund:
Annex B Gender Assessment

associated to protect the Amazon has created a digital inventory of threatened species of trees.

In this sense, many of the organizations are entailing a digital approach gathering women from a variety of countries. For example, Fondo Indígena Mujer Amazónica is a scholarship program designed to indigenous women in the Amazonas leading initiatives in their communities.

Finally, access to the credit market is a crucial demand of women in the region. In Pululampa, Colombia, women entrepreneurship is being considered in the reconstruction of the capital.

Participation in the dialogue tables

The dialogues included 7 tables, all of them with a good representation of women. There was one specific table to address gender issues.

In the gender table there represented were 95% women from the National (Perú) Women's Association Network (ANFEP), and the Indigenous Women of the Amazon (UMIAB) from Brazil, Ecuador's Waorani Women's Association (AMWAE) and Oshun Afro-Colombian Women's Association, to name some of them.

The main insights of these table were the following:
- Common view: Deforestation caused by extractive industries is a threat to biodiversity, traditional crops, amongst them, source of ancestral medicine.

- New inputs: Importance to contribute to reduce machismo in the organizations and help women to reach economic independence as a powerful factor of respect.
- Potential opportunities: Create business opportunities of ancestral medicine as opportunity for development.

* various peasant organizations (Portuguese speakers) (40% of women).
* Indigenous people (Portuguese speakers) (56% of women).
* Afro descendants and local groups (Portuguese speakers) (44% of women),
* International Civil Society Organizations (50% of women)

The Amazon Biodiversity Fund:
Annex 8 Gender and Diversity Action Plan

# Gender and Diversity Action Plan

The Gender and Diversity Action Plan articulates complementary activities with targets on gender and inclusion of indigenous peoples, Afro descendants, and peasant communities of the Amazon. Activities will focus in at least 14 areas, urban and rural, in the seven countries of the Amazon biome. To identify these areas, we will use the results from the Gender Assessment (if available) or conduct a Gender Assessment if no data is available. The plan provides implementation to understand: (a) the different local businesses inserted into bio-economy trends, including those owned by indigenous peoples, Afro descendants and peasant communities; (b) the main challenges these businesses face in their insertion into bio-economy trends; (c) the capacity of the actors involved in the bio-economy to promote environmental sustainability (at least 1 rural area per country); d) a baseline study of the areas prioritized for the trainings and interventions of output 4 and 5.

For outputs 1, 4 and 5 we will work with two different approaches: i) individuals who are willing to participate in the training sessions the project will create (mainly in urban areas); and ii) creation of capacity building programs with local communities and/or schools that are interested in including them in their own development plans.
